# RitterOS

A hobby operating system for AMD64 (x86-64) PCs and 64-bit ARM (AArch64),
written from scratch in C and a little assembly. It is developed on an Apple
Silicon Mac and tested in QEMU: an emulated UEFI PC with an NVMe SSD, and
QEMU's ARM `virt` machine with the same disk, running on the Mac's own cores.

Everything, including the complete toolchain, lives inside this folder;
nothing is installed system-wide.

**Using RitterOS:** the manual, [`docs/manual.md`](docs/manual.md), chapter by
chapter; a page for every command with its options, messages and examples,
[`docs/commands/`](docs/commands/README.md); and the install guide, from
writing a stick to installing on a PC, [`docs/install.md`](docs/install.md).
On RitterOS the same pages are `man NAME` (`man install`, `man manual`).

**Writing programs for RitterOS** on your own computer, alone or with an AI
agent, in a repository of your own, and running them on a RitterOS machine
over SSH: the SDK, [`sdk/`](sdk/) (its guide is [`sdk/CLAUDE.md`](sdk/CLAUDE.md)).

**Written for people and coding agents.** Every program has a manual page,
and the SDK's guide (`sdk/CLAUDE.md`) is written so a coding agent can
follow it: build on a Mac, deploy over SSH, run on RitterOS. RitterOS
itself was developed with Claude Code.

## Releases

A release is named by its year and month, `2026.11` (a second one in the
same month is `2026.11.1`), kept in the file [`VERSION`](VERSION) and as a git
tag of the same name. The kernel says it at the boot, with the commit it
was built from (`RitterOS kernel started: 2026.11 (513e2a4), x86_64`), and
`uname -a` answers it on a running machine (programs: `uname()` from
`<sys/utsname.h>`).

Each release on GitHub's [Releases](https://github.com/eloyr77/RitterOS/releases)
page has USB stick images for both architectures,
`ritteros-2026.11-x86_64.img.gz` and `ritteros-2026.11-aarch64.img.gz`,
built from its tag: [docs/install.md](docs/install.md) explains writing
one. The kernel records no build folder, so anyone building a release's
tag gets the same kernel, byte for byte.

## Quick start

```sh
make toolchain     # once: builds QEMU + deps, fetches Zig into ./tools (~10-20 min)
make               # both architectures + their disk images build/ritteros-<arch>.img
make run           # boot on x86-64 in a QEMU window; this terminal is the serial terminal
make run ARCH=aarch64          # boot the same disk on 64-bit ARM (hardware virtualization)
make run-headless  # boot with the console in the terminal (Ctrl-A X quits)
make test          # all automated test boots, both architectures (~19 min)
make run CMDLINE="crash=pf"   # boot with a kernel command line
make clean         # remove build/ (including the disk image and its RitterFS)
```

After booting, the kernel starts `login`, which reads from the serial port:
in `make run` and `make run-headless` that is the terminal you started it
from. In `make run` you can also type in the QEMU window: it goes to the
emulated USB keyboard (US layout). On the first start of a new disk it asks
you to choose a password for `root`, the administrator; then log in as
`root` and you get the shell `sh` in `/home/root`. Type `help` at the
prompt. `edit notes.txt` opens a file in the text editor (Ctrl-S saves,
Ctrl-Q quits). `useradd NAME` and `passwd NAME` add users. `exit` logs out;
Ctrl-D at `login:` ends `login`, and the kernel's rescue console
(`rescue>`, no password) follows. **Ctrl-A X** quits QEMU (Ctrl-A C switches to the QEMU
monitor and back); Ctrl-C goes to RitterOS: it stops the running program,
or cancels the line at the prompt.

```
ritteros:/$ cd /test
ritteros:/test$ ls docs
     <dir>  notes/
ritteros:/test$ cat docs/notes/deep/nested.txt
Found me, four directories down.
ritteros:/test$ /system/x86_64/test/counter bg 3 500 &
```

## What it does today

1. **UEFI bootloader** (`boot/`, `\EFI\BOOT\BOOTX64.EFI` and `BOOTAA64.EFI`):
   reads the command line, finds the RitterOS partition on the boot disk and
   loads `/system/<arch>/kernel.elf` from its RitterFS into free memory
   (every block checksum-verified; a damaged metadata copy is replaced by the
   second copy, a damaged kernel block stops the boot), collects firmware
   information (memory map, framebuffer, ACPI, SMBIOS, boot device path,
   random seed), builds the kernel's first page tables, exits boot services,
   switches to those tables (x86) and moves the UEFI runtime services to the
   kernel's address space (`SetVirtualAddressMap`; the firmware finds its
   code at both the old and the new addresses during the call), then jumps
   to the kernel. After the display switch, **progress marks** (gray squares in
   the top left corner) show how far it got: 1 display switched, 2 boot
   services exited, 3 runtime services moved, 4 jumping to the kernel, 5 the
   kernel runs; the kernel's console then replaces them. An orange dot below
   them per ExitBootServices attempt, a red square for a stage that failed.
   Serial output first checks that a UART answers (the 16550's scratch
   register), sets it up itself (115200 baud, 8N1) and never waits forever,
   so PCs without a serial port, or with one the firmware left unset, boot
   too.
2. **Kernel** (`kernel/`), in boot order:
   - CPU exception handlers (x86: own GDT/TSS and IDT, double faults on
     their own stack; ARM: vector table, exceptions on their own SP_EL1
     stack), with crash reports on screen and serial;
   - higher-half address space with per-section permissions, NX, an unmapped
     NULL page and a stack guard page; the framebuffer is mapped on its own
     wherever the graphics card puts it (on real PCs often above 4 GiB), on
     x86 write-combining (PAT entry 4) so that drawing stays fast;
   - the console is drawn from the kernel's first line (the bootloader's
     display mode switch blanks the screen, and the bootloader maps the
     framebuffer into the kernel's first page tables): a boot that hangs on
     a PC without a serial port shows how far it got;
   - physical page allocator and kernel heap, both self-tested at every boot;
     then the console draws through a copy of the screen in RAM (video
     memory is fast to write but slow to read) whose text rows form a ring:
     scrolling clears one row instead of moving or redrawing everything;
     once the scheduler runs, changes reach the screen at most every 20 ms
     (the thread `console` copies what is left), so a burst of lines costs a
     few screen copies; crashes copy at once; the boot log reports lines
     scrolled, drawing time and screen updates;
   - firmware information: ACPI tables, SMBIOS, device path, UEFI clock
     (firmware calls run in their own address space where physical memory
     is also mapped at its own address, for firmware that keeps physical
     pointers);
   - interrupts: the interrupt controller (x86: Local APIC, the old 8259 PICs
     masked; ARM: GICv3) and a 100 Hz timer tick (APIC timer measured
     against the PIT; ARM virtual timer); waiting sleeps in `hlt`/`wfi`;
     device interrupts as PCI MSI-X messages (x86: to the Local APIC; ARM:
     to the GIC's MSI frame);
   - PCI Express scan, NVMe driver (sleeps until the controller's MSI-X
     interrupt; polls if there is none), GPT with CRC verification;
   - SATA disks through AHCI (`sata0`, `sata1`, ...; partitions
     `sata0p2`): the controller taken over from the firmware and reset,
     each port's link and device checked (CD drives and port multipliers
     are skipped), IDENTIFY for model, size and sector size, 48-bit (or
     28-bit) DMA reads and writes and cache flushes, an MSI interrupt per
     command (polling without MSI); RitterOS boots from a SATA disk too;
   - the **system partition** is the one the bootloader loaded the kernel
     from, found by its GPT GUID (passed in the boot info) on whatever disk
     holds it: NVMe or a USB stick. RitterOS boots from either;
   - keyboards: USB keyboards (boot protocol) and, on x86, a PS/2 keyboard on
     the i8042 controller (laptops), set up after USB; all are `kbd0`,
     `kbd1`, ... in the order found and share one module for the layout
     (US), Shift, Ctrl, Caps Lock and key repeat;
   - USB: the xHCI controller (`usb0`) is reset and started with its command
     and event rings and an MSI-X interrupt (MSI if it has no MSI-X; without
     either, waits check the event ring continuously); a No-Op command proves the
     round trip; each connected device gets a port reset, a slot and an
     address, and its descriptors are read with control transfers (vendor
     and product, strings, configuration, interfaces, endpoints); **hubs**
     (USB 2 and USB 3, up to 5 in a chain): their ports are powered and
     reset and the devices behind them enumerated in turn (route string,
     root port and transaction translator in the device's slot context);
     **USB mass storage** (sticks; bulk-only transport with SCSI commands:
     INQUIRY, TEST UNIT READY, READ CAPACITY, READ/WRITE 10 and 16,
     SYNCHRONIZE CACHE, REQUEST SENSE; stalled endpoints recovered) as block
     devices `usbdisk0`, `usbdisk1`, ... with partitions `usbdisk0p2` (GPT and RitterFS work
     on them as on NVMe; a RitterFS on a stick is checked at the boot);
     USB keyboards (`kbd0`, HID boot protocol) type into the terminal, with
     Shift, Ctrl, Caps Lock, cursor keys and key repeat;
   - USB hot plug (on the controller's ports and behind hubs, whose status
     change endpoints report their ports): the thread `usb` sets up
     devices plugged in after the boot and releases unplugged ones (their
     drivers end, a stick's disk fails every access, the controller frees
     the slot); a new stick's partitions and RitterFS are checked. Names
     stay reserved until the reboot: a device plugged in again gets its old
     name (by serial number, else by its IDs), in any socket;
   - **devices**: every disk (at the boot or plugged in later) and every
     partition in its table, GPT or MBR (a stick with a file system on
     the whole disk has none), with what each holds, found by its
     signature: RitterFS (label, generation), FAT12/16/32 (label), exFAT,
     NTFS, ext, APFS, or an empty start; programs see them in `/dev`
     (`/dev/nvme0n1`, `/dev/usbdisk0p2`, ...: raw bytes, root only;
     writes are refused where a mounted file system lies) and through the
     system call `device`; when a program closes a disk it wrote, the
     kernel reads its table again (unchanged partitions keep their mounts,
     a new RitterFS partition is mounted); a damaged GPT is read from its
     copy at the disk's end;
   - **elves** (manual, "The system"): programs kept running, like
     `sshd`: `elf add sshd sshd`, `elf stop sshd`; `elvesd` starts them
     at the boot and again when they end;
   - **the network** (manual, "The network"): Intel 8254x cards
     (`kernel/e1000.c`, QEMU's `e1000`), Intel 82576 cards
     (`kernel/igb.c`, QEMU's `igb`, the test PC's two-port card) and
     Realtek RTL8111/8168 and RTL8125 chips (`kernel/rtl8168.c`, the test
     PC's own port and the laptop's; tested on our own model of them in
     QEMU, `scripts/qemu/rtl8168.c`) as `eth0`, ...; Ethernet, ARP, IPv4
     and ICMP echo (`kernel/ipv4.c`; the loopback through a queue and a
     thread of its own, like a card), UDP (`udp.c`), DHCP (the elf
     `dhclient`: an address lent for a time, renewed at half of it), DNS
     (the elf `dnsd`: A records, CNAMEs, a cache by time to live; the
     library's `ritteros_resolve` asks it), TCP (`tcp.c`: the handshake,
     retransmission with a timeout measured per connection, fast
     retransmit and recovery, segments after a gap kept, slow start and
     congestion avoidance, the receiver's window and the persist timer,
     closing and TIME_WAIT),
     and sockets for programs (`socket.c`: file descriptors; UDP and TCP).
     SSH both ways (`sshd`, `ssh`; user/lib/ssh.c), on pseudo-terminals
     (`kernel/pty.c`: a terminal whose keyboard and screen are a program,
     with the console's line editing, its own foreground job and size;
     closing it ends its programs with SIGHUP).
     `login` runs `net up` at the start when there is a card: a file of
     settings per card in `/system/config/network/` (made for DHCP when
     missing); a card without an answer or a link keeps asking by itself;
   - **partition tables**: `disks` shows a disk in detail (blocks, free
     areas) and changes GPTs: a new table, add (1 MiB aligned, a size or
     the rest, a type and a name), delete, rename; root only, written
     after the disk's name is typed, the copy at the end first; `format`
     makes a new, empty RitterFS on a partition or a whole disk (the same
     bytes as `scripts/ritterfs.py format`), or with `-t fat` a FAT32 laid
     out as Windows does it, mounted at once;
   - **volumes**: every other RitterFS or FAT (sticks, more disks, found
     by its content: on a partition of any type or a whole disk; not EFI
     system partitions) is mounted at `/volumes/<label>` (listed by name),
     a RitterFS checked first, (`-2`, `-3` when labels repeat),
     read only if its check finds problems; `mount` and `unmount` (root)
     mount one elsewhere or unmount it; a path belongs to the mount with
     the longest matching path; pulling a stick out unmounts it; the
     system disk's EFI system partition is at `/boot`, read only;
   - **FAT12/16/32** (`kernel/fat.c`), read and written: long names
     (VFAT, UTF-16 to UTF-8) with 8.3 aliases as Windows makes them, names
     found without regard to case, both FAT copies and FAT32's FSInfo kept
     up to date, everything root's with rw for everyone (r only for files
     marked read only); behind one interface with RitterFS
     (`kernel/vfs.c`), which the system calls use;
   - **RitterFS** on partition 2: mount, full consistency check, directory
     tree, file reading verified against a manifest at every boot;
   - **threads** with a preemptive round-robin scheduler (20 ms time
     slices, an idle thread that halts the CPU); the kernel itself is
     preemptible: the timer can switch threads at the end of any interrupt
     unless the interrupted code had interrupts disabled. Shared data is
     guarded by spinlocks (page allocator, heap, scheduler) and mutexes
     (console output, RitterFS, NVMe and xHCI commands); waiting threads
     sleep on wait queues (input, disk and USB completions, timers) and
     leave the CPU to others;
   - a text console with its own font and `kprintf`;
   - a **terminal** after the boot: input from the serial port by interrupt
     (x86: COM1 IRQ 4 through the IO APIC; ARM: the PL011's GIC interrupt
     from the ACPI SPCR table) and from USB keyboards, line editing (as a
     Unix terminal's line discipline), a foreground job; the session appears
     on the serial port and on the screen. For full-screen programs, raw
     input (keys as typed, no echo; the cursor keys as VT100 sequences) and
     a few VT100 escape sequences on the screen: cursor position and moves,
     erasing lines and the screen, reverse video, bold, 16 colors, hiding
     the cursor; the cursor is drawn as a block (the cell's colors
     swapped). When such a program ends, the kernel switches back to lines
     and normal colors, whatever the program left behind. The kernel starts
     `/system/<arch>/bin/login` in it, which starts the users' shells; when
     login ends (Ctrl-D at its prompt), cannot start, or
     `rescue` is on the command line, the **rescue console** (`rescue>`)
     takes over: the kernel's own commands for checking the hardware and
     the file system (nothing that shows or changes file contents):

     | Command | Does |
     |---|---|
     | `help` | list the commands and the programs |
     | `uptime`, `mem` | time since the timer started; free and usable memory (exact: the heap's cached empty pages are returned first) |
     | `ls [PATH]` | list a RitterFS directory (same format as `scripts/ritterfs.py ls`) |
     | `pci` | the PCI devices |
     | `usb` | the USB devices: port, IDs, speed, function, names |
     | `disks` | the disks and partitions (the same text as the program `disks`) |
     | `net` | the network cards, their settings and counters, the ARP cache (the same text as the program `net`) |
     | `threads` | the kernel's threads: state, timer ticks, switches |
     | `ps` | the processes: ID, parent, group, state, name |
     | `run PATH [ARGS]`, or a program's name | run a program as a job in its own process group (names are looked up in `/system/<arch>/bin`); Ctrl-C stops the job |
     | `poweroff`, `reboot` | x86: ACPI sleep state S5 (sleep type from the DSDT's `\_S5_`) and the FADT reset register; ARM: PSCI `SYSTEM_OFF` / `SYSTEM_RESET` |
3. **The shell** (`user/bin/sh.c`): prompt `ritteros:/path$`, words split at
   spaces (quotes keep them), built-in `cd`, `pwd`, `exit`, `help`; other
   commands are programs from `/system/<arch>/bin`, or a path (relative to
   the current directory). Each command runs as a job in a process group of
   its own that gets the terminal while it runs (Ctrl-C stops it); `&` runs
   it in the background, and the shell reports its end before a later
   prompt. `> FILE`, `>> FILE` and `< FILE` give a program a file as its
   output (replaced, or added at the end) or input. `;` separates commands
   on a line, and a word with `*` becomes the names it matches (sorted).
   Up/Down recall earlier commands, Left/Right/Home/End move in the line.
   Ctrl-C at the prompt cancels the line, Ctrl-D ends the shell.
   **`disks`** lists the disks and their partitions, for example:

   ```
   nvme0n1          1.0 GiB  NVMe 'QEMU NVMe Ctrl', GPT, 2 partitions; the system disk
     nvme0n1p1     64.0 MiB  #1 EFI system partition: FAT32 'EFI'
     nvme0n1p2    958.0 MiB  #2 RitterOS: RitterFS 'RitterOS', generation 14, mounted at /
   usbdisk1        64.0 MiB  USB 'QEMU QEMU HARDDISK', MBR, 1 partition
     usbdisk1p1    63.0 MiB  #1 type 0x0C (FAT32 (LBA)): FAT32 'STICK'
   usbdisk2        16.0 MiB  USB 'QEMU QEMU HARDDISK', no partition table: empty
   ```
   **`edit FILE`** is a full-screen text editor (`user/bin/edit.c`): typing
   inserts at the cursor, the arrow keys, Home, End, Page Up and Page Down
   move, Backspace and Delete erase, Enter breaks the line; long lines and
   files scroll. A bar in reverse video shows the name, "(modified)" and
   the cursor's line and column; the last line shows the keys or a message
   ("Saved: 3 lines, 20 bytes.", "Not saved: Permission denied."). Ctrl-S
   saves (a missing final line break is added), Ctrl-Q quits, and asks
   once more if there are unsaved changes. The text is a gap buffer of at
   most 1 MiB (programs have no `malloc` yet); after each key only the
   rows that changed are sent. It draws for the screen's size (the
   serial port gets the same output): in `make run`, use the QEMU window,
   or make the Terminal window at least that large (139x48 at QEMU's
   x86 1280x800).
   `help` lists the built-ins and every program with its own one-line help
   (each program declares it with `PROGRAM_HELP("usage", "description")`;
   it is kept in the ELF file's `.ritteros.help` section, which is not
   loaded). Programs: `ls`, `cat`, `echo`, `edit`, `disks`, `mount`, `unmount`, `uptime`, `mem`, `ps`,
   `threads`, `pci`, `usb`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `rm`, `mv`, `cp`, `touch`, `chmod`, `chown`, `id`, `useradd`, `passwd`, `login`, `snapshot`, `versions`, `attr`, `find`, `format`, `clear`, `poweroff`, `reboot`, `hello`, `false`: everything
   the rescue console offers is available in the shell too (`ps`,
   `threads`, `pci`, `usb` print the kernel's own reports, the same text as
   in the rescue console).
4. **Programs** (`user/`): ELF executables on RitterFS
   (`/system/<arch>/bin`, test programs in `/system/<arch>/test`), each a
   **process**: its own address space in the lower half, its own thread
   (scheduled with all others), run in **user mode** (x86 ring 3, ARM EL0).
   They reach the kernel only through system calls (`common/syscalls.h`;
   x86 `syscall`, ARM `svc`): `read`, `write`, `open`, `close`, `exit`,
   `spawn` (start a child, optionally in a new process group), `wait` (for
   a child; the status as on Unix: exit code, or the signal that stopped
   it; optionally without waiting), `kill`, `getpid`, `getpgrp`, `sleep`,
   `stat`, `readdir`, `chdir`, `getcwd` (each process has a current
   directory, inherited by its children; relative paths work everywhere),
   `foreground` (`tcsetpgrp`: which job has the terminal), `signal` (a
   process may ignore Ctrl-C: it then only interrupts a waiting call, EINTR),
   `sysinfo` (uptime, memory), `power`, `report` (the kernel's ps, threads,
   pci and usb reports as text), `seek` (`lseek`), `terminal` (raw input
   and the screen's size in characters, for full-screen programs),
   `device` (the list of disks and partitions; `/dev/NAME` reads and
   writes them raw),
   `mount`, `unmount`, `time` (the clock: read from the firmware once at
   the boot, then carried on by the CPU's counter). `stat` reports which file system a file is on (a
   mount point differs from its parent).
   An ended process stays a zombie until its parent waits; children of an
   ended process become orphans and are freed when they end. Faults are
   signals (SIGSEGV, SIGILL, SIGFPE); Ctrl-C sends SIGINT to the foreground
   job's process group. Programs may use floating point and SIMD: the
   scheduler saves and restores their FP/SIMD registers (x86 FXSAVE, ARM
   V0-V31 with FPCR/FPSR) when it switches between them; the kernel itself
   never uses them. Standard input is the terminal
   (a line at a time, edited by the kernel; Ctrl-D ends it), unless the
   shell redirects it; children get their parent's standard input, output
   and error. Files are read and written on RitterFS (`open` with
   `O_WRONLY`, `O_RDWR`, `O_CREAT`, `O_TRUNC`, `O_APPEND`); a written file
   is committed when it is closed or its program ends, and every 8 MiB in
   between. Arguments arrive as `argc`/`argv`. The kernel checks
   every pointer against the program's page tables. A program that faults,
   or is stopped with Ctrl-C, ends; the kernel goes on. The programs' C
   library (`user/lib`, standard headers in `user/include`): `printf`,
   `dprintf`, `snprintf`, `puts`, the `string.h` functions, `strerror`,
   `errno`, `open`/`read`/`write`/`close`, `exit`, `spawn`, `wait`/`waitpid`
   (`sys/wait.h`), `kill` (`signal.h`), `getpid`, `sleep`/`usleep`; `printf` also does `%f`;
   `stat` (`sys/stat.h`), `opendir`/`readdir` (`dirent.h`), `chdir`,
   `getcwd`, `getpgrp`, `tcsetpgrp`, `signal(SIGINT, SIG_IGN)`. Programs: `cat`, `echo`,
   `hello`, `false`.
5. **RitterFS** ([`docs/ritterfs.md`](docs/ritterfs.md)): the RitterOS file
   system. Copy-on-write (crash-safe without a journal), a CRC-32C checksum in
   every block pointer, all metadata stored twice, scrub and self-repair;
   snapshots: read-only states that share their blocks with the live one.
   `snapshot` lists them, `snapshot create NAME` takes one, `snapshot
   delete NAME` deletes one (and frees what only it kept); each is readable
   at `/.snapshots/NAME/...` (`ls`, `cat`, `cd`, even running an old
   program from there). Taking and deleting snapshots writes to the disk,
   but only because the user asked. `versions PATH` shows a file's
   history: its version in each snapshot, oldest first, and now, each
   with its size and whether it changed. Instant copies (so far in the kernel:
   `ritterfs_copy`): a file or a directory tree copied without copying its
   data; both share the blocks until one of them changes. Attributes:
   key=value pairs on files and directories (keys up to 63 bytes, values
   up to 1 KiB), in one attribute table (on-disk version 3); `attr PATH
   [KEY | KEY=VALUE | -KEY]` lists, shows, sets and removes them (written
   at once, because the user asked), `find [DIR] [-name PATTERN] [-attr
   KEY[=VALUE]]` searches by name and attributes; in a snapshot they are
   as they were, and read-only.

## Kernel command line

Stored in `\ritteros\cmdline.txt` on the EFI system partition (`make run
CMDLINE="..."` writes it) and passed to the kernel in `struct boot_info`.

| Option | Effect |
|---|---|
| `video=<W>x<H>` | screen mode, e.g. `video=1024x768` (default: the firmware's mode, normally native) |
| `noruntime` | the bootloader skips `SetVirtualAddressMap`; the kernel runs without UEFI runtime services (no firmware clock). For firmware that fails at that call |
| `selftest` | run the large self-tests at the boot: page allocator, heap, scheduler (preemption), exceptions, the RitterFS tree and all files of `/test/manifest.txt` (`make test` sets it on every test boot; a normal boot skips them and is quicker, the RitterFS check always runs) |
| `nomodules` | load no kernel module (67a: the drivers in `/system/<arch>/drivers/`); the boot line says so |
| `memory=N` | (35b) give out only the first N MiB of usable memory (at least 64; the rest stays unused), so swap can be seen at work on a machine with plenty (swap_shell_test: `memory=128`); the boot's `memory:` line ends "(memory=: the rest unused)" |
| `cpus=N` | start at most N of the processor's cores (65b-65c); every core is started otherwise (the default since 65c-4, after the J1900 and the laptop ran on all of theirs), and all run threads. `cpus=1`: the boot core alone. `cpus` lists them |
| `lockcheck` | check the order the kernel takes its spinlocks in (65a-5): an order that closes a circle (B while holding A, after A while holding B, or through other locks) panics before it can deadlock two cores; `threads` reports the names and orders seen (`make test` sets it on every test boot, next to `selftest`). Sleeping or taking a mutex while holding a spinlock panics always |
| `memtest` | allocate, write, verify and free every free page (~2 s) |
| `sleeptest` | (37g-1, x86) sleep in ACPI S3 for 3 s at the end of the boot, woken by the RTC's alarm; with `rescue` (the devices' state is not brought back yet) |
| `disktest` | write/flush/read back 64 KiB at the end of partition 2 and keep a boot record there that the next boot reports |
| `usbdisktest` | the same on the RitterOS partition of every USB stick |
| `satadisktest` | the same on the RitterOS partition of every SATA disk |
| `netloss=N` | TCP loses one segment in N, coming and going (the tests' lossy network) |
| `usblate` | the USB controllers' first look at their ports finds nothing, as when a link comes up late (tests the wait for the boot stick) |
| `fswrite` | RitterFS write test: three commits under `/work`, each checked, then read back |
| `fstorture` | up to 5000 random RitterFS commits under `/t` (the test suite kills the VM meanwhile) |
| `fscopy` | RitterFS instant copy test under `/copy`: a directory with a 300 KiB file copied without its data, the copy changed apart from the original, the original removed and swept, one more copy that stays |
| `fsattr` | RitterFS attribute test under `/attr`: set, read back, replace, remove, bad keys and values refused, a table larger than a block, a removed file's attributes dropped, copies keeping them |
| `fstxn` | RitterFS transaction test under `/txn`: uncommitted changes of every kind thrown away, then a commit as usual |
| `fssnap` | RitterFS snapshot test under `/snap`: snapshot 'before', changes, the old state read through it, snapshot 'after', 'before' deleted and swept ('after' stays) |
| `scrub` | RitterFS scrub: verify every copy of every block, repair damaged metadata copies in place |
| `scrub=relocate` | the same, but damaged blocks move to new places through a commit |
| `crash=<kind>` | deliberate crash after the boot log: `pf` `null` `wp` `nx` `stack` (memory protection), `doublefree` `heapdouble` `heapoverflow` (allocator panics), `spinlock` `locksleep` `lockorder` (lock checks), and per architecture `ud` `de` `gp` `df` (x86) or `ud` `align` `pc` `sp` (ARM) |

Normal boots never write to a disk; only `disktest`, `usbdisktest`, `satadisktest`,
`fswrite`, `fstorture`, `fssnap`, `fscopy`, `fsattr`, `fstxn` and `scrub` do; and programs
the user runs to change the file system (`mkdir`, `rmdir`, `rm`, `mv`, `cp`, `touch`,
`attr`, `snapshot create`/`delete`, a program writing a file, `>` in the shell):
each change is one commit, written at once (a written file when it is closed).

## Source layout

| Path | Contents |
|---|---|
| `boot/` | UEFI bootloader (PE32+), architecture-independent: `main.c` flow, `rfs_boot.c` (the RitterOS partition: the boot disk's GPT read through UEFI Disk I/O, the partition's handle by its device path, read with `common/rfs_read.c`), `loader.c` ELF loading, `memmap.c`, `gop.c` graphics, `sysinfo.c` firmware tables, `console.c` firmware text output; `boot_arch.h` is what each architecture provides |
| `common/` | shared by bootloader and kernel: `boot_info.h` (the hand-over), `vmlayout.h` (also the program layout), `syscalls.h` (system call numbers, shared with programs), `efi.h`, `elf.h`, framebuffer, serial output (`serial_putc` per architecture), `cmdline.c`, `string.c`, `crc32.c`; RitterFS reading: `rfs_format.h` (on-disk structures), `rfs_read.c` (superblock choice, content trees with checksums, directories, path lookup) |
| `kernel/` | kernel (ELF64), architecture-independent: `main.c` boot sequence; `arch_api.h` (what each architecture provides); memory: `paging.h` `pmm` `heap` `memory.h`; output: `console` `font` `kprintf` `panic`; firmware: `acpi` `madt` `smbios` `uefi` `devpath`; `terminal` (input buffer for the serial port and keyboards, line editing, commands); devices: `pci` (scan, capabilities, MSI-X, MSI) `ahci` (SATA disks) `ehci` (EHCI controllers taken from the firmware) `nvme` `blockdev.h` `gpt` `xhci` (USB controller: rings, slots, device contexts, control transfers) `usb` (descriptors, strings, device list, drivers) `keyboard` (keyboards of any kind: names, US layout, modifiers, repeat) `usbkbd` (USB keyboards) `usbhub` (USB hubs) `usbstorage` (USB sticks: bulk-only transport, SCSI); `timer` (the tick, tick handlers); `sched` (threads, scheduler, wait queues) `sync` (spinlocks, mutexes); `process` (processes: loading, threads, exit and wait, signals, process groups, system calls); `report` (ps, threads, pci, usb reports for the rescue console and for programs); RitterFS: `rfs_cache` (block cache), `rfs_dirindex` (directory index), `ritterfs.c` (mount, check; reading is `common/rfs_read.c` plus the uncommitted changes), `rfs_write.c` (changes, commits), `rfs_scrub.c`; tests: `crashtest` `disktest` `fstest` |
| `arch/x86_64/` | x86-64: `arch.h`/`arch.c` (interface, crash kinds), `start.S`, `gdt`, `idt`, `isr.S`, `exceptions`, `apic` (Local APIC, its timer, 8259 masking, MSI vectors, IO APIC), `uart_input.c` (COM1 receive interrupt), `power.c` (ACPI power off, reset register), `user.S`/`user.c` (ring 3: entering with IRETQ, the SYSCALL entry and its MSRs; a new thread's first stack), `switch.S` (context switch), `fpu.S` (programs' FP/SIMD registers), `mmio.h` (device register access), `paging.c`, `timer.c` (PIT delays), `uart.c` (16550 COM1), `io.h`, `cpu.h`; `boot_arch.c` (bootloader page tables, jump to the kernel) |
| `arch/aarch64/` | 64-bit ARM: `arch.h`/`arch.c`, `start.S` (SP_EL0 kernel stack, SP_EL1 exception stack), `vectors.S` + `exceptions.c` (VBAR_EL1, ESR/FAR decoding, nested-exception guard), `gic` (GICv3, virtual timer tick, GICv2m MSI frame, SPI routing), `uart_input.c` (PL011 receive interrupt), `power.c` (PSCI power off and reset), `user.S`/`user.c` (EL0: entering with ERET, cache maintenance for program code; a new thread's first stack; `vectors.S` handles exceptions from EL0 on the program's kernel stack and kernel IRQs on the thread's own stack), `switch.S` (context switch), `fpu.S` (programs' FP/SIMD registers), `mmio.h` (device register access with fixed `ldr`/`str` instructions), `timer.c` (generic timer delays), `uart.c` (PL011), `cpu.h` (system registers); `paging.c` (kernel translation tables in TTBR1: image with per-section permissions, direct map of RAM, framebuffer non-cacheable, devices mapped on demand, lower half off); `uart.h`; `boot_arch.c` (TTBR0/TTBR1 tables with Normal/Device memory types, cache maintenance, jump) |
| `user/` | programs (dynamically linked since 22c), `ldso/` (the dynamic loader `ld.so`, with `ldso.ld` and `arch/<arch>/ldso.S`), `include/` (standard headers: `stdio.h`, `string.h`, `unistd.h`, `fcntl.h`, `stdlib.h`, `errno.h`; `ritteros.h`) and `lib/` (the programs' C library), `arch/<arch>/start.S` (`_start`, the system call instruction), `user.ld` (linked at 0x400000), `bin/` (`sh`, `ls`, `cat`, `echo`, `uptime`, `mem`, `ps`, `threads`, `pci`, `usb`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `rm`, `mv`, `cp`, `touch`, `chmod`, `chown`, `id`, `useradd`, `passwd`, `login`, `snapshot`, `versions`, `attr`, `find`, `edit`, `disks`, `mount`, `unmount`, `format`, `clear`, `poweroff`, `reboot`, `hello`, `false`), `test/` (`kwrite`, `privileged`, `syscalls`, `spin`, `loop`, `args`, `printf`, `files`, `counter`, `family`, `tree`, `orphan`, `fpu`, `term`, `devread`, `devwrite`, `mountuser`, `filesha`, ...) |
| `fonts/` | the X11 misc-fixed fonts, 13 faces (public domain, unmodified BDF; `SOURCE.txt`): installed as `/system/fonts/NAME.bdf`; 9x15 and 9x15B also compiled in (the console, libwindow's default) |
| `scripts/` | `setup-toolchain.sh`, `mkdisk.py` (GPT + FAT32 + RitterFS image), `ritterfs.py` (host RitterFS: format, check, ls, cat, put; `install()` is a copy-on-write commit), `bdf.py`/`bdf2c.py` (font conversion), `test.sh`, `check_screen.py`, `ppm2png.py` |
| `docs/` | `manual.md`: the user's manual (logging in, the shell, every program); `ritterfs.md`: RitterFS on-disk format |
| `sdk/` | building programs outside the tree: `CLAUDE.md` (the guide, for people and AI agents), `API.md` (every header, function and macro, generated from the headers by `scripts/sdkapi.py`), `ritteros.mk` (the build fragment a project includes: build, deploy, run and stop over ssh; the Makefile's `sdk` target builds the library and start code it links), `template/` (a new project), `examples/hellowin/` (a windowed app) |
| `build/` | generated: `esp/` (EFI partition staging: bootloaders, command line), `system/` (kernels and programs, installed as `/system` on RitterFS), `ritteros-<arch>.img` (one disk per architecture, 55a), `test/` |
| `tools/` | generated toolchain |

## The virtual machine

QEMU emulates a q35 PC (Intel ICH9 chipset) with 4 GiB RAM, OVMF UEFI
firmware, a virtio-rng random number generator, an xHCI USB controller (QEMU's: USB 3 on ports 1-4, USB 2 on
ports 5-8) with two USB 1.1 hubs in a chain on port 5 and a USB keyboard
behind them (`usb0 port 5.1.1`), as on many PCs whose ports are connected
through internal hubs, and one NVMe SSD (boot device) backed by
`build/ritteros-x86_64.img` (ARM: `build/ritteros-aarch64.img`; since 55a
one disk per architecture, each with only its own bootloader, kernel and
programs), a sparse 1 GiB file:

| Sectors (512 B) | Contents |
|---|---|
| 0 | protective MBR |
| 1 - 33 | primary GPT header and partition entries |
| 2048 - 133119 | 1: EFI system partition, FAT32, 64 MiB (from `build/esp/`): `\EFI\BOOT\BOOTX64.EFI`, `BOOTAA64.EFI`, `\ritteros\cmdline.txt` |
| 133120 - 2095103 | 2: RitterOS, ~958 MiB, type `B5CA1B3E-6278-48F0-BBEF-87907326A8B3`, RitterFS with `/system/x86_64/kernel.elf` and `/system/aarch64/kernel.elf` |
| last 33 | backup GPT |

`make` rewrites the partition tables and the EFI partition every time, but
formats partition 2 only if it holds no valid RitterFS, so RitterFS contents
survive rebuilds. A new RitterFS contains `/readme.txt`, `/test/` (files
covering every way content is stored, plus `/test/manifest.txt`) and
`/system/` (from `build/system/`). On an existing RitterFS, `make` installs
the kernels that changed with one copy-on-write commit, like the kernel's own
commits: new blocks, then the superblock with the next generation in the
other slot (`mkdisk.py` prints e.g. `RitterFS kept, installed
/system/x86_64/kernel.elf (generation 5)`, or `/system up to date`).

Look into the image from the Mac (never while QEMU is running):

```sh
python3 scripts/ritterfs.py check build/ritteros-x86_64.img 133120 1961984
python3 scripts/ritterfs.py ls    build/ritteros-x86_64.img 133120 1961984 /test
python3 scripts/ritterfs.py cat   build/ritteros-x86_64.img 133120 1961984 /readme.txt
python3 scripts/ritterfs.py put   build/ritteros-x86_64.img 133120 1961984 /notes/todo.txt todo.txt
```

## Virtual address space

| Virtual address | Contents |
|---|---|
| `0x0000000000000000` - `0x00007FFFFFFFFFFF` | lower half: empty (for user programs later); NULL faults |
| `0xFFFF800000000000` + P | direct map: physical address P (RAM, framebuffer, devices, firmware) |
| `0xFFFFFFFF80000000` | kernel image (linked here, `-mcmodel=kernel`) |

Constants in `common/vmlayout.h`; `phys_to_virt`/`virt_to_phys` in
`kernel/memory.h`.

## Testing

`make test` (`scripts/test.sh`) boots QEMU headless many times, reading the
serial log and taking screenshots through the QEMU monitor:

Every test runs on both architectures (x86-64 emulated, AArch64 with
hardware virtualization); ARM test names start with `arm-`.

| Boots | What is checked |
|---|---|
| normal, arm | the whole boot log; a session typed into the shell (`help` lists every program with a description, built-ins, quotes, `cd` and relative paths, `ls`/`cat` identical to the host tool, a background job, Ctrl-C at the prompt, the USB keyboard, `ps`/`threads`/`pci`/`usb` with `pci` and `usb` identical to the rescue console's), then `exit` and the rescue console (`help`, `ls`, `pci`, `usb`, `threads`, `ps`); two kernel threads that never wait take turns (preemption), only `main`, `idle`, `console` and `usb` remain afterwards; the kernel loaded from RitterFS; USB controller running (a No-Op command answered by interrupt), two hubs and the keyboard behind them enumerated (slot, address, descriptors, hub ports, `usb` command); lines typed on the USB keyboard through QEMU's `sendkey` (Shift, Backspace, Ctrl-C, Caps Lock, a held key repeating) (size and generation as the host sees them; on x86 the very kernel just built); the console pixel by pixel; paging; PCI devices; NVMe and GPT; RitterFS mount, check, tree and all 309 manifest files, phase times, the block cache (each block from the disk at most once); kernel and host checker agree; interrupt controller found, ~50 timer interrupts during a 500 ms busy wait; one NVMe interrupt per command (MSI-X); a terminal session typed on the serial port (commands, Backspace, Ctrl-C, an arrow key, `ls`, `cat`, `pci`) and its answers on serial and screen, `ls`/`cat` identical to the host tool |
| ps2 | x86 without a USB keyboard (like a laptop): the PS/2 keyboard becomes `kbd0`; the USB keyboard test's lines typed on it (Shift, Backspace, Ctrl-C, Caps Lock, a held key repeating once per RitterOS repeat, not the keyboard's own) |
| noruntime | `noruntime`: the bootloader skips `SetVirtualAddressMap`, the kernel boots without runtime services |
| noserial | x86 without any serial port (`-serial none`: COM1's ports read 0xFF): bootloader and kernel notice there is no UART and boot to the shell; screenshots are the only witness (ARM's `virt` always has its PL011) |
| power | `reboot` in the terminal, then `poweroff` after the second boot: QEMU must exit by itself |
| programs | programs run from the terminal in user mode: `hello`, an exit code, writing into the kernel and a privileged instruction stop only the program, bad system call arguments are refused, timer interrupts during a computing program; `echo`, `cat` (files, errors, standard input until Ctrl-D), arguments, `printf` conversions, file reading and its errors, Ctrl-C while computing and while reading; processes side by side (interleaving output), a child computing forever does not block others, `kill`, exit codes and signals of children, `spawn`/`wait`/`kill` errors, Ctrl-C stops a job of two processes, an orphan is cleaned up, `ps` empty afterwards; three programs computing with floating point at the same time get bit-exact results; threads of one program (`threads`): four computing in parallel with floating point, bit-identical to the same work alone, an atomic counter, 15 at once and the 16th refused, joining; `exit` from main, a fault in a thread and Ctrl-C end all threads; no memory lost |
| memtest | every free page (about 1,046,000) written and verified; random seeds differ between boots |
| persist-1/2 | raw disk writes survive a reboot; verified in the image file from the host |
| fsdamage | a damaged metadata copy is detected; the bootloader and every file read still use the intact copy |
| fswrite + reboot | three RitterFS commits; the host verifies the written content |
| snapshell-1/2 | snapshots from the shell: `snapshot create one`, the list, `ls`/`cat` in `/.snapshots/one` identical to the host tool, `cd` and `pwd` inside it, a program run from it, the errors (exists, bad name, not found); the host then changes `/readme.txt`; the next boot shows the new text live and the old one in the snapshot, `versions` (changed, unchanged, a relative path, a missing file, the errors), `snapshot delete one` frees exactly the blocks the host counted as kept for it, and the host check is clean |
| fscopy, fscopy-nopin, fscopy-reboot | instant copies: 300 KiB copied with only metadata written, 77 blocks shared (75 data blocks, the index block twice), the copy changed apart from the original, the original removed and swept (the pin drops to 0), a lasting copy; host and kernel agree on shared blocks, pin and held-back frees; with the pin set to 0 both checks report the blocks used twice; a host commit over the copy; after a reboot the kernel agrees with the host |
| fsattr, fsattr-bad, fsattr-reboot | attributes: set, replaced, removed, bad ones refused, 40 more growing the table past a block (filler records), a removed file's attributes gone (its object ID's next owner has none), copies keep them; host and kernel count the same; a host commit keeps them; an attribute of a missing object (made by a valid host commit) is found by both checks; after a reboot the kernel agrees |
| attrshell | attributes from the shell: `attr` sets, lists, shows and removes them, with the errors (no such attribute, no such file); in a snapshot they are visible and read-only; `find -name '*.txt'` identical to a host walk of the same tree, `find -attr` with a key, with a value, one that matches nothing, from `/`, and combined with `-name`; the host check counts what is left |
| fstxn | transactions: a new directory with a 100 KiB file, an attribute and a removed `/readme.txt`, uncommitted, then aborted: everything as before (generation, blocks in use, the file's content); a commit afterwards works; the host check agrees |
| fsshell | file system management from the shell: `mkdir` (and `-p`), `cp` of a file into a directory, `cp -r` of a directory (identical to the original listing), `mv` renaming and moving into a directory, `rm`, `rm -r`, `rmdir`; a removed file and directory restored with `cp` from a snapshot (the host finds them identical to the originals); the errors (exists, not empty, is a directory, not a directory, a directory into itself, read-only snapshots); the host check is clean and sees the shared blocks |
| writeshell | writing files: `echo >`, `>>`, a quoted `>`, `cat <`, `cat` of a text file into a new file, `>` replacing a longer file, typed text into `cat > FILE` (the job has the terminal before it reads), `touch`, the shell's errors; the test program `fwrite`: 10 MiB written and read back (past the 8 MiB intermediate commit), read-write with a seek, a 1 MiB hole, append, truncate, the refusals; the host reads every file as written (the hole not stored) and the check is clean |
| (writeshell) perm | permissions: root prepares files with different owners and rights, the test program `perm` becomes user 1000 and is refused reading a 0600 file, writing, creating, removing and renaming in root's directory, chmod of another's file, chown, a 0700 directory (stat, list, cd), running a 0644 program, copying an unreadable file, attributes, snapshots, becoming root again and switching the machine off; it may create files in its own directory (its owner, mode 644), chmod them, run a 0755 program and copy a readable file (the copy is its own); `ls -l` shows the modes and owners |
| usershell | SHA-256, HMAC-SHA256 and PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 (our own code) identical to Python's `hashlib`; two random draws from the kernel differ; `useradd` (a user ID from 1000, `/home/anna` owned by it, refusing a second `anna` and a bad name), `passwd` (typed twice without being shown, the two differing, an unknown user); `/system/config/users` 0644, `/system/config/passwords` 0600; the host recomputes the stored hash from its salt |
| termshell | the terminal for full-screen programs, with the test program `term`: the size in characters; raw input (a typed byte, the USB keyboard's up arrow as `ESC [ A`, Ctrl-C and Ctrl-D as bytes) not echoed; lines again; screenshots of the escape sequences (cursor position, reverse, green, bold, erase to the end of the line and the whole line, a sequence split between two writes, cursor moves, the last column and the wrap that waits for the next character, the cursor shown and hidden); the program ends with reverse video on, the cursor hidden and raw input on, and the shell afterwards looks and works as before; then `clear` after a listing: the screen empty but for the prompt and the next command at the top |
| editshell | the editor `edit`: a directory and a missing name refused; a new file typed and saved (a screenshot: text, bar, message, cursor); changed with Delete, End, the arrows, Backspace and Enter, some keys and Ctrl-S from the USB keyboard; quitting with unsaved changes (asked once more, the file unchanged); saving into a missing directory fails with the reason; two pages down and End in a 100-line file; `cat` and the host read what was saved |
| disksshell | three more USB sticks (a RitterOS image with GPT, an MBR stick with FAT32, a blank one): `disks` identical to what `scripts/expect_disks.py` finds in the image files; `ls -l /dev` (every disk and partition, root's, mode 600), `ls` inside `/dev`; `mkdir` and `rm` there refused (read-only); the test program `devread`: raw bytes of three devices identical to the image files (SHA-256), `stat`, opening for writing allowed except for the mounted partition, a user who is not root refused; a blank stick plugged in behind the hub appears in `disks`, and is gone again when unplugged |
| volumesshell | two RitterOS sticks with the same label and a FAT32 one: mounted at `/volumes/RitterOS` and `RitterOS-2` at the boot; `mount` lists them, `ls` and `cat` as the host reads the sticks; a file written on a volume and moved to the system disk, a file and a directory tree copied to the other volume (the host finds them on the stick images, and their checks clean); `unmount` refused while the shell is inside, then done; mounted on a directory (its own content hidden, back afterwards); the refusals (mounted already, FAT32, the system partition, no such device, not mounted, `mkdir` in `/volumes`, `rm -r` and `mv` of a volume, `rm -r` of a directory with a volume inside); a user who is not root may not mount or unmount; a third stick plugged in becomes `RitterOS-3` and is gone when pulled out; `disks` identical to the host's reading, and the same in the rescue console |
| partsshell | `disks` changing partition tables on three more USB sticks (blank, a RitterOS image, an MBR stick with FAT32): a blank stick's detail, `init` not confirmed and confirmed, `add` of 16 MiB and of the rest with a type and a name, no room left, `rename`, `delete` (each with the detail it prints); the refusals (no table, an MBR, a bad type, mounted partitions); the test program `devwrite`: a mounted partition not opened for writing, its blocks not written through the disk, `mount` refused while the disk is open for writing, bytes written across a block boundary; the RitterOS partition deleted and added again with `rest` (mounted again, readable); its table's first copy damaged (the copy at the end is read), then both (the mounted partition stays until it is unmounted); `disks` as `scripts/expect_disks.py` reads the images; the host checks both copies of the tables written (`scripts/gptcheck.py`: checksums, positions, the protective MBR) and the bytes written |
| formatshell | `format` on a blank stick's two partitions (one of the type "data"), on a RitterOS stick's partition (958 MiB: a bitmap tree of height 1) and on a whole 20 GiB stick with a GPT (height 2); not confirmed; the refusals (mounted, a whole disk with a mounted partition, a bad label, no such device); each new file system checked by the kernel and mounted at once; `disks` shows them; two sticks pulled out and plugged in again: mounted by their content (the "data" partition, the whole disk), a file written before still there; the host checks each one, finds it identical to what `scripts/ritterfs.py` formats with the same label, UUID and time (`scripts/samefs.py`), and finds no partition table left on the whole disk |
| fatshell | three FAT sticks made on the host by `scripts/fat.py` (FAT32 with an MBR, FAT16 and FAT12 on the whole disk; long, Unicode and lower-case short names, a directory of 60 entries over several clusters, a 320 KB file in pieces, empty and read-only files; checked with macOS's `fsck_msdos` while writing the tool): mounted read only at `/volumes/<label>`, the system's EFI partition at `/boot`; `ls` as the host's tree, `ls -l` modes, `cat` with names in another case, SHA-256 of the big file on each kind and of the bootloaders in `/boot` against the host; writing, removing, `mkdir` refused, `attr` not supported; `cp` and `cp -r` to RitterFS; a current directory on FAT; unmount and mount again; `disks` as `scripts/expect_disks.py` reads the images |
| exfatshell | two exFAT sticks made by `scripts/exfat.py` (an MBR partition and a whole disk): mount (read only), listings as made on the host, file contents by SHA-256, names without regard to case through the up-case table (`üNÏCÖDÉ – ✓.TXT`: failed with the table ignored), a long name over several entries, a directory in one run over several clusters, a file in one run and one in pieces, the zeros past the valid data, a copy to RitterFS, disks with the labels |
| exfatwrite | changing the exFAT sticks and a small third one: new files and a long name, appending, overwriting, a new folder, 320 KB copied in, appending to a file in one run (it becomes a FAT chain) and to a partly written one (the gap reads as zeros), a tree of 60 entries copied in (the folder grows), moves, a rename of case, `rm -r`, the refusals (a folder not empty, `a:b`), chmod, the small stick filled up; on the host `scripts/exfat.py check` and macOS's `fsck_exfat` on every stick (failed with the set checksums not made again), what was written read back, today's date |
| usblate | like the AMD laptop (since 48b with an NVMe drive holding a copy of the stick, which must not become the system): no NVMe, the RitterOS stick on a second xHCI controller, and `usblate`: both controllers set up, the boot waits for the stick and finds its system partition, the shell runs from it; the USB keyboard, skipped by the first look as well, is set up by its hot plug thread and types (without the wait the test fails as the laptop did) |
| fatwrite | changing the three FAT sticks: new files and long names, appending, overwriting, a new folder, copies in (320 KB from RitterFS, a tree with a Unicode name from another FAT volume), moving files and a folder, a rename that only changes the case, removing files and a tree, the refusals (a folder not empty, a name FAT cannot hold, `/boot`), `chmod` as the read-only attribute, the FAT12 stick filled up; the host checks every image with `scripts/fat.py check` (both FAT copies, chains, lost clusters, FSInfo) and macOS's `fsck_msdos -n`, reads the long names, contents and date written, and finds what was removed gone |
| growshell | the user's case: a RitterOS image on a 2 GiB stick (its GPT made for 1 GiB): `disks` shows the free space up to the end and says why; `disks add ... rest` fits the table to the whole disk; the host finds both copies of the GPT and the protective MBR covering the disk, the RitterFS untouched |
| fatformat | `format -t fat`: a FAT32 on a 47 MiB "data" partition (512-byte clusters) and on a whole 9 GiB stick (8 KiB), a 16 MiB partition and a too long label refused; mounted at once with upper-case labels, written to; the host finds both clean (`scripts/fat.py check`, macOS's `fsck_msdos`) and the boot sector as Windows expects it |
| exfatformat | `format -t exfat`: a 16 MiB partition without a label (4 KiB clusters, mounted by its device's name), a 496 MiB partition (32 KiB) and a whole 40 GiB stick (128 KiB, the label 'Fotos Señor'); a too long label and one with ':' refused; each written to at once; on the host `scripts/exfat.py check`, macOS's `fsck_exfat`, the label and the files read back, the boot sector's layout (one FAT, from 1 MiB on beyond 64 MiB, the heap on a cluster boundary) |
| osinstall, osinstalled | `osinstall` (48a) from the boot disk onto a second disk (x86: SATA, ARM: a second NVMe drive) holding a mounted exFAT: stopped at the first question, a wrong answer, then the install (the exFAT unmounted first); on the host the new GPT (`gptcheck.py`) with GUIDs other than the stick's, the EFI partition (`fat.py check`, the bootloader at the fallback path, the command line without `selftest`), the RitterFS (`ritterfs.py check`; `/system/<arch>` only, every program and the kernel byte for byte, no test programs). Then that disk alone boots: the firmware finds the bootloader, the system partition is the new disk's, the first start asks for root's password, login and the shell work |
| typing | text beyond ASCII: UTF-8 typed on the serial port (echoed whole, a two-byte character erased whole by Backspace, a file name), Ctrl-L (a screenshot: the line again at the top of an empty screen, its glyphs drawn), the USB keyboard as US International (dead keys, AltGr) |
| configshell | `/system/config`: a disk in the old layout (users and passwords in `/system`, made on the host): `login` moves both, root's password still works, nothing is left behind, the README is there; `config_get` reads `key = value` lines (spaces, a comment, a missing key, a missing file) |
| syntax | the shell's `;` (two commands, `cd` then `pwd`, empty ones skipped) and `*` (sorted names with a space and UTF-8, a middle part `/system/*/bin/sh`, `*/` for directories, relative names; no match and quotes as typed; 300 names refused, more than 128 words), `uptime; echo` |
| guard | the firewall's kernel half with the test program `guardload`: `in = drop` with rules for ping and the Mac (port 7000 from 10.0.2.0/24 on eth0); replies to the machine's own pings pass; without the rule the Mac's connection times out (a closed port would answer "refused"), the drop recorded and counted; `drop out icmp` stops a ping (Operation not permitted); `off` lets the Mac in again; rules read back in words, wrong ones explained |
| guardd | the firewall's elf: `net up` makes its elf file, its first start the rules (ping, sshd, dnsd let in), loaded; a rule added to the file is loaded within seconds and lets the Mac in (port 7000); a wrong line named, the rules before stay (the Mac still gets in); without the rule the Mac's connection is dropped (a timeout); `elf stop guardd` switches the checks off (a closed port refuses at once again; `net up` says so); `elf start guardd` loads them again |
| guardlimit | `limit in tcp 7000 2/minute`: the Mac's first two connections get in, the third is dropped (a timeout: nobody listens, a closed port would refuse), guard shows the rule and the drop by it; a minute after the first, one gets in again |
| logs | the logs as a real system keeps them (logd on): `kernel.log` from the kernel's first line, dated, without a line typed for `cat` or programs' output; root's login in `login.log`; a program's output in the log it named, a written line; an elf's output in its log, elvesd's lines in `elvesd.log`; a 65,530-byte log becomes `big.log.old` and a new one begins; a full ring's loss noted; `elf stop logd`: it writes what is left first; SIGTERM ignored interrupts a sleep |
| netshell | the network on QEMU's user network: the e1000 card found (MAC, link), `/system/config/network` (put there by the host as the single file of before 42d, moved by `net up` into `/system/config/network/eth0`) applied by `login` at the start, `ping` to the gateway (through ARP), to our own address and 127.0.0.1 (the loopback: our echo replies), to an address nobody has; names through DNS (`scripts/testdns.py` on the Mac, set as `dns = 10.0.2.2:PORT`): an A record, a CNAME in other letter case, a name nobody knows, `localhost`, the cache (the server sees each name once); `net` shows the settings, counters and ARP cache, as the rescue console does |
| netloss | TCP losing one segment in 50 (`netloss=50`; at 1 in 10 QEMU's own TCP gives up): a short exchange and its FINs, 300,000 bytes each way, read slowly, and over the loopback, all intact; segments sent again after timeouts and fast, segments after gaps kept |
| fetch | `fetch` against a web server on the Mac (`scripts/testhttp.py`), by name (the tests' DNS server next to DHCP's address): a page, `-i`, 1,000,000 bytes saved to a file (SHA-256 checked), chunks, redirections (a path, a host name, a loop), 404, a body cut short, https and other schemes refused, an unknown name |
| pty | a shell on a pseudo-terminal (`user/test/ptytest`): echo, Backspace, the size, its own directory, Ctrl-C to a job, exit codes, CR LF line breaks; closing the master ends the shell and its job (SIGHUP); the console's shell works on |
| ssh | `sshd` from the Mac's OpenSSH (through hostfwd): an Ed25519 key in `authorized_keys` (a command, its exit code, a file, a session with `-tt`), the password right and wrong, the fingerprint as `ssh-keygen` shows it; then the client `ssh` here to the same server: the host key asked about and kept, the key in `id_ed25519` (made by the Mac), the remote exit code, the password, a typed session, a changed host key refused |
| lo | a machine without a network card: `lo` in the boot log and in `net` (with "no network cards"), no card set up by `login`; `ping` to 127.0.0.1, `localhost` and 127.9.8.7, UDP and 300,000 bytes of TCP between two threads, `sshd` and `ssh localhost` with a key; as many packets received as sent |
| route | a router between two QEMU networks of its own (`-netdev dgram`) on the two Intel 82576 ports (`igb`, each behind a PCIe port of its own), each with a host played by `scripts/testroute.py` on the Mac: the cards set from `/system/config/network/eth0` and `eth1`, the routes in `net`; forwarding off (dropped; the router's other address answers), on: both ways with the TTL one less and a right checksum, the first packet kept during ARP, the default route, "time exceeded", the machine's own pings by the routes; `forward = on` from `/system/config/routing` and `eth1` without a gateway: "network unreachable"; off again; the counters |
| igb | an Intel 82576 (`igb`) on QEMU's user network: found with its MAC address and link (after the PHY's negotiation), DHCP, `ping`, 1,000,000 bytes by `fetch` (SHA-256 checked; the receive ring goes round ten times), a page, `net`'s lines for the card (driver, model, counters, nothing left unsent) |
| realtek | three Realtek chips on our own QEMU model (`scripts/qemu/rtl8168.c`), each on a user network of its own: an RTL8168H with the laptop's ID, Killer's E2600 (newer: PHY through OCP, the RXDV gate, its family from its version, not its ID: 47b), an RTL8168E (older: PHYAR), an RTL8125 with the Killer E3000's ID (2.5 Gbit/s), every PHY starting powered down; found with version, MAC and link; DHCP on all three cards; `ping` to each gateway, 1,000,000 bytes by `fetch` through each (SHA-256 checked); a route per card; counters |
| dhcp | DHCP that waits: a card on a QEMU network of its own where host A of `scripts/testroute.py` can be a DHCP server, off at the start: `net up` makes `/system/config/network/eth0`, says the card keeps asking; the server switched on: the address comes by itself within 13 s; the link taken away (QEMU's `set_link`): `net up` says "no link", no address; the link back: the address back within seconds |
| security | malformed packets from the LAN must not hang or crash the machine (49a): host A completes a handshake to port 22 with an MSS option of 0, sends a UDP datagram to port 53 with the guest's own address as its source (a martian source), and a DNS response to port 53; the guest keeps answering shell commands and pings its gateway, and `net` still prints its tcp line (without the fixes `net` hangs on the held TCP lock) |
| sshcrypto | SHA-512, ChaCha20, Poly1305, X25519, Ed25519 (`user/lib`) on the RFCs' inputs, derived inputs and spoiled signatures, every line as `scripts/crypto_ref.py` (a Python reference from the RFCs, checked against their literal vectors) computes it |
| sockets | no settings on the disk: `net up` makes `/system/config/network/eth0` and gets an address by DHCP from QEMU's server; programs' sockets (`user/test/sock`) against services on the Mac (`scripts/testnet.py`): UDP echo, UDP over the loopback (a port in use, a receive that times out), TCP: a short exchange, 1,000,000 bytes each way checked byte by byte, 300,000 bytes over the loopback between two threads, a refused connection, a connection from the Mac to a listening program (QEMU's hostfwd); flow control: 1,000,000 bytes read slowly, and sent to a slow reader on the Mac |
| quick, arm-quick | a boot without `selftest`, as on a real PC: the large self-tests are skipped (none of their output), the RitterFS check still runs, and the boot ends sooner than the test boot with them |
| fssnap + reboot | snapshots: the old state read through a snapshot while the live one changed, a second snapshot, the first deleted and its blocks swept (exactly those the check counted as kept only for it); the host checker walks the snapshot and counts the same; a host commit over a shared file holds its frees back; a damaged block only the snapshot uses is found; after a reboot the kernel agrees with the host |
| torture 1-8 | random commits killed by SIGKILL at random moments; the image is always consistent at the last reported generation |
| scrub, relocate | six damaged metadata copies repaired (in place, or relocated by a commit); a damaged data block reported with its path |
| usbboot | a machine without NVMe, the RitterOS image as a USB stick, an xHCI controller with MSI only (like the J1900): the firmware boots from it, the kernel finds its system partition `usbdisk0p2` by the bootloader's GUID, all files verified through USB, the shell runs; commands typed on the serial port and on the USB keyboard; finally the stick is unplugged (the log says the system partition is gone) |
| sataboot | x86: a machine without NVMe, the RitterOS image on a SATA disk: the firmware boots from it, the kernel finds `sata0p2` by the bootloader's GUID, all files verified through AHCI, the write test with TRIM on the SATA disk and a host check afterwards, `ls` in the shell identical to the host; one MSI interrupt per command (ARM's firmware cannot boot from SATA) |
| usbhotplug | a keyboard without serial number plugged in, typed on, unplugged and plugged into another socket (same name); a stick plugged in (partitions, RitterFS check), unplugged and plugged into another socket (same name, "back again"); `usb` lists what is plugged in |
| usbhubplug | behind the first hub: a keyboard plugged in, typed on, unplugged; a stick plugged in (partitions, RitterFS check) and unplugged; a hub plugged in with a keyboard behind it, then the hub unplugged: the keyboard goes first, then the hub |
| satadisk | two SATA disks next to NVMe (x86: q35's AHCI controller, ARM: an `ich9-ahci` card): `sata0`/`sata1`, their GPT, a full RitterFS check of each, `satadisktest` writes on both and the host finds the boot records in the disk files |
| usbstorage | an xHCI controller without MSI or MSI-X (the driver polls); an Intel EHCI controller next to xHCI, taken over and stopped; two USB sticks (fresh RitterOS images), one on a USB 3 port (SuperSpeed), one behind the hub (full speed): `usbdisk0`/`usbdisk1`, their GPT, a full RitterFS check of each, a write test whose boot records the host finds in the stick files |
| kupdate, knone, kdamage | a changed kernel installed by the host's copy-on-write commit boots from generation 2, `mkdisk.py` reinstalls the built one exactly once; no `/system`: the bootloader stops with "file not found"; one flipped bit in a kernel data block: the bootloader refuses it (checksum) |
| crash-* | 14 deliberate crashes per architecture, at a `video=` screen mode: report on screen and serial (below) |

Crash tests (`crash=KIND` on the kernel command line):

| | x86-64 | AArch64 |
|---|---|---|
| CPU-specific | `ud` invalid opcode, `de` divide error, `gp` bad segment selector, `df` double fault on the IST1 stack | `ud` undefined instruction, `align` misaligned load with strict alignment checking, `pc` misaligned branch target, `sp` misaligned stack pointer |
| memory protection | `pf` unmapped write, `null` NULL read, `wp` write to read-only, `nx` execute data, `stack` overflow into the guard page | the same five, as data/instruction aborts; the overflow is handled on the separate SP_EL1 exception stack |
| kernel checks | `doublefree`, `heapdouble`, `heapoverflow`, `spinlock` (taken twice), `locksleep` (a mutex under a spinlock), `lockorder` (A then B, then B then A) | the same |

## Toolchain

| Tool | Version | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Zig | 0.17.0 | bundled clang 22 and LLD: cross-compiles C for x86-64 (UEFI PE32+ and ELF) |
| QEMU | 11.1.2 | `qemu-system-x86_64` (TCG emulation) and `qemu-system-aarch64` (hardware virtualization via `hvf`), built from source |
| edk2 | from QEMU | UEFI firmware for x86-64 (OVMF) and AArch64 |
| glib, pixman | 2.90.1, 0.46.4 | QEMU dependencies, linked statically |
| libslirp | 4.8.0 | QEMU's "user" network (DHCP, DNS, NAT without root rights), linked statically |

The Python venv in `tools/venv` provides meson, ninja, pkgconf and tomli.

## Roadmap

Done: the UEFI bootloader and the higher-half kernel (steps 1-8), the ARM
port alongside x86 (A0-A3b), interrupts and the serial terminal (9a-9e),
USB with keyboards, hubs and sticks (10a-12b), programs in user mode with
processes and threads, a preemptive scheduler and the shell `sh`
(11a-11e), booting from a USB stick and on a real PC (12c), speed
(13a-13c: console, RitterFS cache, batched screen updates, one xHCI
interrupt per event, the full screen width), the PS/2 keyboard (14), SATA
disks (15), USB hot plug (16), RitterFS polish (17), roadmap items 2,
3, 4 and 6 below (18a-21), the text editor `edit` with the terminal
it needs (22a-22b), `/dev` with `disks` (23a), volumes (23b) and partition
editing (23c), `format` (23d), reading FAT (24a), several USB controllers
with a wait for a late boot stick (25), `clear` (26), writing FAT (24b),
GPTs fitted to bigger disks (27), `format -t fat` (24c), `ls /` showing
`/boot`, `/dev` and `/volumes` (28), typing beyond ASCII (29), Ctrl-L (30)
the EFI partition's label `EFI` (31), `/system/config` (32), and the network:
a driver for the e1000 card (33), IPv4 and `ping` (34), UDP and DHCP
(35), DNS (36), sockets (37), TCP (38a), losses and flow control (38b),
`fetch` (39), the cryptography for SSH (40a), pseudo-terminals (40b),
`sshd` (40c), `ssh` (40d), the loopback interface `lo` (40e),
routing between cards (41), drivers for the Intel 82576 (42a) and
Realtek's RTL8168 and RTL8125 (42b) with the fixes from the user's PC
(42c-42f, 44f, 44g, 44j), the tests' halves side by side (43a), elves
(43b) with DHCP and DNS among them (44a-44d, 44h, 44i), and polish from
the PC tests: no wait for links at the boot (44e), the prompt never lost
above a message (44e, 44j), `;` and `*` in the shell (44k), its line
editor and history (44l); the firewall (45a, 45d-45f) and the logs
(45b-45c); exFAT (46a-46c); fixes for the laptop's Realtek (47a-47b);
and `osinstall` (48a), the stick always the system when started from it
(48b), a firmware boot entry for the installed disk (48c), and a security audit (49a). The history below lists every step.

The roadmap, in the order chosen:

| # | Item | State |
|---|------|-------|
| 1 | USB 3 hubs and transaction translators, tested on hardware | done: the user's laptop, after step 64g (a Realtek USB 3.2 hub: SuperSpeed stick and full-speed mouse through its TT) |
| 2 | RitterFS features: snapshots and file history, instant copies, transactions, searchable attributes | done: steps 18a-18g |
| 3 | file system management from the shell: programs to create and delete folders and files, copy, move and rename (`mkdir`, `rmdir`, `rm`, `cp`, `mv`, ...), with the system calls for writing they need; programs for the shell only, not commands of the kernel's rescue console | done: steps 19a-19b |
| 4 | users, login and home folders: owners and permissions in RitterFS (an on-disk format change), a user for every process with permission checks in the system calls, a `login` program (passwords not shown while typed, stored only as salted hashes), `/home/<name>` for each user | done: steps 20a-20c |
| 5 | the PS/2 keyboard tested on a laptop | done: the user's AMD laptop (built-in keyboard on the i8042), after step 25 |
| 6 | self-tests only on request (a `selftest` option set by `make test`) | done: step 21 |
| 7 | disks and volumes: `/dev` and the program `disks` (23a); other volumes mounted at `/volumes/<label>` (`-2`, `-3` when labels repeat) automatically, plus `mount` and `unmount` (23b); partition editing in `disks` (23c); `format` makes a RitterFS (23d) | done: steps 23a-23d |
| 8 | FAT (sticks from a shop, the EFI system partition): 24a reading, 24b writing, 24c `format` makes FAT32 | done: steps 24a-24c |

The user's choices after 45f: like FAT, 46a reading, 46b writing, 46c `format -t exfat`; the full Unicode up-case table for `format` (made at build time from Python's Unicode data, under the Unicode license: agreed); everyone may read and write (root's, 0777/0666, as FAT). 46a reading, 46b writing, 46c `format -t exfat` (done) | done |

35 UDP and DHCP (`address = dhcp`, done); 36 DNS (`ping example.com`, done); 37 sockets for programs (UDP first, done); 38a TCP connections (done), 38b TCP retransmission and flow control (done); 39 `fetch URL` (HTTP, done); QEMU has no such chip: tested on our own model, done); Wi-Fi (the laptop's MediaTek MT7921) not planned: it needs MediaTek's firmware; then, from the user's tries: 42e the Intel 82576's receiving (its queues off while their rings are set up), 42f receive interrupts (MSI) for the Intel and Realtek cards, 44f the 82576's receive tail after RCTL.EN, 44g its interrupt routed (IVAR), 44j MSI-X for it (done); 47a the RTL8168G/H's start settings (FIFO sizes and others through ERI: a laptop's RTL8168H received but sent nothing) | done on the J1900 after 44j: the Intel 82576 (eth0) with DHCP, DNS, HTTP and SSH from the Mac, pings of 0.3 ms with MSI-X; the board's RTL8168G received in the first try (not tried again since); the laptop's Killer E2600 (an RTL8168H) works since 47b (the user, after 48b) |
| 11 | elves (what other systems call services or daemons): 43a the tests' two halves side by side, 43b `elf` and `elvesd` (`/system/config/elves/`, one file per elf: started at the boot, started again when they end); 44 DHCP and DNS as elves, out of the kernel: 44a sockets bound to a card and the link state for programs, 44b the elf `dhcpd`, 44c the elf `dnsd`, 44d `dhcpd` renamed `dhclient` (`dhcpd` is a server's name on Unix); 44h `sshd` an elf from the start; 44i `dnsd` answers every machine (every elf that listens takes everyone: access is the firewall's, item 12) (all done) | done on the J1900 after 44j: `dhclient`, `dnsd` (also asked from the Mac), `sshd` (logged into from the Mac) |
| – | polish from the user's PC tests (after 44c): 44e no wait for links at the boot, with link lines ("eth0: link up, 1000 Mbit/s"); 44e and 44j the prompt and the typed line never lost above a message (the kernel's, an elf's, a background job's); 44k `;` between commands and `*` for names in the shell; 44l the shell's line editor: Up/Down history (in memory), Left/Right, Home/End, Delete (all done) | done on the J1900 after 44l: the prompt after the elves' lines, `;`, `*`, history and editing (over SSH) |
| 12 | security, the user's after 44h: a firewall with its rules, the one place that decides what may reach the machine (which cards, addresses and ports, for example port 22 only from the local network; limits on new connections); the elves do not manage their own security (only what a firewall cannot see, like password or key logins inside SSH, stays with `sshd`). The packets are checked in the kernel; the elf `guardd` loads the rules (at the boot, and again when they change) and reports what was stopped; the command `guard` shows and changes them (names chosen by the user after 44l). Agreed with the user: the rules in one file, `/system/config/guard`, read top to bottom, the first rule that matches decides; `in = drop`/`allow` and `out = ...` the defaults; rules in words, `allow`/`drop`/`limit N/minute` `in`/`out` `[tcp|udp|icmp] [PORT or PORT-PORT] [from ADDR[/N]] [to ADDR[/N]] [on CARD]`; replies to the machine's own connections always pass, `lo` is never filtered; a new system drops what comes in and allows what goes out, and guardd's first start writes rules for the elves already listening (sshd 22, dnsd 53, ping); `guard` lists the rules with counters and the last 20 dropped packets, `guard add RULE`, `insert N RULE`, `remove N`, `in drop|allow`, `out drop|allow` change the file (guardd loads it within 2 s); no console lines for dropped packets; until guardd has loaded the rules at the boot the kernel drops what comes in, and with guardd switched off it lets everything in again (no lockout). Steps: 45a the kernel's rule table, checks and counters (done); then the logs (item 13, the user's wish after 45a, done first: 45b-45c); 45d `guardd` and the file (done); 45e `guard` (done); 45f `limit` (done) | done (45a, 45d-45f); the forward chain (49h) and masquerade/source NAT (49i) added later |
| 13 | logs, the user's after 45a: `/system/logs/NAME.log` (like `/system/config`), written by the elf `logd` (the user's name), at most 64 KiB each (the full one becomes `NAME.log.old`); logged: every elf's output (also still on the console), the kernel's messages (`kernel.log`), logins and logouts (`login.log`); a write at every boot, which the user accepted. 45b the kernel's side: lines kept in memory with their time, one ring per log, the system call `log` (done); 45c `logd` and the logins | done (45b-45c) |
| 14 | installing on an internal disk, the user's after 46c (instead of growing a RitterFS for now): the program `osinstall` on the stick asks its questions one by one (which disk: every disk but the one booted from; mounted volumes on it unmounted; the disk's name typed to confirm), then makes a new GPT on the whole disk (an EFI system partition of 64 MiB, FAT32 `EFI`, and a RitterFS `RitterOS` on the rest, both named as on the stick, with new random GUIDs), copies the bootloader for this machine's architecture to the fallback path (`\EFI\BOOT\BOOTX64.EFI`), `cmdline.txt` without the stick's test options, and `/system/<arch>` without its test programs; no users or homes (the installed system asks for root's password on its first start). Whole disks only (the user's choice); the fallback path only at first, a firmware boot entry since 48c (the user's, after the J1900 did not start the disk). 48a `osinstall`; later: upgrading an installed system from a newer stick. Targets: the J1900's SATA SSD, the laptop's M.2 NVMe drive | done (48a); on the laptop (the user, after 48b): installed from the 48b stick onto its NVMe drive, started from it without the stick, both partitions at the right sizes; on the J1900 (the user, after 48c): installed onto its SATA SSD, started by the firmware through osinstall's boot entry |
| 24 | Wi-Fi (the user's, after TLS): the wireless link apart from the network. A driver presents the wireless card as a regular network card (`wlan0`), so `net up`, DHCP, routing, the firewall and DNS stay as they are; a separate program (an elf) owns the wireless link -- scanning, joining a network, WPA2/WPA3 keys, reconnecting -- and only brings `wlan0`'s link up and down, never caring about addresses. The user's choices (after 23e): the laptop's MediaTek MT7921 (14c3:7961) first; its firmware's licence is acceptable (MediaTek's: redistributable, binary only), installed in `/system/firmware/` with its source and licence noted beside it, like the root certificates; WPA2-Personal and WPA3-Personal (SAE, with protected management frames) from the start; names: `wlan0`, the elf `wifid`, the command `wifi` (`scan`, `join SSID`, `forget SSID`, `status`), the wireless link's settings apart from TCP/IP (the user's, after 23e: `/system/config/network/wlan0` is a TCP/IP file like `eth0`'s, `address`, `gateway`, `dns`, nothing wireless): `/system/config/wifi/networks/NAME`, a file per saved network, root's only (`ssid`, `passphrase`, `security` = `open`/`wpa2`/`wpa3`, `auto`), and `/system/config/wifi/radio` for the radio itself (`country`, the allowed channels); `/system/firmware/`; the system calls for the elf's frames and keys named at their step. Station mode only (no access point, no monitor mode, no WPA-Enterprise, no 6 GHz). QEMU has no Wi-Fi card: a model of our own (as for the Realtek chips) that passes 802.11 frames to a test access point on the Mac (Python, like `testroute.py`); the cryptography against the standards' vectors; register facts from any source, the code our own (as for Realtek). Steps: 24a WPA's cryptography (SHA-1, HMAC-SHA1, PBKDF2-SHA1, the 802.11 PRF, AES key wrap, AES-CCM, AES-CMAC); 24b constant-time P-256 for SAE (hash-to-element, the commit and confirm values); 24c `wlan` cards in the kernel (the 802.11 data path, the elf's frames and keys); 24d the QEMU model and the test access point (open, WPA2, WPA3); 24e `wifid` and `wifi` (scan, join, the 4-way handshake, SAE, reconnecting); 24f the MT7921 driver on the laptop (over SSH, at the user's invitation): 24f-1 the chip found and owned, 24f-2 the firmware downloaded and running, both done on the laptop; 24f-3 (MAC init and scan: `wlan0`, `wifi scan` through the firmware) done; 24f-4 (the country, transmitting, joining, the data path) done on the laptop. | 24a-24e done; 24f-1 to 24f-13 and 24g-1 to 24g-8 done (the laptop joins WPA2 on 2.4 or 5 GHz with 802.11ac at 40 MHz and bursts, BIP, hidden networks, channel switches, roaming, chip restarts, power saving; carries IP over Wi-Fi at 1.5-2 MB/s with the chip doing the encryption, rejoins a dropped link, renews its keys in place, drops what it does not need) |
