| 22 | shared libraries (the user's, after the SDK, because TLS, HTTPS and hardware acceleration are coming: a fix or a faster backend in a library then reaches every program without rebuilding it). The design agreed with the user: standard ELF shared objects and a user-space dynamic loader (as Linux and the BSDs do), before TLS. Names (the user's): `mmap`/`munmap` (`<sys/mman.h>`, `SYS_MMAP` 66, `SYS_MUNMAP` 67), the loader `ld.so` at `/system/<arch>/lib/ld.so`, libraries in `/system/<arch>/lib` named `libNAME.so.MAJOR` (`libritteros.so.1`, later `libtls.so.1`), `ldd`, and the SDK's `LINK = shared` or `static`. Steps: 22a `mmap` (a file's pages shared read-only from one copy in memory, private copies, anonymous memory, which also lifts `malloc`'s 16 MiB limit); 22b `ld.so` (the kernel starts it for a program whose ELF file names it; it maps the libraries and relocates them); 22c `libritteros.so.1`, RitterOS's own programs linked to it; 22d the SDK links shared by default, `ldd`, and a test that an app keeps working when its library is replaced. Then TLS and HTTPS (item 23, as `libtls.so.1`). Fonts as files (`/system/fonts`, the user's) came with it, before 22d. Then, before TLS (the user's, after 22d: everything open about memory and the loader), in this order: 22e demand paging and copy-on-write (pages filled when first touched; private writable mappings share the page cache's pages until written; `SIGBUS`, signal 7, past a file's end), 22f `mprotect` (`SYS_MPROTECT` 68) and read-only relocated data in `ld.so` (RELRO), 22g programs' own code from the page cache and a page cache that keeps recently used files, 22h `malloc` safe from several threads and giving memory back, 22i per-thread data ("thread-local storage": `__thread` in programs and libraries, `__tls_get_addr`, `errno` per thread), 22j writable shared file mappings and `msync` (`SYS_MSYNC` 69), 22k `dlopen`, `dlsym`, `dlclose`, `dlerror` (`<dlfcn.h>`), 22l full address-space randomization (programs position-independent and placed anywhere, `ld.so` relocating itself, libraries, mmap and stacks at random places). Names agreed with the user (the thread-pointer system call's at 22i). | 22a-22l done (item 22 complete) |

This is the groundwork for `owl`'s file operations (New folder, Rename, Delete, Copy) next. Sub-steps: 19a the menu bar, 19b context menus, 19c owl's menus | done (19a-19c); file operations next |
| 18 | GUI polish (the user's, after using the browser): `owl` is windowd's default app, so starting the GUI (`windowd`) opens the file browser (`startup = owl` unless /system/config/windows says otherwise); from it you open terminals and other apps. Sub-steps: 18a owl the default; 18b minimize; maximize added later (README history "21 (max)"). | done |

| 15 | the window system, the user's after step 50 (to make RitterOS usable by a regular user and able to run richer apps): a window server so every app draws the same windows from the same resources. The design agreed with the user: apps draw their own windows into shared buffers and the server only composites them to the screen and routes input (like Wayland, not X11's server-side drawing); the server is a user program (the kernel stays minimal), run as an elf. The GUI starts on demand (the user's choice): not at boot, started with `windowd &` or `elf start windowd`; the console stays until windowd takes the screen and comes back when it releases. On its own windowd is only a compositor (no launcher), so it launches a session program when it starts — the file browser `owl` by default since step 18a (`startup` in `/system/config/windows`) — giving a user on real hardware, with no serial console, a way to open terminals and apps; closing that app leaves the GUI, and Ctrl-Alt-Delete leaves it even if it hangs (the user's, step 15h). Names (the user's): the server `windowd` (config `/system/config/windows`), the shared toolkit library `libwindow` (`window.h`), the pointer device `mouse0`, local IPC as Unix-domain sockets (`AF_LOCAL`), a shared-buffer system call `SYS_SHARE`, a framebuffer-lease system call `SYS_DISPLAY`, and a terminal-emulator app `term`. Steps, bottom-up: 15a the mouse (a USB HID mouse and the PS/2 mouse, `mouse0`, `mouse1`, ... in the order found; pointer events into the kernel, shown by the rescue console's `mouse` command); 15b local sockets; 15c shared buffers; 15d the framebuffer lease; 15e `windowd` (compositing client windows with decorations, the pointer, and input routing through `SYS_DISPLAY`'s `DISPLAY_EVENT`); 15e2 interaction (focus, raising and moving windows with the mouse); 15f `libwindow` (windows, drawing, the console's fonts reused in user space, an event loop, basic widgets); 15g the first apps (`term` running the shell in a window; `edit` in a window later); 15h windowd launches a session program so the GUI is usable without a serial console, plus a Ctrl-Alt-Delete escape; 15i a close box on each window and a dirty-rectangle flush (only the changed area reaches the screen, so two windows redrawing at once no longer lag); 15j resizing windows from any edge or corner (term reflows its rows and columns and tells the pty); 15k a USB tablet (absolute-pointer) driver, so the mouse works in UTM/QEMU without capturing it (the user's, after the pointer did nothing in UTM) | done (15a-15k); a graphical launcher, `edit` in a window and more apps later |

| 31 | several processor cores (the user's, 2026-10-28, after 64t: "it has the potential to improve performance"; started at once). The kernel runs on one core: its spinlocks only switch interrupts off, the scheduler has one `current` and one run queue, and nothing tells another core about changed page tables. Steps agreed: 65a the groundwork on one core (real, ticket-ordered spinlocks; a per-core block reached through GS / TPIDR_EL1 with the current thread, interrupt depth, the reschedule flag, the idle thread and the stacks; every place that relied on interrupts off as a lock given a real one; the same behaviour, and a full test passed, before any other core starts: the user's); 65b the other cores started (x86 INIT-SIPI-SIPI with a start-up routine below 1 MiB, ARM PSCI CPU_ON; the cores from ACPI's MADT, x2APIC entries included) and parked in their deepest sleep (on the laptop this may also raise the boost limit); 65c scheduling on all cores (one shared run queue under a lock first, a tick per core, a wake-up interrupt for an idle core, page-table changes announced: IPIs on x86, broadcast TLB invalidation on ARM; device interrupts on core 0); 65d spreading and measuring (`threads` shows each thread's core and the clock per core; MSI interrupts spread, the network card's first); 65e speed where measurements call for it (per-core run queues, work stealing, keeping a thread on its core). Tests: QEMU's `-smp` (ARM under HVF truly parallel), all test machines with 2 cores after 65c and some with 4, a debug lock checker (order, sleeping under a spinlock); the J1900 (4 cores) and the laptop (8 cores, 16 threads). Names agreed: `cpus=N` on the kernel command line (`cpus=1`: one core, as before), the idle threads `idle` (core 0), `idle1`, `idle2`, ..., a `cpu` column in `threads`, a rescue console command `cpus` listing the cores. | 32 | AVX and AVX2 for programs on x86 (the user's, 2026-10-28, after asking whether the multi-core work helps vector code: separate work, complementary for speed). Today programs have SSE (every x86-64 processor) and ARM's NEON, saved per program thread with FXSAVE (512 bytes) or V0-V31; AVX's 256-bit registers need the kernel to switch the feature on and save more. The plan, not started: switch it on where the processor has it (CPUID 1: XSAVE and AVX; CR4.OSXSAVE; XCR0 = x87, SSE and AVX, AVX-512's parts only on processors with them, later), save and restore each program thread's state with XSAVE/XRSTOR in an area as large as CPUID leaf 0xD says (about 832 bytes for AVX: ARCH_FPU_STATE_SIZE becomes the processor's), and carry it through fork, exec and signal handlers (the signal frame grows: check what programs see of it and the ABI rules, sdk/abi); programs ask the processor themselves (CPUID's OSXSAVE and AVX bits, XGETBV), so no system call is expected. Machines: the laptop's Ryzen 7 5800H has AVX and AVX2 (no AVX-512); the J1900 (Silvermont) has none, so a program must check and fall back to SSE there; QEMU's emulation has AVX2 with a newer processor model (`-cpu max`), which a test machine would use. Our own as has no AVX (VEX) encodings yet: programs built with zig clang can use AVX first, as and cc later if wanted. ARM's wider vectors (SVE) would be an item of their own, for hardware that has them. Names agreed (2026-11-01): the test program `avxtest`, its test `avx_shell_test`; the boot line only shows the vector features (no `cpus` line), no helper header (programs use CPUID and XGETBV) | 32a, 32b done (the kernel saves AVX with XSAVE where the processor has it, FXSAVE elsewhere; on the laptop AVX2 adds 1.86x as fast as SSE2 in the cache, 1.15x from memory; a switch 51 ns dearer; the SDK guide's note). Next, the user's (2026-11-01: "AVX instructions in the cc and as. | 35 | swap (the user's, 2026-11-02, after asking whether RitterOS is a modern operating system: "Let's work on swap memory"). Today a program that touches a page when none is free gets SIGSEGV; every page of a program goes through `kernel/mmap.c`. Both machines' disks are all ESP and RitterFS (the laptop 32 GiB of memory, the J1900 8), and RitterFS never writes a block in place, so the user's choices: the swap area is a file in RitterFS, `/system/swap`, a *fixed* one (inode flag bit 1 `fixed`: its blocks allocated once and written in place, no copy-on-write, no checksums, never in snapshots or instant copies; a compatible addition to the format); its pages encrypted from the start (AES, a random key made at each boot and kept only in memory); off until the user asks: `swap make SIZE` / `swap on` write `on = yes` to `/system/config/swap`, read after the root mount by later boots. Steps: 35a the swap file (RitterFS's fixed files in the kernel, `ritterfs.py`, the check and docs/ritterfs.md; the program `swap` with `swap`, `swap make SIZE`, `swap on`, `swap off`; `SYS_SWAP`; pages written and read encrypted, a self-test's round trip; a `swap:` line in `mem`), 35b paging out (the kernel thread `pageout` when free memory falls below a mark, pages not used lately by the accessed bit (x86) or the access flag (ARM), where each page went kept in its page-table entry, a fault reads it back; a program's own private pages; an allocation without a free page waits for `pageout`; the test program `swaptest`, the test `swap_shell_test`, the boot option `memory=N` to use only N MiB), 35c pages forked processes share and shared anonymous memory, 35d measured on the laptop and the J1900. Names agreed (2026-11-02): `swap`, `/system/swap`, `/system/config/swap` (`on`), `SYS_SWAP`, `pageout`, `fixed`, `swaptest`, `swap_shell_test`, `memory=` (Linux's names mkswap/swapon/swapoff, kswapd, mem=; Solaris has a `swap` for the same job). Not planned: dropping clean file pages (mapped files are read whole at mmap), compressed memory | 35a done (the swap file `/system/swap`, fixed files in RitterFS, `swap`, `SYS_SWAP`, pages written and read with XTS-AES-256), 35b done (paging out: `pageout`, faults reading pages back, waiting for memory, `swap off` reading back, `memory=N`, `swaptest`). 35c done (pages forked processes share: one slot for every holder, fork copies swap entries), 35c-2 done (shared anonymous memory: an object holds its pages, taken out of every process at once). 35d done (measured on the machines: the J1900 9 GiB twice at ~2.5 MB/s, the laptop 34 GiB at 12.7 MB/s; the laptop's power-offs under heavy disk work left open as a hardware question). 35e done (an unused swap file costs nothing: programs' pages from the top of memory through each core, 32 at a time; program starts take a page for their arguments first). Item 35 done |

| 39 | the speed of `cc`'s code (the user's, 2026-11-07, after the report `2026-11-07-cc-speed.md` from SDRfi, a radio modem for the PlutoSDR built on the J1900: its receiver runs 8.7 times slower built by `cc` than by clang, the kernels 2 to 9 times; checked against cc's sources and output). In this order: 1 floats and doubles in registers on x86 (today only vectors get xmm8-xmm15, `x86_64.c`'s register classes; every scalar float lives in a stack slot and is loaded through a general register; ARM already keeps them in its 8 FP registers), and floats loaded straight into xmm registers; 2 inlining small `static inline` functions with struct parameters or results (`inlinable()` refuses every struct: a two-line complex multiply is a call, its values copied through stack slots around it), small float structs kept in registers; 3 induction variables and loop-invariant address arithmetic hoisted (`x[k - i]` recomputed in full each pass, the index sign-extended once per array); 4 later: unrolling, and SSE2 packed operations for simple loops. Parts 1 (39a), 2 (39b, 39b-2) and 3 (39c, 39c-2) done; 4 (agreed 2026-11-08: 39d a loop's test at its bottom, 39e unrolling, 39f-1 and 39f-2 SSE2 packed operations on x86) done. | 40 | cc's remaining gap to clang (the user's, 2026-11-09, after item 39 left cc's code 1 to 1.9 times clang's on the J1900), in this order: 40a an instruction's result may take the register of an operand whose life ends there (`movaps a, x; mulss b, x` without the copy; both architectures, int and float); 40b x86: a load used once, by the operation right after it, becomes that operation's memory operand (`mulss 0(%r8,%rbx,4), %xmm9`); 40c loads and stores marked volatile in the IR, then a load repeated in a block with no store or call between reuses the first (a FIR filter's h[i] read twice); never a volatile one; 40d floats and vectors in xmm2-xmm7 too where no call lies in their lives (14 registers instead of 8; a call's arguments set up without overwriting one another); 40e the loop passes' leftovers: unrolled copies with i + 1, i + 2 as fixed offsets instead of i stepped and extended again, and the packing passes taking indexes like n - 1 - i. Packed loops on ARM need vector registers in cc first: not in this item. No new names (the IR's volatile mark only). 40a-40e done; item 40 done (2026-11-09). |

Growing a RitterFS into free space after it: not yet. After step 32

the laptop is done (step 50: CDC-NCM); first used on real hardware in 50d (the user's PlutoSDR on the laptop).

Items 2, 3, 4 and 6 were done in one night without the chance to ask.
These decisions were taken as defaults then and confirmed by the user
after step 28:

- item 3: RitterOS writes to the disk only when the user asks (a program
  like `mkdir`, `>` in the shell, `snapshot create`); normal boots still
  never write
- item 4: the administrator is `root`, who chooses a password on the first
  start of a new disk (home `/home/root`); passwords are hashed with
  PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 100,000 iterations, our own code; `passwd` is for
  root only so far; the rescue console (Ctrl-D at `login:`) asks for no
  password
- item 6: a normal boot keeps the RitterFS check and skips the large
  self-tests
- names: `versions` (a file's history), `useradd`, `/system/users`,
  `/system/passwords` (moved to `/system/config/` in step 32, as the user
  asked)

Later, not ordered yet: real ARM hardware (the closest standard machine
is a Raspberry Pi 4 with the community UEFI firmware, worproject/rpi4
EDK2). Apple Silicon Macs are not realistic (no UEFI or ACPI, Apple's own
interrupt controller, NVMe, USB and IOMMU; Asahi's m1n1/U-Boot only for
M1/M2).

Raspberry Pi 4 UEFI settings (Device Manager -> Raspberry Pi
Configuration -> Advanced Configuration):
- **System Table Selection: ACPI** (not Devicetree or ACPI + Devicetree).
  RitterOS discovers all its hardware through ACPI tables and has no
  devicetree parser: MADT (the GIC), GTDT (the timer), SPCR (the PL011
  UART), MCFG/PCIe (the ECAM window and the VL805 xHCI). ACPI-only makes
  the Pi look like the standard ACPI/UEFI machine the ARM port expects
  (as the J1900 and QEMU do).
- **Limit RAM to 3 GB: off** is fine (devices then DMA above 3 GB, which
  current firmware and boards handle).

Known gap before it boots (not a firmware setting): the Pi 4's ACPI
describes a **GIC-400, which is GICv2**, while the ARM port today assumes
the **GICv3** of QEMU's `virt` machine (the `ICC_*` system-register CPU
interface). A stock build gets through the bootloader and early paging
but has no interrupts until a GICv2 driver (distributor + CPU-interface
MMIO, redistributor-less) is added. Other Pi 4 specifics to expect:
GIC-400, PL011, and the VL805 xHCI on PCIe.

| 2a, 2b | UEFI memory map; GOP framebuffer and a test picture |
| 3 | bootloader loads the ELF kernel and exits boot services |
| (disk) | GPT disk image on an emulated NVMe SSD |

| 6a-6e | own page tables, page allocator, boot info v2, higher-half kernel, kernel heap |
| 7a-7c | PCI scan, NVMe reading, NVMe writing and partitions |
| 8a-8d | RitterFS: format and mount, reading, copy-on-write writing with crash torture, scrub and repair |
| A0-A3b | ARM port: QEMU `virt` under HVF, architecture split, ARM bootloader, ARM kernel entry and exceptions, ARM translation tables; the whole kernel runs and is tested on ARM |
| 9a | interrupts: Local APIC / GICv3, 100 Hz timer tick, sleeping waits, uptime |
| 9b | device interrupts: PCI MSI-X (Local APIC / GICv2m), interrupt-driven NVMe, MMIO accessors |
| 9c | serial terminal: receive interrupts (IO APIC / GIC SPI), line editing, first commands |
| 9d | terminal commands: RitterFS `ls`/`cat`, `pci`, `poweroff`/`reboot` (ACPI on x86, PSCI on ARM) |
| 9e | the kernel boots from RitterFS: RitterFS reading shared by bootloader and kernel (`common/rfs_read.c`), UEFI Partition Info and Disk I/O, boot info v4, host-side copy-on-write install of `/system` |
| 10a | USB: xHCI controller with command and event rings, MSI-X interrupt, port list |
| 10b | USB enumeration: port reset, Enable Slot, Address Device, control transfers, descriptors and strings, `usb` command |
| 10c | USB keyboard: Configure Endpoint, interrupt transfers, HID boot reports to characters, key repeat; the event ring handled in the interrupt handler |
| 11a | programs in user mode: own address spaces, ring 3 / EL0, system calls `write` and `exit`, faults stop only the program, `run` |
| 11b | programs read: `read`/`open`/`close`, the terminal as standard input, arguments, Ctrl-C, a C library with `printf`; `cat` and `echo` move out of the kernel |
| 11c1 | threads: context switch, round-robin scheduler with 20 ms slices, preemptible kernel, spinlocks and mutexes, wait queues for input, disk, USB and timers, `threads` |
| 11c2 | processes on threads: `spawn`, `wait` with Unix statuses, `kill`, `getpid`, `sleep`, zombies and orphans, signals for faults, process groups and Ctrl-C to the foreground job, `ps` |

| 11d | the shell `sh` as a program: jobs with their own process groups and the terminal, background jobs, `cd`/`pwd`, quotes; `stat`, `readdir`, current directories, `ls`, `uptime`, `mem`, `poweroff`, `reboot` as programs; the kernel terminal becomes the rescue console |
| (11d) | `help` in the shell lists every program with its own description (`PROGRAM_HELP`); `ps`, `threads`, `pci`, `usb` as programs (kernel reports); the boot's test picture removed |
| 12a | USB hubs: hub descriptor, port power and reset, devices behind hubs (route string, root port, TT), USB 3 hub depth; QEMU machines with two hubs in a chain and the keyboard behind them |
| 12b | USB mass storage: bulk endpoints (chained TRBs, short packets, stall recovery), bulk-only transport with SCSI commands, sticks as block devices `sda`..., Linux-style partition names, `usbdisktest` |
| 12c | booting from USB: the bootloader passes the partition's GPT GUID (boot info v5), the kernel takes the disk that has it (NVMe or USB); several NVMe controllers; a test machine without NVMe boots from a stick |
| (12c) | first boot on a real PC (AMI firmware, UEFI 2.3): the bootloader no longer needs the Partition Info protocol (UEFI 2.7+); it reads the boot disk's GPT itself |
| (12c) | second and third real boots: the screen went dark after "Starting kernel..."; the kernel's console now appears from its first line, both kernels and the bootloader map the framebuffer wherever it is (write-combining on x86), and progress marks 1-5 show which stage was reached |
| (12c) | fourth real boot: only mark 1, so the boot stopped between the display switch and ExitBootServices' return; serial output now detects a missing or stuck UART instead of waiting forever (the kernel's serial input looped forever without one), ExitBootServices gets 16 attempts with a dot each, the PIT wait is bounded (fallback delay through port 0x80); new test `noserial` |
| (12c) | the same boot left running: mark 2 after minutes. The PC has a COM1 UART whose baud rate the firmware never set (~5 s per character); RitterOS now programs the UART itself (115200 baud, 8N1, FIFOs) |
| (12c) | fifth real boot: marks 1 and 2 quickly, one ExitBootServices attempt, then a hang in `SetVirtualAddressMap`. The bootloader now calls it with the kernel's first page tables active (physical and direct-map addresses both mapped, as Linux does); `noruntime` skips it |
| (12c) | sixth real boot (Celeron J1900, AMI P2.20): the kernel's console appears and the log runs through ACPI and SMBIOS; then a page fault inside the firmware's `GetTime`, which read a chipset register at its physical address 0xFED08004. Firmware calls now run in their own address space with physical memory also mapped at its own address (x86) |
| (12c) | seventh real boot: the whole boot runs (UEFI time read, timer 5209 kHz, 16 PCI devices) and ends in the rescue console: the firmware had left the USB sockets on the EHCI controller, so xHCI saw no stick and no keyboard. EHCI controllers are now taken from the firmware and stopped (`kernel/ehci.c`); on Intel chipsets the sockets move to xHCI (XUSB2PR/USB3_PSSEN); ports with port power control are switched on |
| (12c) | eighth real boot: the USB keyboard (Dell KB216) and the stick behind the board's hub found, the system partition `sda2` mounted, and after many minutes the shell prompt `ritteros:/$`: the J1900's xHCI has no MSI-X, so every USB transfer waited for the next 10 ms tick. xHCI now uses MSI when there is no MSI-X, and without either polls the event ring while waiting |
| (12c) | ninth real boot: MSI on, but most interrupts lost (`No-Op command: completed, 0 interrupt(s)`), reads from the stick timed out, no system partition, uptime 210 s. Cause: when waiting code handled an event before its interrupt arrived, the interrupt handler found nothing and did not clear "event handler busy", which stops all further interrupts. The handler now always acknowledges; the tick checks the event ring as a safety net and counts what it finds first (`events found by the tick instead`) |
| 13a | console scrolling in a copy of the screen in RAM (pixels move in RAM, the text area is copied to the screen) instead of redrawing every character: x86 in QEMU 2,662 → 1,101 ms of drawing, boot 3.87 → 2.40 s; ARM 91 → 41 ms. `memcpy`/`memmove`/`memset` move 8 bytes at a time; `arch_counter` (TSC / CNTVCT) for measurements |
| 13b | RitterFS block cache (8 MiB, least recently used; the check and scrub still read the disk), each cached block's checksum verified once, file reads keep their lowest index block instead of walking the tree per block (also in the bootloader). Boot from a USB stick in QEMU x86: 8,257 → 1,107 disk reads (each block once), tree and files 580 → 319 ms, boot 2.18 → 1.87 s; the log reports phase times and cache counters |
| 14 | PS/2 keyboard on the i8042 (x86): controller and keyboard self-tests with bounded waits, IRQ 1 through the IO APIC, scan code set 1 (translated) to HID usage IDs; `kernel/keyboard.c` holds what USB and PS/2 keyboards share (names kbd0, kbd1, ..., US layout, modifiers, Caps Lock, repeat; a key already down is ignored, so the PS/2 keyboard's own repeat does not count) |
| 13c | console: screen updates batched (at most every 20 ms once the scheduler runs; the thread `console` copies what is left; crashes copy at once) and the text rows of the RAM copy as a ring (scrolling clears one row instead of moving 4-7 MB). QEMU x86: drawing 1,101 → 323 ms with 13 screen updates for 202 lines, boot 1.87 → 1.37 s; ARM 41 → 15 ms. On the J1900 scrolling took 1.1 s before |
| (13c) | xHCI clears "event handler busy" only when it is set (the J1900 got a second, empty interrupt per event: 14,158 for 7,080); the console uses the whole screen width (cells sized for 4K screens, rows as long as the screen: 175 instead of 160 columns at 1600x1200) |

| 11e | threads for programs: `thread_create`/`thread_exit`/`thread_join`/`thread_self` (`<thread.h>`), up to 16 per program, each with its own stack (64 KiB and a guard page) and FP/SIMD registers; the library's `__thread_start` calls the function; `exit`, faults and signals end all threads (the others leave first, then the memory goes); the last `thread_exit` ends the program |
| 17 | RitterFS polish: new objects take object IDs freed by earlier commits, new directory entries a freed place that is large enough; after each commit the blocks it freed are reported to the disk (TRIM: NVMe Dataset Management / Deallocate, ATA DATA SET MANAGEMENT; the block device's optional `discard`, passed on by partitions); directories larger than one block get an index in RAM (name -> object, built on the first lookup, dropped on changes): the boot's file test x86 319 -> 81 ms. No format change |
| 18a | RitterFS snapshots (on-disk version 2, `docs/ritterfs.md`): a snapshot keeps a committed state's object table inode in an internal snapshot table; blocks born up to the newest snapshot's generation (`pinned_generation`) are not freed when the live state drops them, deleting a snapshot sweeps the blocks nothing reaches any more; snapshots are read through read-only views; the kernel's and the host's check walk every snapshot (a block may be shared between states, never used twice in one) and report the blocks kept only for snapshots; the host's copy-on-write install holds frees back the same way; version 1 is written as long as there are no snapshots |
| 18b | snapshots from the shell: system call `snapshot` (create, delete, list) and the program `snapshot`; `/.snapshots` is a directory that is not on the disk: it lists the snapshots, and `/.snapshots/NAME/...` is a path in that snapshot's read-only view (open, stat, readdir, chdir and spawn; each open file remembers its file system); the shell's `help` puts a long usage's description on the next line |
| 18c | RitterFS instant copies (`ritterfs_copy`): a file's copy is a new inode with the source's tree, a directory's copy has new entries copied the same way; the copy pins everything born so far, so neither side frees a shared block; a sweep recomputes the pin (newest snapshot, or newest shared block; 0 when nothing is shared, so frees are exact again) and runs by itself after enough held-back frees; a failed copy discards its uncommitted changes; both checks allow blocks used twice in one state only when the pin covers them, and report them (`copies: N block(s) shared`) |
| 18d | file history: the program `versions PATH` lists a file's versions in the snapshots (oldest first) and now, with sizes, comparing each with the one before by content ("first version", "changed", "unchanged", "(not there)"); it uses only `/.snapshots` and the snapshot list |
| 18e | RitterFS attributes (on-disk version 3): key=value pairs on files and directories in one attribute table (records like directory entries, multiples of 16 bytes, never crossing a block, free ones reused); set, get, remove, iterate in the kernel; removing a file drops them, instant copies keep them, snapshots see them as they were; both checks verify the records (well-formed, existing objects, no key twice); the host's `install()` can replace an internal object's content (for tests and repairs) |
| 18f | attributes and search from the shell: system call `attr` (get, set, remove, list; changes committed at once; read-only in snapshots; error `ENODATA` "No such attribute"), the programs `attr` and `find` (name patterns with `*` and `?`, attribute conditions, any number of them) |
| 18g | RitterFS transactions in the kernel: every change is part of the open transaction until a commit makes it durable at once (copy-on-write, superblock last); `ritterfs_abort` throws the open transaction away (the file system keeps the last commit's superblock); a failed instant copy uses it too |
| 19a | file system management from the shell: system calls `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `unlink`, `rename` and `copy` (each one commit, all or nothing; read-only in `/.snapshots`; errors `ENOTEMPTY`, `ENOSPC`), the kernel's `ritterfs_rename` (only directory entries change) and copies from a snapshot's view (restoring shares the old blocks); programs `mkdir [-p]`, `rmdir`, `rm [-r]`, `mv` and `cp [-r]` (instant copies) |
| 19b | writing files: `open` for writing (create, truncate, append; truncation sets the old tree aside, freed whole at the commit, so it never reappears in gaps), `write` to files (an inode refreshed before each read and write: another descriptor may have changed the file), a commit at close, at a program's exit and every 8 MiB; children inherit standard input, output and error, `spawn` can choose them, and `SPAWN_FOREGROUND` gives a job the terminal before it starts (a read in the background would end at once); the shell's `<`, `>`, `>>`; the program `touch` |
| 20a | permissions: owner, group and mode in the inode (formerly reserved; all zero = root, 0755), a user and a group for every process (inherited; the kernel starts programs as root), checks in the system calls (read, write, execute; x on every directory on the way; creating and removing need w and x on the directory; chmod by the owner, chown, snapshots and power only by root), new files belong to their creator (files 0644, directories 0755), copies to the copier; system calls `getuid`, `getgid`, `setuid`, `setgid`, `chmod`, `chown`; error `EACCES`; programs `chmod`, `chown`, `id`, `ls -l` |
| 20b | users and passwords: `/system/users` (name:uid:gid:home, everyone may read it; root needs no line) and `/system/passwords` (root only: name, iterations, salt, hash), `<pwd.h>` (`getpwnam`, `getpwuid`); passwords stored as PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 with 100,000 iterations and a 16-byte random salt (SHA-256, HMAC and PBKDF2 in `user/lib/crypto.c`, our own code; 600 ms per hash in emulated x86); the kernel's random generator (ChaCha20 from the firmware's seed and the CPU counter, fast key erasure; system call `random`); the terminal's echo can be switched off (system call `echo`, the foreground job only, on again when the program ends); programs `useradd` (user ID from 1000, own group, `/home/NAME`) and `passwd` (root only so far), `id` with names |
| 20c | login: the kernel starts `login` instead of the shell; a fresh disk first asks for root's password (twice, not shown); `login:` and `Password:` (not shown; an unknown user takes as long as a wrong password), then the user's shell as that user, in the home directory, as the leader of a session of its own (`SPAWN_AS`, `SPAWN_NEW_SESSION`); `exit` logs out and login asks again; Ctrl-D at `login:` gives the rescue console (no password); the shell's `cd` alone goes home; every test session logs in as root first |

| 22a | the terminal for full-screen programs: system call `terminal` (raw input: bytes as typed, at least one, no echo, the cursor keys as `ESC [ A` ...; the text area's size: x86 test screen 48x139, ARM 35x86); the console understands VT100 cursor positioning and moves, erase in line and display, SGR (bold, reverse, 16 colors) and cursor hiding; cells have a background color; the cursor is drawn; when a program that switched raw input on ends, the kernel resets input, style and cursor |
| 22b | `edit FILE`, a full-screen text editor: gap buffer (1 MiB), cursor keys, Home, End, Page Up/Down, Backspace, Delete, Enter, tabs, UTF-8; horizontal and vertical scrolling; a bar with name, state, line and column, a message line; Ctrl-S saves (adds a final line break), Ctrl-Q quits (twice with unsaved changes); only changed rows are redrawn |

| 23b | volumes: the mount table (`kernel/mounts.c`): RitterFS on other disks and sticks checked and mounted at `/volumes/<label>` at the boot and on hot plug, unmounted when pulled out; every path and change goes to the file system that holds it; `mount`, `unmount` (refused while in use); `cp` and `mv` copy byte by byte between volumes; `rm -r` and `mv` refuse a mounted volume; `disks` in the rescue console (one kernel report for both) |
| 23c | partition editing: `/dev` writable for root except where a mounted file system lies (a mounted partition cannot be opened for writing, its blocks on the disk are refused, `mount` waits until no program writes the disk); closing a written disk flushes it and reads its table again (unchanged partitions keep entries and mounts, new RitterFS partitions are mounted, a mounted one gone from the table stays until unmounted); the GPT's copy at the disk's end is read when the first is damaged; `disks DISK` (blocks, free areas), `disks init`, `add`, `delete`, `rename` (GPT written in the program: CRC-32 from `common/crc32.c` in the programs' library, the copy at the end first, confirmed by typing the disk's name); the ARM serial input could stop for good under load (the PL011 interrupt was cleared after the FIFO was emptied: now before) |
| 23d | `format`: a new, empty RitterFS on a partition or a whole disk, written by the program through `/dev` (byte for byte what `scripts/ritterfs.py format` makes; first the old file system's marks and, on a whole disk, both copies of its partition table are cleared), confirmed by typing the device's name; the kernel probes a written device again and mounts the new file system; at the boot and on hot plug every RitterFS is mounted by its content (any partition type, a whole disk); the system call `time`, from a clock read from the firmware once at the boot and carried on by the CPU's counter (RitterFS commits use it too: no firmware calls while a program's registers are live) |
| 24a | FAT12/16/32 read only (`kernel/fat.c`: the BPB checked, cluster chains with a cache of the last position, long names with their checksums, names found without regard to case); the system calls go through one interface for RitterFS and FAT (`kernel/vfs.c`; snapshots, attributes and instant copies stay RitterFS's: `Operation not supported`, or EXDEV so that `cp` copies bytes); FAT volumes mounted at `/volumes/<label>`, the system's EFI partition at `/boot`, other EFI partitions left alone; `/volumes` listed by name; `scripts/fat.py` makes FAT test images on the host; the test program `filesha` |
| 25 | a laptop with an AMD chipset (two xHCI controllers; the stick a USB 3 one) ended in the rescue console: the kernel set up only the first controller, and the stick's link came up after the boot's look at the ports. Now every xHCI controller is set up (`usb0`, `usb1`, ..., a hot plug thread each, each with its own hubs), and when the firmware booted from USB but the system partition is not there yet, the boot looks at the ports again for at most 10 s. `usblate` on the command line imitates late links in QEMU (the first look finds nothing) |
| 26 | `clear`: an empty screen and the prompt at the top (the escape sequences ESC [ 2 J and ESC [ H, for the console and a terminal on the serial port alike) |
| 24b | writing FAT: create, write (gaps filled with zeros, the 4 GiB limit), truncate, remove, rename (also into another folder, a folder's ".." following, a change of case only), `mkdir`; short names with Windows' case flags or long names with a `~N` alias; the FAT in a window written to every copy, FSInfo kept up to date; a full volume keeps what was written; `chmod` as the read-only attribute; `/boot` read only for good; `scripts/fat.py` reads and checks FAT on the host (`tree`, `cat`, `date`, `check`) |
| 27 | the user's stick (28.8 GiB, the 1 GiB image copied onto it) had no free space for `disks add`: its GPT was made for 1 GiB. `disks` now fits such a table to the whole disk when it writes it (the range partitions may use, the copy at the end, the protective MBR) and shows the space up to the end before |
| 24c | `format -t fat` makes a FAT32 (the reserved sectors with FSInfo and the boot sector's copy, two FATs, the root directory with the label in cluster 2; Windows' cluster sizes; at least 65525 clusters), mounted at once |

(`kernel/net.c`), the program `net` and the rescue console's `net` |
