| 37 | sockets (`kernel/socket.c`): file descriptors for programs (`read`, `write`, `close`; children inherit them), system calls `socket`, `bind`, `connect`, `sendto`, `recvfrom` (with a time limit), UDP first: a queue of 16 datagrams per socket; `atoi`, `atol` in the library |
| 38a | TCP (`kernel/tcp.c`): the three-way handshake, 32 connections with 64 KiB buffers each way, the MSS option, retransmission after a timeout measured per connection (RFC 6298, Karn's rule, backing off), slow start and congestion avoidance, the receiver's window (and probing a closed one), FIN in both orders, TIME_WAIT (2 s), resets; `listen`, `accept`; errors `ECONNREFUSED`, `ECONNRESET`, `EPIPE`, `ENOTCONN`, `EADDRINUSE`; packets to ourselves wait in a queue for the thread `loopback` (delivered inside the sender, TCP's acknowledgements nested until the stack overflowed) |
| 38b | TCP under losses: segments after a gap kept in the receive ring (up to 8 ranges) and acknowledged at once, fast retransmit after three duplicate acknowledgements with fast recovery (NewReno for several losses), partly sent-again segments cut to what is new, a FIN sent again answered again, acknowledgements up to the highest byte ever sent (`snd_max`: after a timeout the sender goes back, and an acknowledgement of data the other side had kept was ignored, so the same segment went out until the connection was given up), only new data timed (Karn), the persist timer for a closed window (probes that never give up while the other side answers); `net` counts segments sent again, kept and probes; `netloss=N` on the command line for the tests |
| 39 | `fetch [-i] [-o FILE] URL`: HTTP/1.1 GET over TCP, Content-Length, chunked bodies or to the end, redirections; `strstr`, `strcspn` in the library |
| 40a | the cryptography for SSH, our own from the RFCs (`user/lib/sha512.c`, `chacha.c`, `c25519.c`, `crypto.h`): SHA-512, ChaCha20 (a 64-bit counter, as OpenSSH's), Poly1305 (26-bit limbs), X25519 (Montgomery ladder, 16-bit limbs) and Ed25519 (extended coordinates, scalars modulo L by long division), no branches on secrets; checked against `scripts/crypto_ref.py`. The programs' library is now an archive, so each program links only what it uses (`cat` 28 KB instead of 38 KB) |
| 40a-2 | 40a's register choice for two-operand machines (both architectures): a result takes its first operand's register when that one's life ends there (`imulq b, a`), and on x86 + * & | ^ whose result is in its right operand's register work in place (`imulq a, d`; not -, whose order matters) instead of through rax. The J1900, ns (40a, now, clang): the int32 dot 3.67, 2.91 (39f-2: 3.26), 1.93; rotation through cf_mul 19.7, 15.2, 14.0; the int16 FIR 30.2, 28.6, 31.8; the rest as 40a's. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (cctest's right operand line has an int case now: t = k - t, k * t, (k | 3) - t; proven by subtraction in place too, which cctest without that case did not notice), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 40b | x86's arithmetic from memory (item 40): a load used once, by the instruction right after it, is that instruction's memory operand (int + - & | ^ *, integer compares, float + - * /; a commutative one's left operand swapped there), the load gone; a commutative one only when its other operand ends there too (else the result needs that operand's copy first: the rotation's pr * x[k].re waited on pr, 19.9 -> 22.9 ns, until this rule); a result in a register its folded address names works through rax. The J1900, ns (40a-2, now, clang): dot 2.41, 2.17, 1.72; double dot 2.61, 2.37, 1.85; FIR 75.7, 67.1, 46.8; compare-and-select 9.06, 8.36, 5.4; the rest as 40a-2's. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture) but libc once on x86 again (nanosleep's "slept enough 0", as in 39e's run; five runs again passed): sleeps end by the 100 Hz tick, which QEMU's TCG delivers in bursts, measured on the TSC's clock (an old matter of the kernel, left as it is). Tests: `cc` (cctest's memory operands line: a result in its address's register, swapped products, compares, unsigned, float division, subtractions whose pointers end there; proven by the register check left out: cctest stops there), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 40c | cc merges loads read again (item 40; both architectures): IR_LOAD and IR_STORE carry `is_volatile` (from the type load() and store() are given; a qualified structure's members are qualified too), and 39c's merging takes loads of the same address, size and sign when nothing between may write memory (a store, memcpy, memset, call, atomic, assembly, va_start/va_arg: no alias analysis, any of them ends every load seen), never a volatile one; a scaling it leaves apart counts as what it scales and by what, so h[i] twice is one address and one load. The packing passes refuse volatile accesses. x86's 40b fold of a commutative operation asks whether its other operand's last use in the block is there (not its one use). Checked by hand: two reads of a volatile int and of a volatile structure's member stay two loads on both architectures. The J1900, ns (40b, now, clang): FIR 67.1, 64.3, 46.8; the written-out rotation 19.9, 18.2, 15.0; compare-and-select 8.36, 8.28, 5.4; the rest as 40b's. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (cctest's reloads line: a store through another pointer to the same place, a call, a structure's copy, an atomic, a char and an int of one address, a loop writing what it reads; proven by stores not ending the loads seen), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 40d | x86's floats and vectors in xmm2-xmm7 too (item 40): the allocator's second pool is 14 registers, xmm8-xmm15 first, then xmm2-xmm7 (pool indexes 8-13; vreg and opnd name them), which a value may hold only where no call or inline assembly lies from its start to its end, both included (CLOBBERS_LOW_FPRS, regalloc's nfpr_kept: a call's arguments and asm operands are those registers). Measured: the J1900's kernels as 40c's (none needs more than 8), and the float moves between registers and the stack in user/lib, user/lib/js and user/apps 3,576 -> 3,567: what is left there lives over calls, where x86-64 keeps no xmm register. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (cctest's ten arguments line: ten floats alive at one call in another order; proven by calls not marked: cctest without that line did not notice), `cc max` (the intrinsics' assembly), selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 40e | cc's loop leftovers (item 40's end): invariant code moves out of loops once before the packing passes too (n - 1 of b[n - 1 - i]: packed now); an unrolled body ending in its steps (i's and 39c-2's pointers') runs each copy u on w + u * step and steps once after the copies; `reassociate` moves constants out of addresses (sext(x + c) is sext(x) + c for a signed int's x + c, (y + c) * k is y * k + c * k, base + (z + c) and (p + c1) + c2 regrouped; each when its operand is not set between), the merging runs again after it, leaves additions of a scaled index apart (each access folds its own) and compares loads by their address's shape (base, index, scale, displacement). x86: (base + index * scale) + c folds whole (`8(%r8,%r12,4)`, both additions folded away), and an added constant whose base lives on past the access folds wherever it was made. The first full run found x86's PNG palette decoding wrong on the system built by our cc (6 of 28 checks; bisected on the J1900 to png.c's decode_png, the alpha loop p.palette[i][3] = 255 unrolled): an added constant over an added constant folded into a store while the inner one was still computed, into its base's register (40a's hazard); an addition address() folds through counts as folded now, so it is never computed. The J1900, ns (item 39's end 39f-2, now, clang): dot 2.79, 1.83, 1.72; double dot 3.00, 2.01, 1.83; FIR 88.9, 56.8, 46.9; rotation 24.0, 19.6 (40d: 18.2; each access says base + index * 8 again, which the J1900's Silvermont seems to pay for), 15.0; through cf_mul 22.1, 15.2, 14.0; compare-and-select 9.5, 8.3, 5.4; the int16 FIR 31.4, 28.6, 31.8; the int32 dot 3.25, 2.92, 1.93; saxpy 1.40, 1.39, 0.79. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture) after the fix. Tests: `cc` (cctest's offsets line: i used as a value too, structure arrays, shorts, doubles, a negative offset, the counter's last value, b[n - 1 - i], an unsigned wrap, and the palette's loop; proven by the signed rule applied to an unsigned addition, by copies one offset off, and by the folded-through addition counted as computed), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 68b | `nanosleep` and `sleep` end by the processor's counter (the user's, 2026-11-09, after item 40: twice a cc-built system under QEMU printed libctest's `slept enough 0`): SYS_SLEEP counted 100 Hz ticks alone, and QEMU delivers ticks in a burst when it catches up after a stall (both failed boots had measured a normal 100 Hz tick, 50 in 500 ms, and a 1001 MHz counter), so a 60 ms sleep could end before 60 ms had passed on the clock `clock_gettime` reads. Now the system call works out its end on `arch_counter()` (rounded up) and each tick only wakes it to look: still short, it waits the ticks the rest needs and looks again; without a counter rate it counts ticks as before; a sleep over a century is a century. Proof: a kernel whose tick counts twice (a burst every tick; the boot counted 100 ticks in 500 ms) printed `nanosleep 0 slept enough 0` with the old sleep and passed libc_shell_test with the new. Tests: libc_shell_test, thrd_shell_test, pthread_shell_test, cancel_shell_test, signals_shell_test on both architectures. poll's, futex's and other waits' timeouts still count ticks. |
| 41a | the event loop beside other work (roadmap item 41's start, the user's names): libwindow's `window_wait_timeout(win, &ev, ms)` waits at most ms milliseconds (0 not at all, negative for good; 1 an event, 0 none with `WINDOW_NONE`, -1 the window closed), `window_timer(win, ms, id)` makes a `WINDOW_TIMER` event (`ev.key` the id) every ms milliseconds until set to 0 (8 per window; deadlines on the monotonic clock, a period on from the last, so no drift; one fallen behind gets one event and goes on from now, never a burst), and `window_fd(win)` is the window's connection for a program's own `poll`: readable when windowd sent something; after a wake-up the program takes every event with `window_wait_timeout(win, &ev, 0)` (events window_clipboard_get read early are kept there). Timers live in the library, as X11's and Wayland's descriptors carry no timers: they do not wake a program's own poll. `window_wait` is now the same wait for good (timers included); its messages go through `to_event`. struct window_event is unchanged (the timer's id in `key`), so libwindow.so.1 only gains 4 items (sdk/abi). Waits count the 100 Hz tick (poll's timeout), the clock deciding: a timer is up to a tick or two late, never early. The test program `wtimer`: a 0 ms and a 200 ms wait that run out, timers of 50 and 120 ms for 600 ms (12 and 4 on both architectures, none early), one removed, a ninth refused, a poll on window_fd beside a pipe another thread writes (the pipe first), then a pointer move making window_fd readable. Proof: a library whose waits ignore the timers' deadlines gave 1 and 1. sdk/CLAUDE.md: the loop with an example. Tests: wtimer_shell_test (new), wkeys_shell_test, wfield_shell_test, wmenu_test, wclose_shell_test, wresize_shell_test, sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41b-1 | 2D drawing, its start (item 41; 41b in three steps: 41b-2 circles, ellipses and rounded rectangles, 41b-3 gradients and images): the library `libdraw.so.1` (`<draw.h>`, user/lib/draw.c) draws on a `struct draw_canvas`, any buffer of 32-bit pixels (its size, stride, channel shifts, bits set in every pixel written, a clip), so a window's own pixels (libwindow's new `window_pixels`, nothing copied) or an RGBA image's. `draw_rgba` makes a colour with alpha; `draw_clip` limits drawing; `draw_rect` (filled, or its outline inside it as a CSS border), `draw_line` (square ends) and `draw_polygon` (filled, self-crossing parts wound twice filled once, or an outline centred on its edges with bevelled corners) take float coordinates (pixel (i, j) the square from (i, j) to (i + 1, j + 1)). One rasteriser fills everything: each edge adds to the cells of its rows the signed area towards the cell's right (font-rs's accumulation), so a row's running sum is each pixel's covered area exactly, at most 1; outlines are pieces wound the same way. Worked on 64 Ki cells at a time over the shape's box within the clip; coordinates beyond a million clamped, NaN too; colours blended by alpha times coverage. Single-precision floats in one order, built with -ffp-contract=off, so every machine draws the same pixels. Tests: draw_host_test (on the Mac, scripts/drawref.py: every pixel's exact area by clipping each piece to the pixel's square, 17 fixed and 400 random shapes, 134,332 pixels within 1/255; proven by an edge's x taken at its top in a cell: 2,306 pixels off) and draw_shell_test (drawtest's 8 scenes' checksums as the Mac's build of it, on x86 and ARM; a window drawn on its own pixels checked in the screenshot; proven by -ffp-contract=fast, ARM's checksums differing, and a blend without the pixel behind, the window's colours wrong), sdk_shell_test and selfbuild_shell_test on both architectures. sdk/CLAUDE.md: drawing on a window. |
| 41b-2 | circles, ellipses and rounded rectangles in libdraw (item 41): `draw_circle(c, cx, cy, r, line, color)`, `draw_ellipse(c, cx, cy, rx, ry, line, color)` and `draw_round_rect(c, x, y, w, h, radius, line, color)` (the radius at most half the width and height), filled or their outline inside them (an ellipse's inner edge the ellipse of radii rx - line, ry - line; a rounded rectangle's corners radius - line). Curves are straight pieces into the same rasteriser, a quarter circle cut into enough of them that none strays more than 1/1024 of a pixel (a step of 2 sqrt(2 s / r), up to 2,048 a quarter); the points' sines and cosines are Taylor series in doubles (to the 23rd power, within 1e-16), not the C library's, so the pixels stay the same on every machine. drawref.py computes these shapes' areas exactly (a disk's part of a rectangle by its integral, an ellipse as a disk scaled across, a rounded rectangle as three rectangles and four quarter disks; the formula checked against the whole, half and quarter disk and 300 strip sums); 428 shapes, 144,595 pixels within 1/255. drawtest's new scene `curves` (a circle of radius 1,000's edge among them). Proven by pieces straying 1/16 pixel (2,060 pixels off). Tests: draw_host_test, draw_shell_test (9 checksums as the Mac's on x86 and ARM), sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41b-3 | gradients and images in libdraw (item 41; 41b done): `draw_gradient(c, x, y, w, h, x0, y0, from, x1, y1, to)` fills a rectangle with `from` at (x0, y0) to `to` at (x1, y1) along the line through them (each pixel by where its centre falls on it, every channel and alpha in a straight line), and `draw_image(c, img, x, y, w, h, angle)` draws a libimage `struct image` (RGBA8888, RGB888 or GRAY8; draw.h includes media.h) scaled to w x h and turned `angle` degrees clockwise about that rectangle's centre: each pixel's centre taken back through the turn and the scale, the image read there between its four nearest pixels weighted by alpha (no dark fringe from transparent ones; edge pixels repeat), shrunk more than twice k x k samples a pixel averaged (k at most 8); its own alpha times the coverage blends it. The angle's cosine and sine are exact at right angles (the series, by quarter), so an image at its own size on whole coordinates, or turned by right angles, is copied exactly. The fill routine takes a colour per pixel (`fill_paint`; the shapes' pixels unchanged: the old checksums stayed). drawref.py: gradients by each centre's place, a turned image's rectangle by its area, the gray pattern copied exactly at 0, 90, 180, 270 and -90 degrees; 440 shapes, 146,576 pixels. drawtest's new scenes `gradients` and `images`. Proven by a gradient taken at each pixel's corner (2,282 pixels off) and images read half a pixel off (900). Tests: draw_host_test, draw_shell_test (11 checksums as the Mac's on x86 and ARM), sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41c-1 | scalable fonts, their start (item 41; 41c in three steps: 41c-2 CFF outlines, 41c-3 `wfont_open` and drawing): GNU FreeFont 20120503's FreeSans, FreeSerif and FreeMono, regular and bold (TrueType outlines; GPL-3.0-or-later with the font exception, the package's README and COPYING beside them as fonts/FreeFont-README.txt and FreeFont-COPYING.txt; its signature checked good against GNU's keyring; fonts/SOURCE.txt), installed as /system/fonts/NAME.ttf (7.5 MB). The reader user/lib/ttf.c (inside libwindow, not exported; ttf.h): the table directory, head, maxp, hhea, hmtx, cmap (formats 4 and 12, Windows's before the Unicode platform's), loca and glyf (simple glyphs; composites moved, scaled, by 2x2 or matched by points, 8 deep; a simple glyph moved by hmtx's left side bearing less its xMin, TrueType's first phantom point, also inside a composite, never the composite itself), every read checked against the file. The test program `ttftest` builds the reader in (the Mac and RitterOS run the same code): `dump` prints the sizes, every glyph's advance and outline and every character's glyph; `scripts/fontref.swift` compares that with macOS's CoreText: all 30,710 glyphs of our six faces and every character from U+0000 to U+10FFFF, then the Mac's own 171 plain TrueType fonts (variable ones and 7 our reader does not open left out), all the same; allowed as CoreText's own: a contour beginning with a control point starts at that midpoint rounded down, a character the font does not map may get another's glyph, contours of fewer than 3 distinct points are left out, some contours turned round (none in FreeFont). Found by it: the side bearing shift and the map's order (Chalkduster's two format 4 maps differ). Proven by composites without their offsets (1,885 FreeSans outlines off), no side bearing shift (34), format 4's indirect lists a glyph on (Monaco: 927 characters) and the Unicode platform's map first (Chalkduster: 145); format 4's delta there is 0 in every font, never seen. On RitterOS `ttftest` gives the Mac's checksums of every glyph and character of the six faces (proven by a reader one advance off on RitterOS only). The existing `fonttest` (22c's bitmap fonts) is unchanged: I had overwritten it while writing this one, before any commit; restored, and the new program named `ttftest` by the user. Tests: ttf_host_test (on the Mac), ttf_shell_test, fonts_shell_test and selfbuild_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41c-2 | CFF outlines (item 41): the reader (user/lib/ttf.c) opens 'OTTO' files through their CFF table (Adobe's technical notes 5176 and 5177): the header, Name, Top DICT, String and Global Subrs INDEXes; the Top DICT's CharStrings, FontMatrix (its scale to font units) and Private DICT with its local Subrs, or a CID-keyed font's FDArray and FDSelect (formats 0 and 3); Type 2 charstrings: every line and curve operator, flex, hflex, hflex1, flex1, stems counted for hintmask's bytes and otherwise skipped, the width before the first clearing operator dropped (advances come from hmtx), local and global subroutines with their bias, 10 deep, 20,000 operators a glyph, the arithmetic operators; cubic control points (TTF_CUBIC) into the same outlines. GNU FreeFont's six faces also as .otf (the user's choice: installed beside the .ttf ones, /system/fonts/NAME.otf, 4.8 MB; the OpenType package's signature checked good, its README and COPYING the same files). fontref.swift: our 12 files and the Mac's 208 fonts (37 of them .otf; --system takes both) all as CoreText reads them. Proven by subroutines' bias always 107 (FreeSans.otf: 3,912 outlines off), hintmask's bytes not skipped (29 of 34 fonts), h/vhcurveto's last extra argument dropped and hflex's sign (both seen only in the Mac's STIXTwoMath). No font here is CID-keyed or uses the arithmetic operators: that code no oracle sees. ttftest on RitterOS gives the Mac's checksums of all 12 files. Tests: ttf_host_test (on the Mac), ttf_shell_test, fonts_shell_test and selfbuild_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41c-3 | scalable fonts drawn (item 41; 41c done): `wfont_open(path, size)` (libwindow) opens a TrueType or OpenType file (a path, or a bare file name in /system/fonts: FreeSans.ttf...) at 4 to 1000 pixels an em and gives a `struct font` like any other (struct font's new `scalable`: the file mapped, the reader, its scale, sizes and kept glyph masks), kept per path and size for the program's life; `wfont_width` is its widest advance, `wfont_height` its ascender + descender + line gap (each rounded), `wfont_text_width` the exact advances' sum rounded once, `wfont_text` (so `window_text_font` too) keeps the pen as a float and draws each glyph's mask at the pen's quarter pixel, blended byte by byte by its coverage (a background fills each advance's cell first); a character the font lacks gets U+FFFD, else .notdef. Masks (`ttf_mask` in the reader: the contours as TrueType/CFF draw them, curves in pieces straying at most 1/1024 pixel, then the coverage) come from the rasteriser libdraw uses: its pieces moved, unchanged, into the internal header user/lib/raster.h (drawtest's checksums the same), which both libraries include. Kept masks (4,096 a font) are never changed or freed, under a lock: fonts may be used from several threads. ttf.c, wfont.c, ttftest and fonttest build with -ffp-contract=off. Tests: ttf_host_test's drawref.py --glyphs (12 glyphs at 9, 17 and 40 pixels, the pen on a whole and a half pixel, every FreeFont file: 219,108 pixels within 1/255 of the outlines' exact area; proven by a control-point pair's curve ending on the second point instead of their midpoint and by cubics as one straight piece), ttf_shell_test (ttftest's line now also checksums a sample line's masks at 4 sizes and 4 pen places: as the Mac's), fonts_shell_test (fonttest: one font by name and path, the line and widest advance from the file's hhea, the text width, "Ag" drawn exactly as the reader's masks, a background, FreeSerif.otf, refusals; proven by the pen's quarter pixel ignored), draw_shell_test (drawtest's window shows "Ritter" in FreeSans at 24 pixels: full and partly lit pixels; proven by text at full strength wherever a glyph touches), sdk_shell_test, selfbuild_shell_test and wfield_shell_test on both architectures. sdk/CLAUDE.md and the manual: smooth text. |
| 41d | text over several lines (item 41; the user's choices: the two functions as proposed, `text_size = 15`): libwindow's `wfont_wrap(font, utf8, width)` gives the bytes of the first line that fits in width pixels (a line breaks after the spaces following a word, spaces at its end not counted to its width; inside a word only when the word alone is wider than a line; after a newline, its byte counted; at least one character), for every kind of font (a scalable one by its exact advances, rounded as wfont_text_width rounds); `wfont_layout(buf, stride, bw, bh, font, x, y, width, utf8, fg, bg)` draws the text so wrapped, a line wfont_height below the one before (newlines not drawn), and returns its height (buf 0: only measures). `wfont_open(path, 0)` opens a font at the user's size: `text_size` in ~/.config/windows, else /system/config/windows (8-64; 15, the built-in font's height, if not set), read once; its line is in the user's windows file's default text (common/settings_defaults.h; windowd writes the file when missing). Tests: fonts_shell_test (fonttest: 9x15's exact wraps: a line that just fits with its space hanging, a pixel less, a word cut, a newline, width 0, UTF-8, nothing; two lines laid out, measured and drawn as wfont_text draws them; FreeSans's lines each fitting with the next word not; size 0 is 15, then 21 after the session adds `text_size = 21` to root's file; 41c-3's refusals now with size -1; proven by spaces counted to a line's width and by the user's file left unread), settings_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. sdk/CLAUDE.md and the manual: text over several lines, text_size. |
| 41e-1 | the widget set's start, and focus events (item 41: 41e-1 with 41f-1). `libwidget.so.1` (`<widget.h>`, user/lib/widget.c; needs libwindow and libdraw): a tree of widgets -- `widget_row`, `widget_column`, `widget_grid(columns)` with `widget_add` and `widget_stretch` (a row's or column's spare length to the stretching children by weight; leaves at their natural size, centred in a row; 8 pixels apart, the root 8 from the edges), `widget_label` (wrapped at 400 pixels), `widget_button`, `widget_check`, `widget_radio` (groups as rings: choosing one clears the rest) -- with `widget_on` (a callback when used), `widget_value`/`widget_set_value`, `widget_text`/`widget_set_text`; `widget_run(win, root)` lays the tree out to the window (again after a resize), draws it with libdraw on the window's pixels in FreeSans at the user's text_size (Nord's dark colours, as windowd's), and runs its events: a click (press and release on the same control) or Space/Enter uses the control with the keyboard, Tab and Shift+Tab move it round the controls (a focus ring in the accent colour, shown only while the window has the keyboard); a change marks a widget to draw again (its rectangle only) or the tree to lay out again; until the window closes (0) or `widget_quit` (1); `widget_free`. (41f-1) windowd tells each window when it gets or loses the keyboard: each client's thread, before sending its queue (every 10 ms at most), compares `focused_window()` with what it last told it (`focus_told`, -1 at its creation) and queues `WMSG_FOCUS` (18), so every way focus moves (a click, a window opened, closed, minimised) is seen; libwindow's `WINDOW_FOCUS` (11, `ev.key` 1 or 0). The test program `widgettest` (a form; `focus` prints a window's focus events) and the SDK's example `sdk/examples/widgets/` (built by sdk_shell_test). Tests: widget_shell_test (keys Tab, Space, Tab, Enter, Tab, Tab, Space, Shift+Tab, Space and a click on Big: the callbacks in that order and the values; the background and the focus ring in screenshots; focus 1, 0 when wclient opens in front, 1 when clicked; proven by windowd telling only the first state, Shift ignored and no ring drawn), sdk_shell_test, windowd_shell_test, wkeys_shell_test, wfield_shell_test, wmenu_test, owl_menu_test, termwin_shell_test (their programs now also get focus events) and selfbuild_shell_test on both architectures. sdk/CLAUDE.md and the manual: controls. |
| 41e-2 | sliders, progress bars, tabs; window limits (item 41: 41e-2 with 41f-2). libwidget: `widget_slider(min, max, value)` (dragged: its value follows the pointer while pressed; Left/Down, Right/Up a step, Page Up/Down a tenth, Home/End the ends), `widget_progress(max, value)` (filled to value, no input), `widget_tabs()` (its children pages, each titled by its text: widget_set_text now takes any widget; the open page its value, chosen by a click on a title or Left/Right while it has the keyboard; only the open page is drawn, clicked or reached by Tab (`shown`); as large as its largest page); values kept within each widget's range. (41f-2) `window_set_limits(win, min_w, min_h, max_w, max_h)` sends `WMSG_LIMITS` (19: the least and greatest content size packed as w | h << 16, 0 none); windowd's `within_limits` keeps a resize's and a maximize's size to them; `widget_run` sets the least to what its layout needs and grows a window opened smaller. Found on the way: wresize_shell_test failed on x86 every time since 41e-1 (bisected to windowd's focus messages, which changed the timing): windowd's first full frame's copy to the screen (DISPLAY_FLUSH) failed partway with EFAULT, leaving the console's rows on the screen (the 41d picture had a smaller strip too); windowd ignored it. Now a failed flush is marked again and copied the next frame (`flush_failures`, "N again" in the frame report); wresize passed 3 of 3. Why the kernel's copy from windowd's valid shared back buffer fails is open (to look at separately). widgettest's `more` (tabs, a slider, a progress bar in a 200x100 window that grows) and `limits` modes. Tests: widget_shell_test (keys through tabs, slider and a button filling the progress bar; the window grew; dragged 200 pixels smaller it is 250x150, maximized 400x300; proven by windowd ignoring the limits, no growing, and hidden pages reached by Tab), wresize_shell_test (proven: without the retry it failed 3 of 3), windowd_shell_test, owl_max_test, wmin_test, termwin_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41e-3 | lists, tables, trees; hover events (item 41: 41e-3 with 41f-3; the user's: `WMSG_HOVER`, items as text). libwidget: `widget_list(lines)`, `widget_table(columns, rows)` (a tab between cells, its first line the header; columns as wide as their widest cell) and `widget_tree(lines)` (leading tabs a node's level; a node followed by deeper ones a branch, closed at first, a triangle showing it) share rows parsed from their text (`parse_rows`; widget_set_text gives new ones): widget_value is the chosen row (a table's under its header, a tree's line among all; -1 none), widget_text its text; 8 rows high at most by nature (3 at least), a scroll bar (a thumb as long as what shows, dragged) when they do not fit, the wheel scrolling the rows under the pointer 3 a step; a click chooses (a tree's triangle opens or closes), Up/Down, Page Up/Down, Home/End move the choice (kept in view); a tree's Right opens, Left closes or goes to the parent, Space/Enter open or close; the row and the button under the pointer are highlighted; text clipped to its widget (drawn into that part of the window's pixels). (41f-3) windowd's `hover_follow` after every pointer event: the window whose content is under the pointer (none over a frame, a title bar, the desktop, while a menu is open), each change sent to both as `WMSG_HOVER` (20); libwindow's `WINDOW_ENTER` (12) and `WINDOW_LEAVE` (13). widgettest's `rows` and `hover` modes. Tests: widget_shell_test (the wheel over the list and a click on its first row: row 3; keys through the list, the tree (open, to the parent, close, Space) and the table; the pointer in, onto the title bar, in, out to the desktop: enter, leave, enter, leave -- the pointer parked on the desktop first, as QEMU moves it 127 pixels a step and a way across the window entered and left it; proven by the wheel ignored, Left not going to the parent, and a title bar counted as content), windowd_shell_test, wmenu_test, owl_menu_test, wfield_shell_test, termwin_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41e-4 | an editor; typed text and pointer shapes (item 41: 41e-4 with 41f-4). libwidget's `widget_editor(text, lines)`: a text over `lines` lines wrapped at its width (wfont_wrap; scrolled to keep the cursor in view, by the wheel, a scroll bar as a list's), or with 1 a one-line entry (scrolled sideways; newlines typed or pasted become spaces); typing (WINDOW_TEXT) replaces the selection; Left/Right a character (with a selection: to its end), Up/Down and Page Up/Down keep the column, Home/End the line's ends (Ctrl: the text's), Backspace, Delete, Enter (a newline, several lines only); Shift with any of them, or a drag, selects (shown in the accent colour, translucent); a double click selects a word; Ctrl+A, Ctrl+C, Ctrl+X, Ctrl+V through windowd's clipboard (64 KiB); the cursor a 1.5-pixel bar blinking every 530 ms (widget_run's window_timer); widget_value is the cursor (a byte), widget_text the text; its function at every change. (41f-4) windowd turns the key bytes into typed text (`text_of`: a whole UTF-8 character, never control bytes nor a key's escape sequence, ESC [ or ESC O to its final byte; a lone ESC no text) and sends each as `WMSG_TEXT` (22) after its `WMSG_KEY` bytes; libwindow's `WINDOW_TEXT` (14, `ev.key` the code point). `window_set_cursor(win, WINDOW_CURSOR_ARROW/TEXT/HAND)` sends `WMSG_CURSOR` (21): windowd draws an I-beam or a hand (16x16, white with a black outline, the hotspot at the I-beam's stem) while the pointer is over that window's content; widget_run asks for the I-beam over editors. widgettest's `text` and `editor` modes. Tests: widget_shell_test (a, Shift+B, Up, Escape, ' then e: 61, 42, e9; the editor typed into, a selection replaced, a click at the first line's start, Ctrl+A/C, Ctrl+V into the entry (its newline a space), Backspace, Home: `Zhello|wxld` and `AZhello wxl` with the cursor at 1; an I-beam over the editor, an arrow over Done; proven by escape sequences taken as text, no shape asked for, and the entry keeping newlines), wkeys_shell_test, wfield_shell_test, termwin_shell_test, owl_newfolder_test, windowd_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41e-5 | dialogs (item 41: 41e-5 with 41f-5). libwidget's `widget_message(parent, title, text)` (an OK button) and `widget_question(parent, title, text, yes, no)` (1 for yes; 0 for no, Escape or the dialog closed): a column of the text (a label) and a row of buttons pushed right, measured before its window opens (at least 240 wide), run by a widget_run of its own until answered (so from a callback too); the first button has the keyboard (the root's `start`), Escape presses the last (`cancel`); the window behind is drawn without the keyboard's ring meanwhile (the run under it, found through the runs' chain `innermost`/`under`). (41f-5) `window_dialog_open(parent, w, h, title)`: libwindow keeps windowd's id of each window (WMSG_CREATED's) and sends a dialog's parent as (id + 1) << 16 in WMSG_CREATE's c beside the title's length (old clients send lengths below 64: unchanged); windowd's `parent` per window: the dialog opens centred over it and in front, a press on a window with a dialog open (`dialog_of`, the innermost) brings the dialog forward and does nothing else, and when the parent closes its dialogs belong to nobody. widgettest's `dialog` mode. Tests: widget_shell_test (a question answered by Tab+Enter, Enter, Escape: 0, 1, 0; a message while the window behind is clicked on its Quit: the dialog forward, its Escape closes it, Quit only after; no ring behind an open dialog; proven by windowd without the tie (the message never closed) and by the screenshot before the ring fix: 396 ring pixels behind), windowd_shell_test, windowd2_test, wmenu_test, wmin_test, owl_newfolder_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41e-6 | full screen, minimising, drag and drop (item 41: 41e-6 with 41f-6; the user's names and full screen's behaviour). libwindow's `window_fullscreen(win, 1)` sends `WMSG_FULLSCREEN` (23): windowd keeps the window's place and size, takes its frame and title bar away (side(), title_h(), content_left() give 0 for it; its menu bar is hidden) and asks for the screen's size at (0,0), in front (also after a mode change); the taskbar is drawn under it and takes no clicks; `window_fullscreen(win, 0)` puts the place and size back; its limits do not apply, and leaving is the program's (windowd has no key for it). `window_minimize(win)` sends `WMSG_MINIMIZE` (24): to a taskbar button, as the `-` box. Drag and drop: `window_drag(win, text, n)` sends `WMSG_DRAG` (25) with up to 64 KiB of text; windowd takes it only while the left button is still down on the window in front, draws a page beside the pointer, and when the button is let go over a window's content (not one with a dialog open) queues `WMSG_DROP` (26: where, and the text's length) for it, its client thread writing the text right after the message; libwindow reads the text as it reads the message (also while waiting for the clipboard) and gives `WINDOW_DROP` (15, `ev.x`/`ev.y`) with `window_drop_text(win)`. owl drags an entry pressed and moved 5 pixels (`..` excepted) as its full path. widgettest's `screen` and `drop` modes. Found on the way: wtimer_shell_test failed now and then since 41e-1 (a window's first focus event comes from windowd's client thread up to 10 ms after it opens, and fell into wtimer's waits that must find nothing): wtimer waits for it first (passed twice on each architecture). Tests: widget_shell_test (f, f, m and a click on the taskbar button: sizes 1280 x 800 (ARM 800 x 600) and 300 x 200, focus 0 and 1; the screenshots at full screen (its corners the window's) and back (its frame); a file pressed in owl and moved 270 pixels onto a full-screen window: the page on the pointer, `widgettest: drop 30 257 /home/root/d/note.txt` (ARM 30 193); proven by windowd placing a full-screen window's content inside a frame (the corner the desktop's), ignoring WMSG_MINIMIZE (no focus 0) and ignoring drags (no page, no drop)), windowd_shell_test, windowd2_test, wmenu_test, wmin_test, owl_max_test, wresize_shell_test, owl_newfolder_test, wtimer_shell_test, wfield_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. sdk/CLAUDE.md, the manual and owl's page. |
| 41g-1 | app files and the desktop menu from them (item 41: 41g-1; the user's split, names and keys). Each windowed app has a file beside it, `/system/<arch>/apps/NAME.info` (user/apps/NAME.info, installed by the Makefile's APP_INFOS), `key = value` lines as the settings files': `name` (the label), `about` (one line), `types` (the file endings it opens; used from 41g-2): iBrowser, Image viewer, owl, Terminal, Video player. windowd's desktop menu (a right-click on the background) reads them each time it opens (an app installed later is there): the apps by name, sorted without regard to case (NAME without a `name`), then Close window server; the choice starts the program NAME as before. A file ending `.info` in apps is no program: the shell's `help` leaves it out, osinstall does not count it, owl opens it in the editor. sdk/CLAUDE.md: how an app gets into the menu. Tests: wdesktop_shell_test (the test's own aaa.info, "A test app", first: Terminal at item 4 starts term, Image viewer at item 2 imageview, Close window server at item 6; `help` lists imageview and no .info file; proven by windowd ignoring the names (the order by program: no terminal) and a shell listing them (6 .info files)), windowd_shell_test, owl_newfolder_test, osinstall_test and selfbuild_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41g-2 | opening a file with its app (item 41: 41g-2; the user's names). libwindow's `window_app_for(path, buf, size)`: the app files /system/<arch>/apps/NAME.info whose `types` lists the file's ending (any case; the first NAME in order when several do): NAME into buf, 0 for a windowed app, 1 for one that runs in a terminal (`terminal = yes`), -1 for none. New app files for the command-line programs that open files: tune.info (`types = wav`) and edit.info (`types = txt md c h`), both `terminal = yes`; the desktop menu lists windowed apps only (NAME in apps: tune and edit are bin's). The new program `open FILE...` (macOS's meaning) opens each file as owl's double click: a folder in owl, a file in its app (`term NAME FILE` for a terminal one), another program in a terminal, anything else in the editor; a line for each (`open: FILE in imageview`), exit 1 when one could not be opened (the others are). owl asks window_app_for instead of its fixed table (so .html files now open in iBrowser). docs/commands/open.md, the manual, owl's page, sdk/CLAUDE.md. Tests: open_shell_test (new: Solid.PPM in imageview, a video in reel, a folder in owl, note.txt and a .wav in edit and tune in terminals, a file of no app's in the editor, a script run in a terminal, a missing one refused, rc=1; four terminals; proven by a window_app_for minding the ending's case and one ignoring `terminal`), wdesktop_shell_test (its test app now a real program in apps, as only windowed apps are listed; proven by a menu listing tune and edit: Terminal moved), wopen_shell_test, man_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. Found: owl_shell_test fails at 41g-1's commit too (a row lower than it expects: /system/<arch>'s test/ above kernel.elf), left for the user. |
| 41g-3 | app icons (item 41: 41g-3; the user's names and size). An app file's `icon = NAME.png` names a 32x32 PNG with transparency beside it; windowd (now linking libimage) loads it once per app, halves it (each pixel the alpha-weighted mean of four) and draws it at 16x16 left of the app's name in the desktop menu (the rows keep their height, the menu is 20 pixels wider) and on the taskbar buttons of the app's windows. windowd knows a window's app by its program: libc's `getprogname()` (BSD's and macOS's: the last part of argv[0], set at the start) goes in WMSG_CREATE after the title behind the new flag bit WMSG_CREATE_APP (a length byte and the name), so older clients still work. The icons (ibrowser, imageview, owl, reel, term) are drawn by our scripts/mkicons.py (shapes in the Nord colours, 4x4 samples a pixel) and kept in git. An app's own files in apps (NAME.info, NAME.png) are no programs: owl and `open` now open them by type (41g-1's owl would have run a .info in a terminal: installed files have x bits). sdk/CLAUDE.md, the manual, owl's page. Tests: wdesktop_shell_test (owl's and reel's icons in the menu, term's on its taskbar button; proven by no icons drawn: all three fail, and by no app name sent: only the taskbar's), libc_shell_test (getprogname, compared with the Mac's), owl_launch_test (its key count now from the installed apps folder: broken since 41g-1's .info files), open_shell_test, widget_shell_test, sdk_shell_test, osinstall_test and selfbuild_shell_test on both architectures. Seen again: x86's selfbuild hung 9 hours in one `ssh 'cat > FILE'` copy (the file whole on the guest, its cat waiting for the end of its input, sshd never given the channel's EOF; the guest idle): the copy ended by hand, the test rerun alone passed. |
| 41g-4 | notifications (item 41: 41g-4; the user's names). libwindow's `window_notify(win, title, text)` sends WMSG_NOTIFY (27: the title's and text's lengths, then both; the text up to WINDOW_NOTIFY_MAX 512 with its NUL); win 0 opens a connection of its own, whose one message it is (windowd's client_work takes a first WMSG_NOTIFY without a window). windowd draws each as a box at the top right (320 pixels wide, 8 from the edges), over every window and under an open menu: the title in bold, the text wrapped below (wfont_layout); up to four, the newest on top (a fifth drops the oldest); the thread `notes_thread` takes each away after 5 s. The new program `notify TEXT...` shows the words (exit 1 without windowd, 2 without words). docs/commands/notify.md, windowd's page, the manual, sdk/CLAUDE.md. check_screen.py's new `textat-X-Y-FONT-FG-BG-TEXT` (text pixel-exact where it is drawn). Tests: notify_shell_test (new: notify without windowd and without words; widgettest's new `notify` mode, a window's notification with a title and a text in two lines, and the shell's, both pixel-exact, the shell's on top; both gone 6 s later; proven by windowd ignoring notifications, the four texts missing, and by boxes that never go), widget_shell_test, wdesktop_shell_test, man_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41g-5 | the user's colours (item 41: 41g-5; the user's names and choices). Two keys in ~/.config/windows (else /system/config/windows), in its default text: `theme = dark|light` (dark if not set: Nord's polar night, every window's colours before; light: its snow storm) and `accent = RRGGBB` (the colour of what is in front and chosen). libwindow's `window_color(WINDOW_COLOR_*)` gives a part's colour as 0xRRGGBB, read once: BACKGROUND, PANEL, FACE, FACE_PRESSED, HOVER, TEXT, EDGE, ACCENT (focus, progress; 88C0D0 dark) and SELECTION (chosen rows, the title bar in front; 5E81AC), the accent both when set (so nothing changes for anyone without it). libwidget's palette is window_color's; the scroll bar's trough too. windowd keeps its frame colours in two tables chosen by `theme` (the light one: a D8DEE9 desktop, E5E9F0 bars and dropdowns with dark text), the window in front's title bar and menus' chosen items in the accent; owl and term take light colours when the theme's background is light (term: 2E3440 on ECEFF4). The manual, windowd's page, sdk/CLAUDE.md. Tests: theme_shell_test (new: a light theme with accent BF616A: term's light background and dark text, the light desktop, owl's title bar behind with its dark title and its light panel, widgettest's title bar in front in the accent, libwidget's background and button face at exact points (the dark theme's text, edges and background are the light one's background, face and text: regions would not tell) and its checked box in the accent; proven by windowd, window_color, owl and term each ignoring the theme, and windowd and window_color ignoring the accent), settings_test (the new default text), termwin_shell_test, widget_shell_test, wdesktop_shell_test, owl_menu_test, notify_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures (the dark theme unchanged). |
| 41h-1 | a window server without a screen (item 41: 41h-1; the user's names). `windowd headless WxH` (64-8192 each way; else `usage: windowd [headless WxH]`, exit 2) takes no screen and no input devices: it composes into its back buffer only (the screen's pixel format, from the display's info, so clients' pixels fit), flushes nothing, waits for no vertical blank, starts no input thread and no `startup` program, and any user may run one (it never asks for the display). The environment variable `WINDOW_SOCKET` names where windowd listens and libwindow connects (window_open, window_notify), /run/windowd when unset, so a headless one runs beside the screen's: `windowd: listening on /tmp/w1, screen 640x480 (headless)`. windowd's page, window.h, sdk/CLAUDE.md (testing a windowed program over SSH). Tests: headless_shell_test (new: root's screen windowd and a headless one at /tmp/w1 side by side, widgettest's window in the headless one and not on the screen, bad sizes refused; bob over SSH runs his own with a window in it; proven by a headless windowd ignoring WINDOW_SOCKET, one taking the screen, and libwindow ignoring the variable), man_shell_test, wdesktop_shell_test, wuser_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41h-2 | screenshots (item 41: 41h-2; the user's names). The program `wshot FILE.png` sends WMSG_SHOT (28) as a connection's one message to the windowd at $WINDOW_SOCKET or /run/windowd (its user's or root's); windowd draws what is pending, copies its whole screen under its lock into a new shared buffer and answers with the size and pixel format; wshot saves it through image_save (`wshot: FILE (WxH)`; exit 1 without a windowd, 2 without a file). libimage writes PNG: user/lib/deflate.c, our own DEFLATE in a zlib stream (LZ77 through hash chains of three-byte prefixes, 48 links, 32 KiB back; one block with the fixed Huffman codes), and png.c's encode_png (RGB, or RGBA when anything is transparent; each row's filter by the smallest signed sum); image_save chooses .png by the ending. A desktop-like 1280x800 picture: 4,096,000 bytes of pixels -> 23,025. wshot's page, windowd's, sdk/CLAUDE.md (a headless program's picture), image.h. Tests: pngenc_host_test (new, on the Mac: deflate.c and png.c under ASan/UBSan, 11 streams and 10 pictures read back by Python's zlib and scripts/pngcheck.py's own PNG decoder, and by ImageIO; proven by distances' extra bits one off at 32768 and the average filter rounding up), wshot_shell_test (new: a headless 640x400 windowd with widgettest's window and a notification; the PNG decoded on the Mac to the desktop, window, title bar and notification text where windowd draws them, and by imagetest on RitterOS to the same SHA-256; proven by wshot taking blue for red and by image_save writing PPM for .png; the pending drawing before the copy is not tested: the session waits a second), image_shell_test, png_shell_test, headless_shell_test, man_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41h-3 | input for a headless windowd (item 41: 41h-3; the user's names). The program `wsend` (`click X Y`, `right X Y`, `move X Y`, `drag X1 Y1 X2 Y2` in steps of at most 16 pixels, `type TEXT` as key bytes, `key enter|tab|escape|backspace|up|down|left|right` as the keyboard sends one: the physical key's press, its bytes, its release) sends WMSG_INPUT (29) messages, each a struct sys_input_event, on a connection of its own to $WINDOW_SOCKET or /run/windowd. windowd's input thread's work per event is now input_event(), which both the devices' events and WMSG_INPUT go through; a headless windowd answers each event (a: 1) once every program's queue has gone out (at most 2 s: it keeps 32 events a window and drops the rest, which a person's typing never meets but wsend's would: without the wait 54 typed bytes arrived as "the quick brow"), a windowd on the screen refuses (a: 0; `wsend: refused: only a headless windowd takes input`, exit 1). A click on the desktop menu's Close window server gets no answer (windowd ends): `wsend: the window server stopped answering`. wsend's page, windowd's, sdk/CLAUDE.md. Tests: wsend_shell_test (new: refused on the screen, bad arguments; a click focusing widgettest's editor, 54 bytes typed, Enter, Backspace, Left, a click on the close box: the editor's text exact; a title bar dragged, seen by wshot; a right-click's desktop menu closing that windowd; proven by a windowd taking input on the screen, one answering before the queues went out, and a drag moving with the button let go), wdesktop_shell_test (its help check now counts names ending .info or .png, not wshot's usage text), wshot, headless, widget, wkeys, owl_menu, man and sdk on both architectures. The first run lost the end of the editor's input on ARM once (not repeated alone): the queue's overflow, found and fixed above. |
| 41h-4 | the SDK's window parts (item 41: 41h-4; item 41 done). sdk/CLAUDE.md: a map of item 41's parts (header, library, step: windows and events, libdraw, fonts and layout, libwidget, the app file and desktop, testing without a screen) and code for the controls it only named (slider, progress, list, table, tree, editor, tabs, the two dialogs); every interface already in sdk/abi (abicheck). A new example, sdk/examples/notes: a note kept in ~/notes.txt (an editor, Save with a notification, Clear behind a question dialog), its app file notes.info, and its own test.sh as a developer would write one: on the machine over SSH, a headless windowd of its own beside the screen's, the note typed and saved with wsend, the screen kept with wshot, ~/notes.txt checked. Tests: sdk_shell_test builds it outside the tree as the guide says, deploys it and its app file over SSH, runs its test.sh on the machine (`notes test: ok`) and reads wshot's PNG on the Mac: the notification's title and text pixel-exact; proven by notes without its notification (both texts missing) and a test.sh that types nothing (`notes test: FAILED`); on both architectures. |
| 41g-6 | owl shows apps with their icons (item 41, after the user's testing: owl listed each app's program, NAME.info and NAME.png; the user's design: dot files). The app files are installed as dot files, /system/<arch>/apps/.NAME.info and .NAME.png (`icon = .NAME.png`; their sources stay user/apps/NAME.info and NAME.png), read so by windowd's desktop menu and icons, window_app_for and open. owl lists no name starting with a dot, in any folder (Unix's hidden files: ~/.config too; `ls` still shows them all), and draws a program's icon (16x16, halved as windowd does, blended over the row) left of its name when a .NAME.info beside it names one, in any folder; a folder with such an app moves every name right by 20 pixels. scripts/mkdisk.py had left every dot file out of the disk images since 12c (meant for the Mac's .DS_Store): now only .DS_Store and ._ files. A machine updated in place keeps its old undotted NAME.info and NAME.png: delete them there. owl's page, the manual, open's and windowd's pages, sdk/CLAUDE.md and the notes example. Tests: owl_launch_test (owl in apps: the rows count no dot file, owl's and reel's icons drawn; proven by owl listing dot files and by owl drawing no icons), wdesktop_shell_test (its test app's .aaa.info), open_shell_test, wopen_shell_test, owl_menu_test, osinstall_test, man_shell_test, sdk_shell_test (the notes example's .notes.info) and selfbuild_shell_test (our make's `apps/.%.info` rule) on both architectures. |
| 42a | each user's file types (item 42: 42a; the user's design and names). `~/.config/filetypes` holds `ENDING = PROGRAM` lines, the program a double click in owl and `open FILE` start for a file (the same 13 as before: ppm, bmp, png, jpg, jpeg in imageview, rvid in reel, html, htm in ibrowser, wav in tune, txt, md, c, h in edit); its default text is common/settings_defaults.h's SETTINGS_USER_FILETYPES, written by login at a login (with ~/.config's other files; its README names it) and by window_app_for when missing; a line there wins over /system/config/filetypes (optional, an administrator's). window_app_for reads it (the ending in small letters) and the program's place says how it runs: an app in /system/<arch>/apps (0, its window), a program in bin (1, a terminal), else none (-1). tune.info and edit.info are gone (terminal programs have no app file; the `terminal` key with them); an app file's `types` now says what the app can open (for 42c's Open with...). open's and owl's pages, the manual (a File types part), window.h, sdk/CLAUDE.md. Tests: open_shell_test (the same nine lines, and copy.pic in imageview by a line root added; proven by a window_app_for that minds the ending's case, one that reads only /system/config/filetypes and one that runs every program in its window), settings_test (written back by window_app_for, and by login; proven by each not writing it), userconf_shell_test (root's ~/.config: README filetypes login windows), wopen_shell_test, wdesktop_shell_test, owl_launch_test, man_shell_test and sdk_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 42b | app bundles (item 42: 42b; the user's design and names). A windowed app is the folder /system/<arch>/apps/NAME.program/ holding its program NAME, its `info` (name, about, types: what it can open) and its `icon.png` (the `icon` key gone), built from user/apps/NAME.c, NAME.info and NAME.png (the Makefile's app_program rule per app; 41g-6's dot files gone). Every way to find a program by name takes NAME.program/NAME in apps: sh (and `help`, which lists bundles), exec.c's search for execvp and posix_spawnp (in every folder of PATH), sudo, env, xargs, ldd, term, windowd (startup, the desktop menu from the bundles' info, icons from their icon.png), open (a bundle given as a folder is started: `open: DIR started`; a bundle's own files are data) and window_app_for; osinstall counts bundles. owl shows a bundle, in any folder, as one item among the files: its icon, NAME; opening runs NAME; **Show contents** (Edit menu, a row's menu) goes inside. The SDK's `make deploy` with KIND = apps builds the bundle (the program, APP.info and APP.png as info and icon.png). Found by the new test: since 41g-5 a local `sel` (the selection's colour) in owl's main hid the selected row, so File > Open, Edit > Rename and Show contents read a colour as a row (Show contents crashed): now sel_color. A machine updated in place keeps its old apps/NAME files and dot files: delete them there. The manual, owl's, windowd's, open's and the lookup programs' pages, sdk/CLAUDE.md. Tests: owl_launch_test (bundles as items with icons, Show contents into term.program, term run; proven by owl taking bundles for folders), exec_shell_test (execlp by name finds imageview's bundle; proven by a search without bundles), ldso_shell_test (sh by name; proven by sh's lookup without bundles), wdesktop_shell_test (its test app a bundle; proven by a menu reading no bundles), and owl_menu, owl_go, owl_cursor, owl_max, owl_newfolder, owl_resize, open, wopen, image, video, termcmd, termwin, wtermsel, theme, wdouble, wfit, widget, wmenu, wuser, osinstall, man, sdk and selfbuild on both architectures (owl_shell_test fails as before: kernel.elf a row lower). |
| 68c | osinstall copies the drivers (outside an item, after the user installed the 42b stick on the VivoBook's SATA disk: "WiFi is not working"; net said no network cards, pci listed the 8265). Since the drivers became modules (67a), /system/<arch>/drivers holds every network, Wi-Fi, sound and graphics driver, and osinstall never copied it: a machine installed from a stick since then had none (the three machines were installed before 67a and got their modules over SSH). osinstall now copies it with the other folders; its page says so (a machine installed before: copy the folder by hand, booted from the stick: cp -r /system/<arch>/drivers /volumes/NAME/system/<arch>/drivers). Tests: osinstall_test (the installed drivers' names and contents as the build's; the installed /system/<arch> lists drivers/; proven by an osinstall that copies no drivers) and man_shell_test on both architectures. |
| 41h-5 | wsend holds keys, by many more names (after a report from the agent porting Doom: wsend could not drive a game; the user's choice of names). `wsend down NAME` presses a key (its physical key, then its bytes) and leaves it held until `wsend up NAME`; `key NAME` presses and lets go as before. The names: a-z, 0-9, space, enter, tab, escape, backspace, the arrows, home, end, pageup, pagedown, insert, delete, ctrl, shift, alt (the left ones), f1-f12; each sends the bytes the keyboard sends for the key alone (none for the modifiers and the F keys). wsend's page. | 41h-6 | waiting for a window server or a window (after the Doom port's report: scripted tests waited with `sleep 1`; the user's choice of names). `wsend wait` returns once the windowd at $WINDOW_SOCKET (or /run/windowd) answers, `wsend wait TITLE` once a window of that title is open, looking every 100 ms for at most 10 s (else `wsend: no window server at PATH (waited 10 s)` or `wsend: no window "TITLE" at PATH (waited 10 s)`, exit 1). N/minute` (roadmap item 12, its last step): a limit rule lets at most N new connections a minute through, counted per other machine (the sender coming in; a machine guessing passwords uses up only its own allowance: the choice of RitterOS's, a global count would let one machine lock everyone out), the minute counted from its first connection; 64 (rule, machine) pairs counted at once, the oldest giving way; loading rules starts the counts afresh. Test: `guardlimit` |
| 46a | exFAT read (`kernel/exfat.c`, roadmap item 9): the boot region checked (its checksum; the backup copy if the first is damaged), the FAT, files and directories in one run (NoFatChain) or in pieces, entry sets checked by their checksum, names in UTF-16 over several entries, found without regard to case through the volume's own up-case table (its checksum checked; else a-z only, logged) and the name hash, the valid data length (the rest reads as zeros), times converted from their zone to UTC; IDs by the file entry's place with a bit for a directory in one run (its set may go on in the next cluster). Mounted read only (writing: 46b). `scripts/exfat.py` makes test sticks (and checks them: macOS's `fsck_exfat` found ours OK) and reads them. Test: `exfatshell` |
| 46b | exFAT written (roadmap item 9): new files and folders (entry sets with their name hash and checksum; folders always as a FAT chain), writing and appending (a file in one run becomes a FAT chain when it grows; past the valid data the gap is written as zeros), overwriting, truncating, removing (the set's entries no longer in use, the clusters freed in the bitmap and the FAT), renaming and moving (the set made anew, also for a change of case), the read-only attribute; a folder grows by a cluster when it is full. Bitmap and FAT a sector at a time in memory, written at the latest at each commit; VolumeDirty set from the first change to the commit (and the percent in use then). TexFAT (two FATs) stays read only. Test: `exfatwrite`, checked on the host by `scripts/exfat.py check` and macOS's `fsck_exfat` (attached read only as a device) |
| 47a | Realtek RTL8168G and H (the cause it was meant for was another one: 47b): the start settings Linux's driver makes (`rtl_hw_start_8168g`, `_8168h_1`) through the chip's ERI registers (ERIDR 0x70, ERIAR 0x74): the receive and send FIFOs' sizes, the pause thresholds, the packet filter reset, power saving that gets in the way off. The user's laptop photo after 46b (an RTL8168H, version 0x541) showed the chip receiving (MSI 69, broadcasts 76) but sending nothing ("chip sent 0", 13 frames to send); the J1900's RTL8168G worked, its firmware had probably set them. `net` shows an `ERI` line (the FIFO sizes, the first frame still waiting). Our QEMU model now has the ERI registers and, like that laptop, sends nothing until the send FIFO is set: the `realtek` test, without the new settings, showed the laptop's picture exactly (chip sent 0, TxConfig 0x57100f80, no address) |
| 46c | `format -t exfat`: a new exFAT on a partition or a whole disk, as the specification and Windows lay it out (the boot region and its backup with their checksum, one FAT from 1 MiB on, sector 24 under 64 MiB, the cluster heap on a cluster boundary: the allocation bitmap, the full up-case table and the root directory with the label, each chained in the FAT); clusters of 4 KiB up to 256 MiB, 32 KiB up to 32 GiB, 128 KiB beyond; labels of up to 11 characters, also beyond ASCII. The up-case table is made at build time from Python's Unicode data (`scripts/exfat_upcase.py`, into `build/gen`). Test: `exfatformat` (a 16 MiB and a 496 MiB partition, a whole 40 GiB stick), checked on the host by `scripts/exfat.py check` and macOS's `fsck_exfat` |
| 47b | Realtek: a chip's family (RTL8168 or RTL8125) from the version it reports, as Linux decides it, not from its PCI ID. The laptop's card is a Killer E2600 (10ec:2600, its `pci` photo after 47a), an RTL8168H; the ID list took 0x2600 for the Killer E3000 (an RTL8125, really 0x3000), so the driver used the RTL8125's registers: receiving (shared registers) worked, sending did not (the RTL8125's TxPoll at 0x90 instead of 0x38): "chip sent 0, 13 to send". 47a's FIFO settings, a wrong guess at the cause, stay (Linux makes them); the model no longer claims a chip needs them. The `realtek` test's first chip has the laptop's ID and version: the old driver failed it exactly as the laptop did (chip sent 0, 42 to send, no address) |
| 48a | `osinstall` (roadmap item 14, the user's design and name): asks which disk (every disk but the one the system runs from), shows what is lost and the volumes it unmounts, installs after the disk's name is typed: a new GPT on the whole disk (an EFI system partition of 64 MiB with FAT32 'EFI', a RitterFS 'RitterOS' on the rest, new random GUIDs), the bootloader for this machine's architecture at the fallback path, `cmdline.txt` without test options (only `noruntime` and `video` stay), `/system/<arch>` without its test programs and `/system/config/README`; no users, settings or homes: the new system's first start asks for root's password. The writers of `disks` and `format` moved into the programs' library (`gpt.h`, `mkfs.h`), so osinstall shares their code. Test: `osinstall` and `osinstalled` (x86 onto SATA like the J1900's SSD, ARM onto NVMe like the laptop's), then a boot from that disk alone |
| 48b | The system partition on the disk the firmware started from: the kernel searches the disks on the boot controller first and waits for a late USB stick (at most 10 s) before it looks at the other disks. The laptop (its boot log after 48a: booted from `USB(0x4,0x0)`, the stick's EFI partition) had an older RitterOS on its NVMe drive, put there as a copy of a stick image, so with the stick's GPT GUIDs; its USB 3 stick comes up late, the NVMe was searched first, and its copy became `/` (root's password then went there too). Now the copy is reported ("has the same GPT GUID as usbdisk0p2 (a copied disk?); using usbdisk0p2") and only mounted as a volume. `osinstall` gives new GUIDs, so its disks never clash. Test: `usblate` with an NVMe drive holding a copy of the stick; the old kernel took the copy |
| 48c | `osinstall` writes a firmware boot entry `RitterOS` (UEFI's `Boot####`, first in `BootOrder`; an earlier `RitterOS` entry in the order is reused): the J1900's firmware (AMI, 2019) did not offer its SATA SSD with only the fallback path, also with CSM and the storage OpROM set to UEFI (the user, after 48b). The entry is the short form Windows writes: `HD(1,GPT,<the EFI partition's GUID>,start,size)/\\EFI\\BOOT\\BOOTX64.EFI`. The new system call `efivar` gets and sets firmware variables (root only) through the runtime services, saving the program's FP/SIMD registers around the firmware call. Test: `osinstall` with a copy of the firmware's variables; the second boot has no boot order from QEMU, and the test program `efivar` on the installed disk finds `BootCurrent` = the entry. Proven: with a wrong GUID in the entry the disk did not start |
| 49a | Security audit (the user's overnight task): two code reviews (local privilege, remote network), a host fuzzer of the disk parsers (fat.c, exfat.c, gpt.c) under AddressSanitizer and UBSan (millions of rounds, no crash), and a non-destructive check of the live J1900. Fixed: a user could signal root's processes (and so reach the passwordless rescue console) or bind ports below 1024, and `cp -r` of a readable directory gave away root-only files inside it (now checked per file); a remote SYN with MSS 0 hung all networking and could divide by zero (MSS clamped); a spoofed martian source made `dnsd` loop forever (the kernel drops martian sources; dnsd ignores responses); dnsd answers are validated and ephemeral ports and the IP id randomized; a SYN flood filled the TCP table forever (dead half-open connections freed). Open items and design questions are in `docs/security-audit.md`. Test: `security` (malformed packets must not hang or crash the machine) and the privilege checks in `perm` |
| 49b | Audit open item: the file-descriptor table, shared by the threads of one program, had no lock. A per-process lock (`p->fds`) makes claiming, installing and closing a slot atomic (a claimed slot is reserved while open()'s file-system work runs without the lock), so two threads cannot collide on a descriptor or double-close one; a blocking socket call holds a reference across itself, so a socket closed by another thread is not freed under it. Test: `threads fds` (concurrent opens and sockets never cross their slots; a socket closed under a blocked recvfrom survives) |
| 49c | Audit open item: a reply built while delivering a received frame (a TCP ACK/RST, an ICMP reply) waited up to 1.5 s for ARP in the card's receive thread, blocking it and holding the TCP mutex. The kernel now learns an on-link sender's MAC from the Ethernet frame of each IP packet (so a reply to it needs no ARP), and a thread delivering a received frame queues a still-unresolved reply (as forwarding does) instead of waiting. Test (`security`): a ping from an on-link source we never answer ARP for is answered at once |
| 49d | Audit open item: any allowed packet opened a firewall flow and the oldest was evicted, so a flood from many sources could evict the machine's own outbound flows and drop the answers to what it asked. A TCP flow now opens only on a SYN; eviction prefers inbound-opened flows, and inbound flows never take more than `FLOWS - OUT_RESERVE` slots, so a flood cannot evict outbound flows. Test (`security`): host A floods the flow table between the guest's UDP request and the reply; the reply still gets back |
| 49e | Audit open items: FIN_WAIT_2 had no timeout (a peer that never sent its FIN kept the slot) — a connection in FIN_WAIT_2 is now freed after `FIN_WAIT2_MS` (5 s); and RFC 5961 checks — a RST resets only on the exact sequence number (in-window gets a challenge ACK), and a segment whose ACK is unacceptable gets a challenge ACK and is dropped, so a blind segment cannot move the window or inject data on a guessed sequence alone. Tests (`security`): a bad-ACK data injection is rejected; a FIN_WAIT_2 connection is freed after the timeout |
| 49f | Audit open item: a SYN made the listener allocate a connection (two 64 KiB buffers, a table slot) before the handshake finished, so a SYN flood filled the 32-slot table. True SYN cookies (the user's choice): a listener keeps no state for a SYN, answering a SYN-ACK whose sequence number is a cookie (the peer's MSS from a small table plus a keyed hash of the four-tuple and a coarse time), and builds the connection only when a valid ACK returns (backlog full then: a reset, not an orphan). Test (`security`): 40 SYNs with no ACK leave no half-open connections (they fill the table without cookies) |
| 49g | ARP hardening the user asked for: sticky entries (a fresh ARP entry is not overwritten by a different MAC, whether from an ARP reply, a gratuitous ARP, or a passively learned frame, so a flood cannot hijack a neighbour like the gateway) and anti-smurf (an echo request to a broadcast or multicast address is not answered). Tests (`security`): a spoofed gratuitous ARP does not replace the gateway's MAC; a ping to the broadcast address is not answered. (Pinned/static entries left for the console's system-wide security.) |
| 49h | The firewall's forward chain (the user's, completing item 12): forwarding was already switchable off (`net forward`, default off), but forwarded packets bypassed the firewall. Now a packet passing between two cards is checked against `forward` rules (`allow\|drop forward [proto] [port] [from] [to] [in ethN] [out ethN]`), with its own default `forward = drop`; a forwarded connection opens a flow matched both ways, so the answer passes without a mirror rule. Test (`route`): with `allow forward icmp` the routing works and the rule's counter shows it decided the packets; without a forward rule nothing is forwarded |
| 49i | Masquerade (source NAT, the user's; a further step in a complete IPv4): `/system/config/routing` `masquerade = ethN ...` marks the private card(s) (not the public ones, so a host with two public uplinks and one private net is handled); `net masquerade [ethN ... \| off]` shows or sets them. A packet forwarded from a private card out a card that is not private gets its source rewritten to that outgoing card's address (TCP/UDP a fresh port, ICMP echo a fresh identifier), kept in a NAT table so the reply is rewritten back and forwarded in. IPv4 only. Test (`route`): with eth0 private, host B sees A's ping from the router's address, not A's, and the reply still reaches A |
| 50 | USB Ethernet (the user's, for a USB network adapter later): CDC-NCM, the class modern adapters use (frames carried in NCM transfer blocks, NTB, on the bulk endpoints; not the older ECM or RNDIS). `kernel/usbnet.c` makes such a device an ordinary card (`eth0`...) through `net.c`, so IPv4, DHCP, the firewall and everything else work on it as on the PCI cards; `kernel/usb.c` detects the NCM interfaces and reads the MAC from the Ethernet functional descriptor; the receive thread polls the bulk IN (the controller keeps no bulk IN pending, so an idle adapter answers with a zero-length packet). QEMU has no CDC-NCM device, so the test uses our own model, `scripts/qemu/usb_ncm.c` (`usb-ncm`, built into QEMU by `setup-toolchain.sh`). Test (`usbnet`): the adapter is hot-plugged after the boot (OVMF's own NCM driver hangs binding it at boot), the kernel makes it `eth0`, DHCP gives it 10.0.2.15 on QEMU's user network, and it pings the gateway. Tried on real hardware later |

The top resize strip no longer eats the bar: the top edge resizes only within `BORDER` (4 px) of the very top, so the rest of the title bar drags (the left/right/bottom edges keep the 8 px grab margin). Content sits 6 px lower, so the GUI tests' window-content, menu-bar and dropdown coordinates moved with it (and the boxes are wider, so owl's `+` click moved). All GUI tests pass on both architectures. |

A new `check_screen` mode `notpx-X-Y-R-G-B` (the pixel is not that colour) backs the test. The `+ - X` boxes stay visible while maximized; dragging or edge-resizing a maximized window clears the maximized state. | 19c | `owl` uses the menu system (roadmap item 19). `owl` declares a "Files" menu bar (Open, Up, Refresh, Quit) with `window_set_menu`, and on a right-click an entry it pops up a context menu (Open, Up) with `window_popup_menu`; it acts on the `WINDOW_MENU` ids (open the selection, go up, re-read, quit). | 19a/19b | a menu system -- menu bars and context menus (roadmap item 19, the user's). A program declares its menu through `libwindow`: `window_set_menu(menus, n)` gives a menu bar (each menu a title and a list of `{id, label}` items), `window_popup_menu(x, y, items, n)` a context menu at a point; both serialize to a blob (`WMSG_SET_MENU` / `WMSG_POPUP`). `windowd` stores the bar per window and draws it below the title bar -- the content shifts down by the bar's height -- and a click on a title opens a dropdown; choosing an item sends the client `WMSG_MENU` with the id, surfaced as a `WINDOW_MENU` event. Context menus reuse the dropdown drawing. No malloc (fixed 6x16 menu tables each side); the window geometry (`rect_window`, `content_top`, the pointer-to-content map, the resize math) accounts for the menu bar. Test (`wmenu`, both architectures): a client's File menu (Open=1, Quit=2) and right-click context menu (Copy=3, Paste=4) -- opening File and choosing Open prints `menu 1`, right-clicking and choosing Copy prints `menu 3`; proven by not delivering the chosen item. The interaction tests (drag, focus, minimize, resize, close) still pass after the input refactor. |
| 18b | minimizing windows (roadmap item 18, the user's). The title bar gets a `-` box left of the `X`; clicking it sets the window `minimized`, so `windowd` stops drawing it and instead shows a button with its title at the bottom of the screen (`taskbar_button`/`taskbar_hit`, 160 px wide, 20 px tall). Clicking that button restores the window (un-minimized, raised to the front) to its size and place. Minimized windows are skipped by `window_at` and `focused_window` (off the screen) and by the redraw, and their damage is ignored; a minimized window that closes clears its button. Test (`wmin`, both architectures): clicking a window's `-` (pointer over QMP) hides it -- the desktop shows through and its button appears at the bottom -- and clicking the button brings it back; proven by not clicking the `-`. |

The compositor tests that drive a terminal set `startup = term` explicitly (a new `windows_startup` test helper), and the Ctrl-Alt-Delete test now sees `owl: started`. |
| 17b | `owl`, a graphical file browser (roadmap item 17, the user's name for a keen-sighted animal; `user/apps/owl.c`). It opens a window with `libwindow` and lists a directory: directories first and marked (green), then files with their size, the path at the top (yellow); a selection bar (blue) follows the arrow keys or a click. Opening by the user's rule, told apart by where a file lives: a directory is entered; an app under `/system/<arch>/apps` is run directly (`ritteros_spawn`); any other executable opens in a terminal (`term PATH`); a data file opens in the editor (`term edit PATH`). No malloc (a fixed 512-entry list).
