| 17a | `term` runs a given command, not only the shell (roadmap item 17, for the file browser to open a program): `term CMD ARGS...` runs that command on the pseudo-terminal (resolved like the shell does it -- a path as given, else `/system/<arch>/bin` then `apps`), with the command's name as the window title. Because it was given a command, `term` keeps its window open after the command exits, showing an `[CMD exited (N) - close this window]` line so the output can be read, until the user closes the window; plain `term` (the session terminal) still exits when the shell exits, as before. Test (`termcmd`, both architectures): `term echo RitterOS` renders echo's output and the exit line and the window stays open (proven by making term exit at the command's exit). |
| 16 | windowed apps live in `/system/<arch>/apps` (roadmap item 16, the user's, before a graphical file manager). Command-line programs stay in `/system/<arch>/bin`; windowed apps get a sibling `apps/` directory, and the directory is how the system tells the two apart (a future file manager will run an app in `apps/` directly, but open a `term` to run a program from `bin/`). `user/apps/*.c` build into `/system/<arch>/apps/*` (the Makefile), the shell looks up a bare program name in `bin` then `apps` (and `help` lists both), and `windowd` resolves its `startup` program (and so a double-clicked app later) in `apps` then `bin`. The window server `windowd` and the elves stay in `bin` (they are not windowed apps); for now only `term` moves to `apps`. `osinstall` copies `apps/` too. No new program or command. Tests updated (the term path, the program counts, the installed-system layout); `make test`: 3050 checks on x86 and ARM. Next: the graphical file manager, which lives in `apps/`. |
| 15k | a USB tablet (absolute-pointer) driver (roadmap item 15, the user's, after the mouse did nothing in UTM). UTM and QEMU use an absolute pointing device (a "tablet") so the host and guest cursors stay aligned without capturing the mouse; our boot-protocol mouse driver (relative) could not read it, so `windowd` got no pointer. `kernel/usbtablet.c` attaches a generic HID interface (class 03, subclass 0, protocol 0) only after reading its HID report descriptor and confirming it is an absolute X/Y pointer -- a small report-descriptor parser finds the bit offsets of the buttons, the absolute X and Y (with their logical maximum) and the wheel, so it works for QEMU's tablet and similar devices. Reports feed the same `mouse0`/`winput` path through a new `mouse_absolute` (scales the device's range to the screen). A HID interface that is not an absolute pointer is left unbound. `make run` now attaches a `usb-tablet` so the GUI can be used with the mouse on the Mac. Test (`tablet`, both architectures): the kernel attaches the tablet as `mouse0`, and an absolute move and click driven over QMP (`abs`/`btn`) are shown by the `mouse` rescue command; proven by the driver rejecting the descriptor (then the pointer does not move). Real hardware still uses its relative USB mouse (`usbmouse.c`), unchanged. |
| 15l | each user's own settings and a chosen interface (the user's; the names theirs: `~/.config`, `~/.config/login`, `sh -l`, `~/.config/windows`): `login` makes `~/.config` (700, with a README) at a console login when it is missing (root's at the first start, a new user's at their first login, older users' too) and starts the shell as `sh -l`, which runs the lines of `~/.config/login` before its first prompt (blank lines and `#` comments skipped; a file over 4,095 bytes is refused whole, never cut); SSH logins start plain `sh`. `windowd` reads `~/.config/windows` before `/system/config/windows`. So `windowd` in the login file makes the desktop a user's interface, with the shell as the fallback when it ends. Test `userconf` (both architectures): both `.config` folders made, anna's login file and windows file make her second console login come up as the desktop with her picture open; proven by a shell ignoring `-l` and a windowd reading only the system's file. |
| 50a | `grep` (the user's: new console commands; regular expressions, the user's choice): `grep [-ivnclr] PATTERN [FILE...]`, basic regular expressions of our own (`.` `*` `^` `$` `[set]` `[^set]` `\\`; a small backtracking matcher), files or standard input, -r through folders, binary files reported, exit 0/1/2 as on Unix, output through a buffer of its own. Checked on the Mac first against `/usr/bin/grep` (28 cases identical, under ASan/UBSan). Test `grep` (both architectures): 20 commands compared with the Mac's grep run on the same files at test time, -r sorted, a binary file; proven by a `*` that needs at least one. |

A process has a real user (`ruid`, who started it) besides the one whose rights apply; `load()` gives a setuid program its file's owner, only on the system's own file system (a stick or a snapshot could hold a root-owned one: Unix's nosuid), `getuid()` is the real user and the new `geteuid()` (SYS_GETEUID 73; the user's name) the effective one; `SPAWN_AS` and root's `setuid` set both; `chown` takes the bit away. `ritterfs.format`/`install` take `modes` (mkdisk's `SETUID_PROGRAMS`), `osinstall` gives the copied programs their bit back (a copy keeps rwx only, as cp). `id` shows `euid=` when it differs. Test `setuid` (both architectures): root's 4755 copy of id shows rws and runs as root for anna (`uid=1000(anna) euid=0(root)`), not from a snapshot, chown clears the bit, anna cannot chmod root's file; proven three times (a kernel ignoring the bit, one honouring it anywhere, a chown keeping it). |
| 50c | `su` and `sudo` (the user's: both; sudo's users in `/system/config/sudo`): setuid root programs (50b; mkdisk marks them 4755). `su [USER]` asks USER's password (root: none asked) and starts their shell in a session of its own in the current folder (as login does, so it can run jobs), `exit` returns; `sudo COMMAND...` checks the caller is in `users = ...` of `/system/config/sudo` (else refused without a password asked), asks the caller's own password every time, runs the command as root in sudo's group and ends with its exit code; both use `password_check` and log every attempt to `login`. Test `sudo` (both architectures): sudo as anna with her password (root) and a wrong one, `su bob` and back, `su` with a wrong password, bob refused unasked; proven by a sudo without the list and a su without the password check. |
| 51a | pipelines and lists in the shell (roadmap item 28, phase 1; the user's choices: the name `SPAWN_JOIN_GROUP`, "NAME: exit code N" only at the end of a line typed at the prompt, open files shared between processes now): `|`, `&&`, `||`, `!`, `$?`, `2>`, `2>&1`, `>&2` and any `N>`/`N<`/`N>&M` for 0-2. The shell has a tokenizer and a parser for POSIX's grammar (lists, and-or lists, pipelines, commands) and expands each command's words when it starts. A pipeline is one job: its commands joined by kernel pipes, the first leading a process group, the others joining it at spawn (`SPAWN_JOIN_GROUP`), so Ctrl-C ends them all. The kernel's open files are now shared, counted objects (POSIX's open file descriptions): a program's children share its standard input, output and error with one position, so `sh -c "echo a; echo b" > f` and `cmd > log 2>&1` keep everything in order (before, each program had its own copy and overwrote the others). A close still closes at once and wakes calls waiting on the file (descriptors and calls in progress are counted apart). Empty commands between `;` stay skipped (the user's choice; POSIX calls them a syntax error); `sh -c` (and so `ssh HOST 'cmd'`) prints no "exit code" lines. Tests: `pipeline` (new, 18 checks), and the changed expectations of `writeshell` (the syntax error's message), `wuser` and `sdk` (no report under `sh -c`) and `formatshell` (one commit at the last close: generation 2); proven by a shell that starts every command in a group of its own (Ctrl-C then leaves the second command running) and by the kernel before the change (only "three" in the file) |
| 51b | expansions in the shell (roadmap item 28, phase 1; the user's choice: split unquoted expansions as POSIX): `$(...)` and backquotes (run by another `sh -c`, as the shell has no fork: it gets the shell's own variables and parameters; trailing line breaks dropped; an assignment alone ends with the substitution's code), `$((...))` (64-bit integers, C's operators, `=` and `op=` assignments, division by 0 refused), `$$ $! $# $0 $1... $@ $*` (`"$@"` a word per parameter; `sh -c TEXT NAME ARGS`), `~` and `~user`, the patterns `?`, `[abc]`, `[a-z]` and `[!x]`, and `\` quoting one character. A typed word now becomes fields: unquoted `$VAR` and `$(...)` are split at IFS (spaces, tabs, line breaks), quoted ones stay whole; `$5` is now the fifth parameter, not the text `$5`. Tests: `expansion` (new, 16 checks), `environment` (its `$5`); proven by a shell that does not split |
| 51c | control flow and scripts in the shell, and `#!` in the kernel (roadmap item 28, phase 1; the user's choices: subshells through another `sh -c`, `exec` runs then ends the shell, `set -e -u -x -f --`, `#!` honoured by the kernel): `if`/`elif`/`else`, `while`, `until`, `for` (also over `"$@"`), `case` (patterns, `\|`), `{ ...; }`, `( ... )`, functions `NAME() { ...; }` with `return`, `break`/`continue` N levels; the built-ins `:`, `.`, `eval`, `exec`, `set`, `shift`, `read`; `#` comments; a command not finished at a line's end goes on after the prompt `> `; scripts (`sh FILE ARGS`, `~/.config/login` as a whole). The parser makes a tree, one per text (a function's body is parsed at each call). There is no `dup2` yet, so the shell keeps its own current standard input, output and error and passes them on: a compound command's or a built-in's redirections apply in the shell (`while read l; do ...; done < file` keeps its variables, `pwd > f` works). The kernel runs a file starting with `#!INTERPRETER [ARG]` through its interpreter (the script's path and arguments after it); a file without that line stays "Exec format error" (run it with `sh FILE`): files from the image are executable by default, so running every such file as a script would run `/readme.txt` too. Tests: `control` (new, 28 checks); proven by a kernel without `#!` (env cannot start the script) |
| 51d | small programs for scripts (roadmap item 28, phase 1; POSIX's options plus common extras, the user's choice): `true`, `test` and `[` (strings, numbers, files, `! -a -o ( )`; extras `<` `>` `-nt` `-ot`), `printf` (every POSIX conversion and escape, `%b`, the format used again), `sleep` (fractions and `s m h d` as extras), `date [-u] [-d @SECONDS] [+FORMAT]` (display only), `basename`, `dirname`. `[` is `test` installed under a second name. Tests: `commands` (new, 11 checks): every line as the Mac's own `/bin/test`, `/bin/[`, `/usr/bin/printf`, `basename`, `dirname`, `date -u -r`; proven by a `test` whose `-lt` is `-le` |
| 51e | text commands (roadmap item 28, phase 1, which it completes; POSIX's options plus common extras, the user's choice): `head` and `tail` (`-n`, `-c` an extra, `tail -n +N`, `tail -f` following a file), `wc` (`-l -w -c -m`, 8 columns as BSD's), `sort` (`-n -r -u -f -b -t -k F.C,G.C` with per-key options, `-o`, `-c`; extras `-h` and `-V`; equal keys by the whole line, `-u` keeps the first), `uniq` (`-c -d -u -f -s`, `-i` an extra), `cut` (`-b -c -f -d -s`), `tr` (`-c -d -s`, ranges, escapes, classes, `[c*N]`), `tee` (`-a -i`), `xargs` (`-n -L -I -t`; exit codes 123/124/125/126/127). Two fixes found by them: `fflush(NULL)`, and so `exit`, did not flush `stdout` and `stderr` made buffered by `setvbuf` (the output was lost); a script or `sh -c` printed its background jobs' `[PID]` lines (POSIX's shells report jobs only when interactive). Checked on the Mac first (the programs built against the Mac's libc, the same lines as `/usr/bin`'s). Test `textcmds` (new, 21 checks): 18 lines of commands run in the guest and by the Mac's own tools on the same files, `tail -f`, xargs's exit codes; proven by a `sort -V` comparing numbers as text |
| 52a | `dup`, `dup2` and `pipe()` (roadmap item 28, phase 2; the names the user's: `SYS_DUP2` 79, `pipe()` the POSIX wrapper over `SYS_PIPE` 65): `dup2(fd, newfd)` makes descriptor newfd refer to fd's open file too (one position, closing what was open there; `dup2(fd, fd)` does nothing), `dup(fd)` the same at the lowest free number (SYS_DUP2 with -1); EBADF for a closed descriptor or a number out of range (16 per program), EMFILE when none is free, EBUSY for a slot another thread is opening. In `unistd.h`, recorded in libritteros.so.1's ABI. Test `dup` (new, 9 checks): a copy writes to the same place, two descriptors share one position (also after the first is closed), dup2 onto stdout and back, the errors, a pipe's end only after its last (dup'd) write end, and a pipe's read end put on 0 as a spawned cat's input; proven by a dup2 giving the copy an open file of its own |
| 52b | `open` with a mode, and `fstat` (roadmap item 28, phase 2; the user's: `open(const char *, int, ...)` variadic in libritteros.so.1, `SYS_FSTAT` 80): with `O_CREAT` open reads a third argument, the new file's mode, which the kernel takes less 022 (Unix's usual umask; there is no `umask` call yet) and marks with the new flag `OPEN_MODE`, so programs built before, which pass 0 there, still get 0644; `creat`. `fstat(fd)` gives an open file's inode as it is now (a size written but not yet committed included) and, for the others, their kind (`S_ISFIFO` pipes, `S_ISCHR` terminals and sound devices, `S_ISSOCK` sockets, new in `sys/stat.h`). Every creating `open` in the tree now passes its mode (0644, as before; the shell's redirections and `fopen` 0666, as Unix's, which the mask makes 0644). abicheck records the changed `open` as an agreed exception (`AGREED`): a program built before that creates a file reads an unknown mode. Test `fstat` (new, 5 checks): fstat against stat, the modes 0600, 0666, 0777, 0 and `creat`'s, an existing file keeping its mode, a pipe's ends, the terminal; proven by a kernel ignoring the mode. `write`, `users`, `libc`, `fat_write` and `sdk` pass on both |
| 52c | `/dev/null`, `/dev/zero` and `/dev/tty` (roadmap item 28, phase 2; the names agreed with the user): open for everyone, read and written as on Unix (null: the end at once, writes taken and gone; zero: zero bytes as many as asked; seeking either stays at 0), `stat` and `fstat` say character devices of mode 0666. `/dev/tty` opens the caller's own terminal: its pseudo-terminal (a slave end of its own), or the console when that is its terminal, else ENXIO (new: `ERR_NXIO` 6, "Device not configured" as BSD's) -- so an `ssh HOST CMD` command or an elf has none. Like `/dev/pcmN` they are not listed by `ls /dev` (only disks are). Test `devnull` (new, 7 checks): `>` and `>>` into /dev/null, reading it, 5,000 zero bytes and zeros through `tr`, `/dev/tty` reached by an `sh -c` whose output went to /dev/null, `test -e` and `-f`; the fstat test checks their kinds. Proven by a kernel without the names (every check fails) and one whose /dev/tty always says ENXIO |
| 52d | `poll` (roadmap item 28, phase 2; the name agreed: `SYS_POLL` 81; `poll.h` with `struct pollfd` and Linux's numbers for POLLIN, POLLOUT, POLLERR, POLLHUP, POLLNVAL): how many of up to 64 descriptors have something to report, waiting up to a timeout (-1 none, 0 not at all; EINTR on a signal). A wait queue can be marked `pollable`: a wake-up there also counts an event and wakes every thread in poll, which looks at its descriptors again (a count taken before looking, so nothing between the look and the sleep is missed); marked are the pipes', the pseudo-terminals', the terminal's input, the sockets' (UDP, every TCP slot, local streams and listeners). Each kind says what a read or write would find (`pipe_poll`, `pty_poll`, `tcp_poll`, `local_poll`, `socket_poll`, `terminal_input_waiting`: a typed byte; a line read still waits for its Enter); files and devices never wait. Test `poll` (new, 12 checks): pipes (nothing, which of two, a 300 ms timeout, a wake-up by another thread, POLLHUP and POLLERR at their ends), POLLNVAL and a skipped descriptor, a file, a pty, a UDP socket on 127.0.0.1, a local listener and connection; proven by a kernel whose queues do not wake poll |
| 52e | termios (roadmap item 28, phase 2; the name agreed: `SYS_TERMIOS` 82, one call for get, set, set-and-flush and flush): `termios.h` with POSIX's `tcgetattr`, `tcsetattr` (TCSANOW, TCSADRAIN, TCSAFLUSH), `tcflush`, `tcdrain`, `cfmakeraw` and the speed calls, Linux's flag numbers, speeds as plain numbers (BSD's). Changing is the terminal's foreground job's (EPERM otherwise), a descriptor that is no terminal is ENOTTY (new: `ERR_NOTTY` 25); the process that set the modes gets them back to the start when it ends (as the raw switch of 22a). The old raw switch (`edit`, the shell's line editor) keeps one byte or more and Enter as typed. | 55c | `posix_spawn` and `posix_spawnp` (roadmap item 28, phase 4; the user's: file actions only for descriptors 0-2 until fork), in the library over the kernel's spawn: `spawn.h` with file actions (open, dup2, close; one leaving the child a descriptor past 2, or one of 0-2 closed, is ENOTSUP) and attributes (SETPGROUP, SETSIGMASK, SETSIGDEF, RESETIDS); `posix_spawnp` finds a name as `execvp` does (and runs a script without `#!` through sh). Tests: `spawn` (new: spawntest's 20 lines identical to the Mac's own posix_spawn, 5 lines of RitterOS's own); proven by a library that ignored the file actions. exec and sdk pass on both |
| 56a | `fork` with copy-on-write (roadmap item 28, phase 4; the user's order and name SYS_FORK 93): the child is a copy of the calling process with one thread (the caller's, under the same number), the same open files, folder, umask, users, group, session, handlers, ignored and blocked signals, no pending ones. Its memory is shared until written: the program's own pages are held by both and copied at the first write (a child of a program with 8 MiB written that writes one page itself cost 65 pages, not 2,048); `MAP_SHARED` anonymous memory stays one memory. The library: `fork`, `pthread_atfork`, `_exit`. Tests: `fork` (new: forktest's 14 lines identical to the Mac's fork, 3 of RitterOS's own: the cost, no page left after 10 children, only the calling thread); proven by a kernel without copy-on-write (the child wrote over the parent's stack). mmap, signals and sdk pass on both |
| 56b | posix_spawn by fork and exec (the user's choice, as POSIX): the child has every descriptor not marked FD_CLOEXEC and the ignored signals, the file actions work on any descriptor, a failed action or exec comes back as posix_spawn's error through a pipe; and `getppid` (SYS_GETPPID 94), `setpgid`/`getpgid` (SYS_SETPGID 99) and `setsid` (SYS_SETSID 100), the user's names. Tests: `spawn` (now 28 lines identical to the Mac's: descriptor 5, a closed stdin, inherited and FD_CLOEXEC descriptors, an ignored signal, another child's group, getppid), `fork` (19 lines: getppid, setpgid, getpgid, setsid, EACCES after exec); proven by a library whose child skipped the file actions and a kernel whose setpgid changed nothing. sdk passes on both |
| 56c | the shell on fork (the user's choice): `( ... )`, `$(...)`, built-ins, functions and compound commands in a pipeline and `a && b &` run in a fork of the shell instead of another `sh -c`; `$$` there is the shell's own number (POSIX), the shell's variables, functions, options and parameters are in the child's memory (nothing sent as text), and the child keeps no descriptor but its three. External commands still start with spawn. Tests: `subshell` (new, 10 lines: `$$`, a subshell's changes, a function in `$(...)`, `(exit 3)`, read in a pipeline, a loop in a pipeline, a subshell reading a pipe to its end, nested `$(...)`, `a && b &`, `$(...)` in a loop); proven by a subshell whose `$$` was its own. pipeline, expansion, control, commands, textcmds, environment, exec, sshexec and sdk pass on both |
| 57a | symbolic and hard links in RitterFS (the user's names: SYS_SYMLINK 95, SYS_READLINK 96, SYS_LSTAT 97, SYS_LINK 98, `ln [-s] [-f]`, `readlink [-f]`): a new object type 4 holding the path it names (no new format version, as with setuid: the bootloaders on installed machines would refuse one), hard links on the existing link count (a folder keeps one name); every path is resolved component by component with links spliced in (40 at most: ELOOP), `..` after a link leaving its target's folder (POSIX), and every folder passed must allow searching. `ls` shows `<link>  name -> target`, `ls -l` an `l` and the link count; `test -h -L -c -p -S`; `rm -r` and copies never walk into a link; both checkers and `ritterfs.py` know links. Tests: `links` (new, 24 checks: relative, absolute, folder links, `..`, a loop, a dangling link, rm, hard links, as anna a link out of a 0700 folder refused, the host check clean); proven by a resolver that skipped the search right (anna read through it). The `ls -l` lines of login, disks, fat, exfat, sudo, setuid, hdamulti, umask, users and write follow the new column; fat no longer expects the other architecture's bootloader on its disk (missed in 55a). ldso, mmap, exec, spawn, fork, sshexec, sdk and control pass on both |
| 57b | the folders of a disk (the user's choice): `/bin` and `/usr/bin` link to `/system/<arch>/bin`, `/lib` to `/system/<arch>/lib`, `/etc` to `/system/config`, and `/tmp` is everyone's with the sticky bit (mode 1777: a new bit 01000 in the format, only an entry's owner, the folder's owner or root removes or renames it); made by mkdisk (new and kept images) and osinstall. `ls -l` shows `t`, `chmod 1777`, `test -k`. Tests: `links` (now 32: `#!/bin/sh`, `/usr/bin/env`, `/etc`, `/tmp`'s mode, anna removing her own file there but not root's, nor renaming it), proven by a kernel without the sticky check; osinstall expects the new folders on the installed disk. write, config, osinstall and sdk pass on both |
| 58a | `regex.h` in the C library (the user's choice): `regcomp`, `regexec`, `regerror`, `regfree`, POSIX's basic and extended syntax with groups, `{m,n}`, classes, back references, `REG_ICASE`, `REG_NEWLINE`, `REG_NOTBOL`/`NOTEOL`, `REG_NOSUB`; the leftmost-longest match in time proportional to text times pattern (a Thompson simulation, then a remembering backtracking pass for the groups). `grep` uses it and has `-E`. Tests: `regex` (new: regextest's 73 lines identical to the Mac's regex; on the Mac first, 30,000 random cases, the 41 where the Mac disagreed judged by brute force: ours right in all), `grep` (now 31: eight `-E` and class cases against the Mac's grep); proven by a regexec that kept the first match's end. links and sdk pass on both |
| 58b | `sed`, POSIX's stream editor (the user's: plus `-E` and `-i`): addresses (numbers, `$`, `/RE/`, `\cREc`, ranges, `,+N`, `!`), `s` with `g`, `N`, `p`, `w`, `&` and groups, `y d D p P n N q = l a i c r w b t :`, the hold space, groups `{ }`, `-n -e -f`; `-i[SUFFIX]` rewrites each file in place (mode and hard links kept). Tests: `sed` (new, 52 checks: 49 commands against the Mac's /usr/bin/sed on the same files, -i, -i.bak, a hard link seeing the edit); checked on the Mac first (51 cases); proven by a sed whose `s///g` replaced only the first match |
| 58c | `awk`, POSIX's (the user's choice): patterns and ranges, fields (FS one character, blanks or an ERE; `-F`), records (RS one character or paragraphs), numbers, strings and number-like input, associative arrays (SUBSEP, `in`, `delete`, for-in), functions (recursion, arrays by reference, locals), every built-in function, printf, getline in its six forms, output to files and pipes, `-v`, `-f`, ARGV assignments, `next`, `nextfile`, `exit`. With it `popen`/`pclose` in the C library. Tests: `awk` (new, 161 programs against the Mac's /usr/bin/awk on the same files; on the Mac first 164); proven by an awk whose blank splitting took single blanks. sdk passes on both |
| 59 | `make`, POSIX's with the GNU make most Makefiles use (the user's choice): rules, pattern, static pattern and suffix rules, the built-in C rules, recipes with `@ - +`, every variable flavor (`= := ::= ?= += !=`, override, define, export, target-specific), the automatic variables, the common functions (`patsubst`, `wildcard`, `shell`, `call`, `eval`, `foreach`, `if`, ...), `ifeq`/`ifdef`, `include`/`-include`, `-n -s -k -i -q -B -t -e -C -f`, recursion with `$(MAKE)`. With it `touch` sets an existing file's time to now and `cp` writes over an existing file (POSIX's). Tests: `make` (new, 62 steps of 38 Makefiles against the Mac's GNU make; on the Mac also `make -n` of the SDK template for every target of sdk/ritteros.mk: the same lines); proven by a make that never found a target older than its prerequisites. write, fat, sed and links pass on both |
| 60 | Our own C compiler (the user's: written by us, no port): `cc`, `as` and `ld` for x86-64 and AArch64, first goal RitterOS's own programs built on RitterOS. cc: C11 with the GNU additions the system's sources use; a preprocessor (its output token for token clang's on every user/bin file), a parser with C's types and the ABIs' layouts (25,237 size and offset checks judged by clang), a small intermediate code, and a backend per architecture (System V and AAPCS64 calls, structures by value, varargs, long double through x87 or binary128 helpers; every value in a stack slot: no optimization yet). as: GNU syntax into ELF objects, byte for byte clang's on every user/bin file. ld: objects, archives and shared libraries into the system's kind of program (position-independent, ld.so, RELRO, --as-needed). On the disk: /system/include (the C library's headers and cc's own stddef.h, stdint.h, stdarg.h, ...), /system/<arch>/lib/start.o and libritteros.a; osinstall copies them. All 81 programs of user/bin built with it (on the Mac) passed the scoped tests of those programs (x86-64 925 of 925 checks; AArch64 924, the one left osinstall's listing of /system, which this step's new folder had reached mid-run); on RitterOS cc built by itself builds user/bin byte for byte as the Mac's build of cc does. Not yet: optimization, _Complex, __int128, per-thread variables, inline assembly. Tests: `cc` (new: cctest's 69 lines as clang's build prints them, wc, -c/-S/-E, a message and exit code 1, cc building cc, as and ld, and that toolchain building cctest again); proven by a cc that subtracts where it should add. owl, osinstall and programs pass on both |
| 61a | cc keeps values in registers (the user's: optimization next): liveness over the code's blocks (a value live across a loop's back edge is known), linear-scan allocation into the registers a call keeps (x86-64 rbx, r12-r15; AArch64 x20-x28, d8-d15), scalar locals and parameters whose address is never taken promoted to values (volatile ones stay in memory); `-O0` keeps everything on the stack. A benchmark on RitterOS (ARM: a sieve, an insertion sort, string hashing): clang -O2 0.80 s, cc -O0 4.12 s, cc 1.16 s. All of user/bin built with it passed the scoped program tests (504 checks on each architecture) and cc built itself on RitterOS; the shell's object went from 327 to 195 KB. Tests: `cc` (its self-build names cc's parts with `*`: regalloc.c is new) passes on both |
| 61b | cc says more inside each instruction (both architectures): constants as immediates (x86-64 32-bit ones in arithmetic, compares and stores; AArch64 add/sub 0-4095, logical bit patterns, shifts, cmp 0-4095, a store of 0 from wzr/xzr), a comparison and the branch on it as one compare-and-branch, frame and global addresses and constant offsets inside loads and stores (off(%rbp), sym+off(%rip); [x19, #off], adrp + :lo12:), AArch64's indexed loads and stores (`ldr w22, [x22, w21, sxtw #2]` for `g[n]`), multiplications by a power of two as shifts, results computed in their own registers; a copy right after the instruction making its value is merged into it; spilling by weight (a use in a loop counts 8 times per level). The benchmark (RitterOS): AArch64 clang -O2 0.77 s, cc 0.77 s (61a: 1.16 s); x86-64 (emulated) clang 4.12 s, cc 7.24 s. Found on the way: our `as` wrote a `sxtw` register offset as `uxtw` (wrong for negative indexes), and a folded global address took the page of `sym` for `sym+8192` (`tr`, built by cc, read past a page in the batch). All of user/bin built with it passed the scoped batch (916 checks on x86, 916 on ARM). Tests: `cc` (cctest has an addressing part now: offsets beyond a page, negative indexes, immediates, compares; proven by the page bug: `pages: 0` for 86) passes on both |
| 61c | cc improves the code before the backends see it (both architectures): constants worked out at compile time (conversions, arithmetic and comparisons of constants; additions of constants merged, a global's address plus a constant one address; x + 0 and x * 1 copies; constants second where the order does not matter), instructions whose results nobody uses dropped, no zeros before a scalar's initializer, an extension of what a load extended already left out (and a load whose only use extends the other way does that itself), a copy whose only use follows in its block replaced by its source (`i++` needs no copy), and an assignment's right side first when it is a constant or a variable in a register or neither side has effects (C leaves the order open; the address then folds into the store). x86-64: values also live in r8, r9, rsi and rdi wherever no call, memcpy, memset or atomic lies inside their lifetime (the allocator's `clobbers`), indexed addresses (`movl 0(%r13,%rdi,4), %edi`), `imul $c, src, dst` (new in our `as`), conversions in their own registers, `test` for zero; both: a comparison with the constant first is turned round. The benchmark: AArch64 clang -O2 0.71 s, cc 0.76 s; x86-64 (emulated) clang 4.14 s, cc 5.12 s (61b: 7.24 s). A copy was merged into an instruction outside its loop (the coalescing of 61b, exposed by the forwarding: in awk, `for (r = repl; ...)` inside a `while` set r only once, so gsub stopped after its first match and windowd drew a black screen); merging now stays inside one block. All of user/bin built with it passed the scoped batch (916 checks on x86, 916 on ARM); its objects went from 1.90 to 1.62 MB (x86) and 2.01 to 1.90 MB (ARM). Tests: `cc` (cctest has a folding part: constant edges, merges, values kept across calls, memcpy, memset and atomics, which fail with the clobber mask off, and a copy inside a loop, 13 instead of 39 with the old merge) passes on both |
| 61d | smaller stack frames in cc (the user's, after 62f: our kernel needed 14.8 KiB of stack where clang's needs 9.8): AArch64 values also live in x2-x7 wherever no call, helper call, TLS access or return comes in between (as x86-64's r8, r9, rsi, rdi since 61c), the AArch64 frame header is 16 bytes (x19 is saved in the frame with the other registers; the save area holds only the registers used, since 62f), locals promoted to values get no frame space, and locals of blocks that never overlap (a switch's arms) share it, as clang's stack colouring (statement expressions' locals take the block around them: their result may point into them). The kernel's deepest stack use (stacks filled with a pattern, the deepest point found at each switch): 12.4 KiB, from 14.8 (clang: 9.8 KiB; the rest is mostly functions clang inlines); all frames of the ARM kernel 187 KB (clang 168, before 327). Found by its tests: our ARM kernel answered pings in up to 20 ms where clang's needs 0.2 (igb, realtek), with or without the new registers; a bisection over the kernel's files (each round with half of them from clang) named kernel/pmm.c: the search for a run of pages (a new thread's stack, large buffers) went frame by frame from frame 0 with interrupts off, a call per frame in our code (clang inlines it), over 1.3 million frames on the ARM test machine. It now passes a word of 64 used frames at once (the same pages found, for both compilers). Tests: 96 scoped tests on x86 and 92 on ARM with everything built by our toolchain (1,597 and 1,556 checks; the two ping failures fixed as above), then the allocator's (kernel tests, programs with the leak check, mmap, igb, realtek) on both architectures with both toolchains, and cc on the default build. Not yet: inlining |
| 61e | inlining in cc (the user's, after 63b): a call to a static function of the same file is replaced by its body when the body is small (at most 40 tree nodes) or the function is called only once (at most 600; then its own copy is not written unless some call of it was not inlined), it takes and returns scalars, has no labels, VLAs, va_list or alloca, keeps at most 128 bytes of locals in memory (a packet buffer inlined into arp_learn put 1.5 KiB on every path through it: the deepest stack grew to 17.6 KiB until this rule), and is not the caller itself; four levels deep at most; `__attribute__((noinline))` is respected. Parameters and locals get copies in the caller's frame, placed at the call's position in the blocks, so copies of different calls share room. The ARM kernel makes 7,262 calls where it made 9,006; its deepest stack use stays about where it was (12.7 KiB; clang: 9.8 KiB), as the rest is in frames clang has nearly as large: kernel stacks stay 32 KiB. cctest has an inlining section (parameters changed in the callee, returns from loops, address-taken locals, nested and repeated calls, narrow results, pointers, static locals, recursion, noinline): proven by an inliner giving every parameter the first argument. Tests: the 61d batch and selfbuild and links on both architectures with everything built by our toolchain, cc on the default build |
| 62a | cc has `__int128` and `_Complex` (the self-hosting track, item 30: the C library's sae.c, math.c and complex.c need them). Both are kept in memory and passed as their ABIs say (SysV: two INTEGER eightbytes, SSE parts; AAPCS64: an even register pair, HFAs; x86's long double _Complex comes back in st0/st1); 128-bit arithmetic on halves (a new IR operation for a product's high half: `mulq`, `umulh`, which our `as` learned), division and float conversions through compiler-rt's names, which the C library now has (`user/lib/int128.c`, static-only, checked on the Mac against its compiler-rt on 2 million cases); complex `+ -` by parts, `* /` through the library's `__muldc3` and friends, real operands part by part as clang does; imaginary constants (`1.0i`), `__real__`/`__imag__` (lvalues), `__builtin_complex`, constants of both in static initializers; `__builtin_sqrt`/`fabs` as instructions (math.c defines `sqrt` with it). Found on the way, both since step 60 or older: a file-scope `long x = -5;` was written as 0x00000000fffffffb (a conditional of an unsigned and a signed arm converts both to unsigned), and math.c's `fma` shifted a 128-bit value by 128 or more for products far below the subnormals (undefined; clang's and our builds gave different wrong results, now 0). Checks: mathtest with our math.c, wpacrypto with our sae.c, complextest with our complex.c, each built by cc into clang's test programs, print what clang's builds print on both architectures; cctest grew to 168 lines (both levels, both architectures). Tests: `cc`, `math`, `complex`, `sdk` pass on both |
| 62b | per-thread variables (`__thread`, `_Thread_local`) in cc, as and ld (item 30): cc writes the sequences clang writes for `-fPIC` (x86-64 general-dynamic through `__tls_get_addr`, AArch64 TLS descriptors), so lld can still relax them, and the data into `.tdata`/`.tbss`; as learned `@tlsgd`, the `data16`/`rex64` padding, `:tlsdesc:`, `:tlsdesc_lo12:` and `.tlsdesccall` (objects byte for byte what clang's assembler makes); ld lays out the template (PT_TLS, only when there is per-thread data) and fills TLS GOT pairs through ld.so (also local-dynamic's module pair for clang's objects). tlstest (initial values, four threads with their own copies, a 64-byte aligned one, errno per thread) built by our toolchain passes on both architectures, also linked by lld; every other program links byte for byte as before. Tests: `cc` (cctest's per-thread part, 169 lines) passes on both |
| 62c | inline assembly in cc (item 30): GCC's extended `asm` with outputs, in-out operands, inputs, clobbers, named operands, the constraints the tree uses (`r`, `m`, `i`, `n`, x86's `a b c d S D N`), the size modifiers and `%=`, and register variables (`register long x asm("x0")`, ARM's power.c). No value stays in a register over an asm statement (a barrier for the register allocator), so operands take any free register and callee-saved ones it uses are saved by the prologue. Our as learned stmxcsr, ldmxcsr, fnstenv, fldenv, fnclex and fclex (bytes as clang's). Checks against clang: a test of every operand form on both architectures (also values kept over an asm that overwrites rbx/r12/rsi/r8 or x20/x21/x9/x1), libctest (206 lines) with our build of fenv.c; cctest grew to 177 lines (x86) and 176 (ARM). Tests: `cc` passes on both |
| 62d | `make TOOLCHAIN=ritteros` (item 30): the Makefile builds our cc, as and ld for the Mac (build/host) and with them compiles and links all of user space for both architectures: every program, the shared libraries, the static programs and ld.so (zig stays the default; a switch rebuilds every object). ld learned lld's `-static` (no loader: addresses filled in when linking, ARM's TLS descriptors pointed at a small stub), `-shared` with `-soname` and `--version-script` (only the listed functions exported), `--no-dynamic-linker` for ld.so, `-T` for the system's two layouts, `-z defs`/`norelro`, and `_GLOBAL_OFFSET_TABLE_`, `__ehdr_start`, `_DYNAMIC`. cc learned `visibility("hidden")` (local, reached PC-relative: ld.so reads `_DYNAMIC` before anything is relocated; without it every dynamic program faulted at once), `.weak` for referenced weak declarations (written strong, ld.so pulled in pthread.c), `constructor`/`destructor` (`.init_array`/`.fini_array`, the dlopen test's library), triples in `-target`, and a compound literal whose address is taken stays in memory. as learned `%fs:`/`%gs:` operands, `mov` to and from control registers, ARM system registers by name and MSR immediates. Tests: 54 scoped tests on both architectures, every program built by our toolchain (1,090 checks each half; the one failure, the missing constructor, fixed and rerun), and the default build's `cc`, `ldso` and `dl` |
| 62e | the `.S` files through our cc and as (item 30): with `TOOLCHAIN=ritteros` the programs' start code, system call stubs, setjmp and ld.so's assembly are ours too. cc preprocesses `.S` files as clang does (`__ASSEMBLER__`, the source's spacing, `$` before a macro name); as learned macros (`.macro`/`.endm` with parameters, defaults, `\()` and `\@`), `.rept`, `.irp`, `.irpc`, numeric labels (`1:`, `1b`, `1f`) and the kernel's instructions (x86-64: cli, sti, cld, iretq, lretq, swapgs, wbinvd, rdmsr/wrmsr, pushfq/popfq, fxsave64/fxrstor64, lgdt/lidt/sgdt/sidt, ltr/str, invlpg, int, in/out, inc/dec, moves to and from segment registers; AArch64: wfe, sev, sevl, yield, smc, udf, tlbi, ic, dc). All 18 `.S` files of the tree, the kernel's included, assemble byte for byte as clang's objects; a jump to a label made global later is now long with a PLT32 relocation, as LLVM's. Tests: cc, ldso, dl, aslr, libc, signals, pthread, thrd and subshell on both architectures with everything built by our toolchain |
| 62f | the kernel built by our own toolchain (item 30): with `TOOLCHAIN=ritteros` the kernel's C, its assembly and its link are ours too. cc learned `-fno-pic` (every symbol reached directly), `-mgeneral-regs-only` (no FP/SIMD register at all; floating point is an error, the variadic prologues save no xmm/q registers), `-mcmodel=kernel`/`-mno-red-zone` (taken), calls through `ms_abi` function pointers (the kernel's UEFI runtime calls: rcx, rdx, r8, r9 and 32 bytes of shadow space) and attributes inside a declarator; ld reads linker scripts (the subset kernel/linker.ld uses: ENTRY, SECTIONS, addresses, ALIGN, NOLOAD, `. +=`, symbols, /DISCARD/) into a static executable with one segment per permission run and a symbol table. Both kernels boot (x86 1.76 s, ARM 3.42 s to "boot complete"). Found on the way: our ARM kernel overflowed its 16 KiB thread stacks on the deepest paths (USB boots, the realtek test: a spawn read a damaged process record); measured with stacks filled with a pattern: clang's kernel 9.8 KiB, ours 14.8 KiB once cc's ARM frames keep only the registers they use (the median frame 208 -> 128 bytes; clang's 48), and over 16 KiB before. Kernel stacks are now 32 KiB for every thread (the user's choice). Tests: about 55 scoped tests on each architecture with the kernel and all of user space built by our toolchain (861 checks on x86, 822 on ARM: crashes, kernel self-tests, USB/SATA, processes, memory, file systems, network, SSH, sound, GUI, Wi-Fi, osinstall, cc), and on the default build the quick boot, the program checks with their leak check, crashes, kernel tests, usbboot, realtek, mmap and cc (229 checks each) |
| 62g | the bootloader built by our own toolchain (item 30; level 1 complete): with `TOOLCHAIN=ritteros` cc compiles BOOTX64.EFI's and BOOTAA64.EFI's C as ELF objects and `ld --efi` (the user's name) writes the PE32+ image, laid out as lld-link lays out a UEFI application (ImageBase 0x140000000, .text at 0x1000, then .rdata, .data with the bss after its file bytes, .reloc; subsystem 10), with a DIR64 base relocation for every 64-bit address (our code is PC-relative, so there are none: an empty block). Only `efi_main` and the firmware's functions use the Microsoft convention (EFIAPI): cc now also defines `ms_abi` functions (arguments from rcx, rdx, r8, r9; rsi and rdi kept for the caller); every other function stays System V, as nothing but our own code calls it. Tests: a boot-oriented batch on both architectures with everything built by our toolchain (x86 168 checks: quick boot, power, no serial port, SATA boot, the crash tests with video=, kernel tests, noruntime, USB boots, osinstall, display, cc; ARM 140), and the default build's quick boot, osinstall and cc |
| 63a | level 2's tools (item 30): `ar` (the user's name; user/bin/ar.c, also built for the Mac as build/host/ar), the GNU archive format written as llvm-ar writes it (both libritteros.a archives byte for byte the same as zig's), now the archiver of `TOOLCHAIN=ritteros`; the build's Python generators rewritten in C (the user's choice): scripts/bdf2c.c (the fonts' tables), scripts/libexports.c (the shared libraries' version scripts), scripts/exfat_upcase.c (the exFAT up-case table: the Unicode Character Database's capitals, which Python's str.upper() gave until now, carried as 183 runs) and scripts/mathconst.c (the math library's constants, in our own 1600-bit fixed point: pi by Machin, ln, square roots, Bernoulli numbers, zeta and Euler's gamma by Euler-Maclaurin), built for the machine running the build. Each writes what its Python script wrote (checked on all 13 fonts, all six libraries, the table unit for unit), except one word: the last of 2/pi's 22 words was wrong in its last 30-odd bits at Python's 420 digits (checked with 700): it is right now. With `TOOLCHAIN=ritteros` the build needs neither zig nor Python (only the Mac's disk images still come from scripts/mkdisk.py). Tests: fonts, math, libc, exfat_format, sdk, ldso, dl, cc and programs on both architectures with both toolchains |
| 63b | RitterOS builds RitterOS (item 30, level 2): the test `selfbuild` copies the sources the build needs (590 files: boot, common, kernel, arch, user, scripts/*.c, two fonts, the Makefile) into /src over SSH, one file at a time with `cat > FILE` (the user's choice: no tar; 59 s on ARM), and RitterOS runs `make TOOLCHAIN=ritteros HOSTCC=cc arch-build`: its own cc builds the host toolchain (cc, as, ld, ar) and the generators from their sources, and those build the bootloader, the kernel, the libraries and every program, 76 s on ARM. The Mac builds the same copy with our make (built for the Mac) and tools, and all 185 files of build/system/aarch64 and the bootloader are byte for byte the same (proven by a line appended to true.c on the guest only: then exactly bin/true differs). On x86 (under emulation: copying 72 s, the build 280 s) five files first differed in one byte each: a NaN constant's sign, because cc folded 0.0/0.0 with the machine's own division (x86's NaN has the sign bit set, ARM's not); cc's folding now gives arithmetic's NaN (also complex constants') as the positive quiet NaN, as clang does (negation and conversions keep a NaN's sign, as there), and `__builtin_nan` (NAN) is that NaN's bits, not cc's own 0.0 / 0.0 at run time. Both architectures now build all 185 files byte for byte as the Mac does. Needed on the way: `rm -f` (POSIX: a missing file is no error; also `-R` and combined options), the archives' folder made by their rule. | 63i | here-documents in sh (the user's choice, with tar): `<<WORD` and `<<-WORD` as POSIX has them: the tokenizer reads a line's bodies after its line break (in order), up to the delimiter's line; quoted words keep the lines as they are, else they are expanded as in double quotes (a `"` itself, `\` before a line break joins), a line at a time so no buffer limits a body; the body reaches the command through a pipe (written at once up to the pipe's 64 KiB, else by a thread while the command reads). When a text ends inside a here-document, the reader gathers lines up to its delimiter before parsing again (parsing the whole text at each line took 41 s for a 20,000-line bundle, now 1 s). Found on the way: a shell reading a pipe at the console (`cat script | sh`) switched the console into its line editor and echoed the pipe's bytes as if typed (its second here-document then broke): the line editor is only for a terminal now. | 63m | real file times (the user's choice after the outside agent found that every project sent with tar was built again: RitterOS could not set a file's time). The system call `SYS_UTIMENS` (101; path or open file, a link itself or what it names, UTIMENS_NOW and UTIMENS_OMIT), and POSIX's `utimensat` and `futimens` (sys/stat.h, `UTIME_NOW`, `UTIME_OMIT`; fcntl.h's `AT_FDCWD`, `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW`), `utime` (a new `utime.h`) and `utimes` (sys/time.h): the time now needs the owner, write permission or root (EACCES), any other time the owner or root (EPERM); the access time is taken but not kept (none is kept); RitterFS keeps nanoseconds, exFAT 10 ms, FAT 2 seconds (`vfs_set_time`; FAT's and exFAT's "now" encoders split so they take any time). `tar x` restores times (folders' at the end; `m`: now, as POSIX's), `touch` is POSIX's (`-a -m -c -r -t`; it wrote a byte back to fake "now" before), `cp -p` keeps time, mode and (for root) owner, through a tree with -r. Found on the way: plain `cp` gave an instant copy its original's time (the copy shares the inode, times and all), so a source copied over could look older than its object; now a copy gets the time now, as POSIX's cp. The Mac's utimensat takes a tv_nsec of 2e9 (POSIX: EINVAL), so that case is a `libctest-own` line, not compared. | 27o | JavaScript (item 27, the user's: our own engine, a modern core first; names agreed: the program `js`, `print()` and `console.log()`, the test `js_shell_test` with its cases in `scripts/js/`): `user/lib/js/`, an interpreter in C11 of our own. The source is parsed into a tree (`parse.c`: recursive descent, arrow parameters and destructuring targets read as expressions first and turned into patterns), scopes and bindings resolved (`compile.c`: a binding no inner function uses lives in a register of its frame, captured ones in scope cells), and compiled to a stack code (`opcodes.h`) run by one loop (`interp.c`: calls between scripts stay in the loop, `finally` written out at each way out of its `try`). Values are NaN-boxed 64-bit words; strings UTF-16 with ropes for long concatenations; objects keep properties in insertion order and arrays dense while they can (`object.c`); a mark-and-sweep collector (`gc.c`) finds what C code holds by looking at the C stack. The language: ES2023 but generators, `async`, Promises (27r), modules, BigInt, Proxy, typed arrays and Intl; the standard objects Object, Function, Array, String, Number, Boolean, Symbol, Math, JSON, RegExp (a backtracking engine of our own, `regexp.c`: lookbehind, named groups, the `dgimsuy` flags), Map, Set, WeakMap, WeakSet, Date, Reflect and the Error kinds. `js FILE [ARG...]`, `js -e CODE` or a prompt `js> `. | 27r | Promises, `async` functions and generators (item 27; the web's names, nothing new to name), the first part of 27r (fetch and XMLHttpRequest follow). A generator's frame is kept in its object while it is not running (its arguments, registers, operand stack, place and open try handlers, with offsets in place of pointers) and put back on the value stack by next(), throw() or return(); a return at a yield runs the finally blocks around it (the compiler writes that path at each yield, the expression's values on the stack dropped first: each statement notes the stack's depth where it began); `yield*` passes next, throw and return on to the inner iterator and its results out as they are. An `async` function runs on the same machinery, `await` stopping it like `yield` and its reactions resuming it; Promises follow the specification's records and jobs (then, catch, finally, resolve, reject, all, allSettled, any, race, withResolvers, subclassing through the species); their jobs go on the engine's queue (27q's), which `js` now runs after a script and iBrowser after each script, listener and timer. Async generators and `for await` not yet. Checked on the Mac against JavaScriptCore: the new case files generators.js and promises.js (46 and 33 lines, among them the order of interleaved awaits and thens), the 13 earlier ones, 30,000 random expressions, all with the collector run at every allocation under ASan; test `ibrowser`: a promise and an await in a page's stages in order. Then fetch and XMLHttpRequest (27r's second part): `fetch`, `Request`, `Response`, `Headers`, `AbortController`/`AbortSignal`, `XMLHttpRequest` (asynchronous only: a synchronous one would stop the window) and `URLSearchParams`, written in JavaScript (prelude.c, run in each page's world) over two natives in dom.c: `start` checks a request (http and https only, no http from an https page, no `file:`, forbidden headers dropped) and a thread of its own makes it (`dom_perform`: a CORS preflight when the request is more than a simple one, the request one redirection at a time with cookies only for the page's own origin or with `credentials: "include"`, then the CORS check of the answer; `no-cors` gives an opaque answer); the answer comes back to the script as a job under the page's lock. http.c's `http_request` takes any method, header lines and a body, keeps the answer's headers and status text, gives a redirection itself when asked, reads no body for HEAD, 204 and 304. | 27s-2 | Unicode's case tables in the JavaScript engine (item 27; the user's after 27s: "accept and create the table", under the Unicode License v3 like the exFAT capitals; no new names but the files): `scripts/casetab.py` makes `user/lib/js/casetab.h` (checked in: the build needs no Python) from the Unicode Character Database 17.0.0, the version the Mac's JavaScriptCore follows (UnicodeData.txt's simple mappings, SpecialCasing.txt's unconditional full ones, CaseFolding.txt's simple folding, DerivedCoreProperties.txt's Cased and Case_Ignorable): runs of code points with one offset (204 upper, 186 lower, 209 folding), 103 full mappings, about 14 KB. | 50d | USB NCM on real hardware (the user's, before 27t: "make sure the NCM support works", with their PlutoSDR running Tezuka set to NCM, plugged into the laptop; no new names): the Pluto is a composite device (NCM control and data, mass storage, a CDC ACM serial port, its IIO interface), and three things kept it from working. (1) A device got one driver: the storage function took it and NCM was never set up; now storage and NCM are both attached after one SET_CONFIGURATION. NCM's data interface is the one its CDC Union descriptor names (the last class-0x0A interface was taken, the serial port's). (2) Bulk transfers held the controller's lock while waiting, one per controller, and an idle NCM read waits on a real device (it NAKs; our model had answered with an empty packet): every read held up sending and the storage for 5 s, and a timed-out transfer stayed on its ring. Now each bulk endpoint has its own transfer state and lock (8 per device), the lock is held only to post, a transfer out of time is cancelled (Stop Endpoint, Set TR Dequeue), and `usb_bulk_transfer_timeout(..., 0)` waits until the device sends or is unplugged: the receive thread keeps one read pending. The NTB settings go before the data interface is switched on, as the class asks, and the input size comes from GET_NTB_PARAMETERS (up to 16 KiB, several frames an NTB). (3) The Pluto prepares its storage seconds after its USB function appears ("medium not present", sense key 2, code 0x3a): a disk without its medium now waits on a thread of its own, asked every 2 s, and is mounted when it comes (the boot or a replug). Also: an NCM adapter plugged in again (same MAC) gets its card and name back, so dhclient asks again when the link comes (it became eth2 without settings before); 8 adapters instead of 2; sent frames are counted. On the laptop: the Pluto is eth0 with 192.168.2.10 from its DHCP server, pings 192.168.2.1 in 0.6 ms, its 3.9 MB dashboard page comes over HTTP, /volumes/TEZUKA reads; after a replug the storage waits 4 s for its medium and mounts. The QEMU model (scripts/qemu/usb_ncm.c) now NAKs while idle and has the Pluto's serial-port interfaces after NCM's. Its timers run in the thread `link`'s 100 ms look (`ip6_tick`); that thread now also starts for a card found after the boot. The stack is the same for every card (the user's: "the stack has to work on any network card, ... the driver is specific"): a card is asked for its multicast groups through one operation, `netdev.set_multicast(d, macs, count)` (called by `net_set_multicast` from the thread link when they change; -1 for every multicast frame), and its driver sets the chip's filter: the e1000 and 82576 their 4096-bit table (MTA; MPE only for -1), the Realtek chips their 64-bit hash (byte-reversed and the words swapped on every RTL8168/8125, all ones on the RTL8168F, facts from Linux's r8169; our QEMU model reads MAR so now too); a driver without the operation passes every multicast frame (USB NCM's filter, Wi-Fi's access point). ipv6.c keeps only its groups, and drops our own frames coming back (an access point repeats group frames). `ping` takes IPv6 addresses, `ADDRESS%CARD` for link-local and link groups; `net` shows `IPv6 ADDRESS/64 (link-local)` per card, `::1/128` on lo and `ndp:` neighbours. common/ip6text.c (shared by the kernel and the library) writes addresses as BSD's inet_ntop (checked against the Mac's on 2 million addresses) and reads RFC 4291's forms (not the Mac's leniencies: a %card, five hex digits, a leading zero in a.b.c.d). testroute.py's hosts speak IPv6 (`ping6`, `claim6`, `mldquery`). | 64c | UDP and TCP over IPv6 (roadmap item 29; the names agreed: `SYS_CONNECT6` 103, `SYS_SENDTO6` 104, `SYS_RECVFROM6` 105, `SYS_ACCEPT6` 106, `SYS_SOCKNAME6` 107, `SOCKET_INET6`; the user's yes to the interim firewall). One TCP and one UDP for both families: `kernel/inet.h`'s `struct inet_addr` (16 bytes, IPv4 as ::ffff:a.b.c.d, a link-local address's card in `scope`) in TCP's connections, SYN cookies (hashed over the 16 bytes), UDP's bindings and the sockets, and `inet_pseudo`/`inet_source`/`inet_send`/`inet_mss` (ipv6.c) choose the family by the address; ipv4.c maps its addresses in. An IPv6 socket (`SOCKET_INET6`) talks to IPv6 and IPv4 peers, an IPv4 one to IPv4 peers only (ERR_AFNOSUPPORT); a port is one binding for both; the IPv4 calls on an IPv6 socket see 0 for an IPv6 sender. TCP's segments are 1440 bytes for IPv6 (the router's MTU less 60), and an ICMPv6 "packet too big" lowers a connection's (at least 1220, IPv6's 1280; `net`'s `tcp:` line counts `N smaller (too big)` when there are any). Pieces (fragment header) are put back together, up to 9,000 bytes, four at a time, for 30 s; overlapping ones end it (RFC 5722); an atomic fragment is taken as whole (RFC 6946); RitterOS sends none. UDP datagrams wait in memory of their own (up to 8,992 bytes) and reads take 9,000 bytes at a time; an IPv6 datagram without a checksum is dropped. The firewall checks IPv6 too (guard.c: `struct packet` and flows on `inet_addr`; until 64e a rule without an address counts for both, ICMPv6 counts as icmp, neighbour discovery, router advertisements, MLD and ICMPv6's errors always pass, a dropped IPv6 packet's addresses are 0.0.0.0 in the drops); ipv6.c checks before delivering and before sending on a card. The heap's lock is a ticket lock waiting on the futex (the threads get it in turn; until then a thread that slept a tick between tries could starve behind a busy one, and a page's files waited 30 s for their TLS); a fork's child starts it again. Measured with iBrowser's code on the Mac (www.apple.com): its expensive timer 137.5 ms with the old heap, 7.9 ms with the new one (4.3 ms with the Mac's); a restyle 98, 21 and 11 ms. Freed small blocks are kept for their size, so an arena holding one is not given back while the program runs |

The kernel keeps 128 datagrams a UDP socket now and has room for 128 UDP ports (BINDINGS, 16 before: every lookup binds one while it waits); the library's lookup asks dnsd again after 2 s when nothing came (6 s in all, as before); `net` shows a `udp:` line (ports bound, datagrams dropped by a full queue). The kernel's argument limit (ARGS_MAX) is 128 KiB, 4,096 bytes before: the kernel's link line had grown to 4,155 bytes with 64a's IPv6 files, so RitterOS's own make could not start it (the selfbuild test failed since 64a). Test `dnsburst_test`: 40 lookups at once through dnsd with the tests' DNS server answering after half a second (testdns.py `--delay`; `net6test burst N NAME`): with the old kernel and library 12 of 40 answered |
| 64k | dnsd keeps empty answers too (found with 64j's test): "no such name" (NXDOMAIN) and "no records of that type" (NOERROR without answers) are kept like addresses, for the SOA record's TTL or its minimum, whichever is smaller (RFC 2308), else a minute, at most five minutes, and answered from the cache with the server's code. On a network with IPv6 every lookup asks for an AAAA record first; for a name without one each question went to the server again while dnsd made the others wait. Test `dnsburst_test`: a second burst of 40 lookups of an IPv4-only name; with 64j's dnsd none was answered and the tests' DNS server was asked for the AAAA record 26 times, now once |
| 64l | the window system's lag on the J1900 (the user's: any window lagged, more with every window, worst when dragging, and iBrowser's page loads made it worse): windowd redrew the whole desktop into its back buffer for every change, a pointer step included (1600x1200 pixels and every window's whole content, a pixel at a time), and flushed only the changed part; now it redraws only the rectangle it flushes (a clip every drawing helper keeps to; windows outside it skipped; contents copied by rows). The scheduler: a thread woken from a wait (input, the network, a lock) runs before the threads that were already ready (a queue of its own, served first: until now it waited behind each busy thread's whole 20 ms slice, so windowd's input thread waited a multiple of that while iBrowser's threads loaded a page); a thread whose slice ran out goes to the end as before, one interrupted early by a woken thread back to the front. The owl test pressed Down seven times for x86_64/, the eighth row since 50j's /system/doc (it opened sdk/): eight now. The user, after both on the J1900: a lot better, though not yet smooth |

The cause, in the xHCI driver: an event for a TRB of an earlier transfer that was not a plain success (the AMD controller's second event after a short packet) was taken as the current transfer's end, which left its TRBs on the ring, so every read after it was matched with the event of the one before and the NCM card received nothing again. Such events are ignored now (counted); an endpoint halted by an error other than a stall is reset on the controller's side (only a stall was, and a halted endpoint never ran a transfer again); a Stop Endpoint that finds the endpoint halted resets it first; zero-length transfers are taken (the NCM driver's zero-length end of a transfer of whole packets was refused, so the adapter took the next NTB as part of the transfer). The NCM driver sends from a thread of its own (`eth1 send`; a queue of 128 frames, a sender waits for room, the receive thread never waits), counts everything and shows it in `net`. On the user's laptop with the Pluto: `iio_info` three times in a row, each run complete in 2-4 s (before: the link dead after the first, which took about two minutes); 71 late events ignored in 3,647 reads. The usbnet test sends a 484-byte frame (a 512-byte NTB): with the old controller code the zero-length end was refused and a ping after it was lost |
| 64n | the NCM link's gaps (the user's laptop with the Pluto: 289 of 245,804 reads began without an NTB's header, and TCP kept 69,707 segments after a gap): the driver now reads every NTB in a transfer (an adapter that leaves a transfer's end out between NTBs sends several in one), logs the first five reads without a header with their first bytes and the read before, and shows the NTB size it reads and the one the adapter offers; datagrams an NTB names but does not hold are counted. Our NCM model was fixed on the way: frames QEMU held while an NTB waited were never asked for again (the first two frames close together stopped receiving for good), and it now gathers frames for 300 µs into one NTB as Linux's gadget does (until then the driver's handling of several frames in one NTB was never tried in QEMU); `split=on` sends two NTBs in one transfer. The usbnet test plugs the adapter in again with `split=on` and downloads 1,000,000 bytes: with the old driver (only the first NTB of each transfer) the download stalled. Not yet seen on the Pluto: the cause of its reads without a header (the log will tell) |

No test reproduces the race (it needs a switch inside a few microseconds); sshcrypto, ssh and tlscrypto pass on both architectures |

Our Realtek model got a `stuck` switch (`qom-set`): it sends nothing until the driver resets it. The realtek test sticks the first chip: reset after 5 s, pings answered again, the other chips never reset (proven by a driver without the watchdog: no reset, the pings after it lost). The Intel cards (e1000, igb) have no such watchdog yet |

The tests' DNS server (scripts/testdns.py) answers each question after its delay on a thread of its own, and `*.ZONE` names every name in a zone; `net6test burst` takes a name with `%d` (each thread its own). The dnsburst test's third burst looks up 30 different names (60 questions, each answer half a second late): all answered, each question asked of the server once (proven by the dnsd of 64k: 26 questions asked, lookups failed) |

On the way: the library's loops now pass on a throw from listing an object's keys (assign, spread, JSON, for-in, defineProperties), to_desc reads a descriptor's fields in the standard's order, and for-in no longer lists a name from the prototype that a non-enumerable own property hides. The new case scripts/js/proxy.js (every trap logged, 40 invariant cases, throwing traps inside the library's loops) prints what the Mac's jsc prints; checked first on the Mac under ASan and UBSan, with the collector running at every allocation, and by 1,700 random Proxy programs compared with jsc |
| 64s | the Pluto's reads without an NTB header, found (the user's, after 64r: the laptop running 64r and this step): 64n's log showed the read before each one ending early by a short packet, and a second event for that finished read's last TRB; the log now also says what the read before's NTB header promised and which TRB and code ended both reads. Every such read before was a whole NTB, and the next NTB's header lay in its later pages: the laptop's AMD xHCI, after a short packet ended a transfer in one of its first TRBs, sometimes wrote the next transfer's start into a later TRB of the transfer it had finished (9 of 6,206 reads while downloading the Pluto's 3.9 MB page 20 times). Bulk transfers now have a TRB per piece up to a 64 KiB boundary, not per page (as Linux, whose one TRB per buffer never meets this), and the NCM receive buffer is aligned to its 16 KiB, so a read is always one TRB. On the laptop afterwards: 0 reads without a header and 0 stray events in 24,366 reads (60 downloads, 215,612 frames). QEMU's xHCI does not do this, so no test can show the old fault; the USB tests (NCM, serial, storage, USB boots, hot plug, FAT and exFAT sticks) pass on both architectures with the new TRBs. Still seen on the laptop: TCP keeps about 1 segment in 2,000 after a gap (a segment lost before it reached RitterOS, cause not looked into) |
| 64t | the processor's clock (the user's, after 64s: 18% of the CPU for the Pluto stream and an FFT seemed high, and the clock should boost; the names are the user's): `threads` ends with the clock while running and the share of time running since the last list (APERF/MPERF, read only at a list: nothing is measured between), on x86 processors that count them (AMD, Intel; not QEMU's, not ARM). The laptop's Ryzen 7 5800H boosted a busy loop to 4,073 MHz by itself, but under the stream's short bursts it ran at the base 3,188 MHz. AMD's CPPC (the interface of Linux's amd-pstate) was tried with each request: its highest level always gave 3,539 MHz (27.3% running instead of 30.1%) but kept the idle clock near 3.2 GHz; letting the processor choose, with the energy preference at 128 or at 0, gave 3,205 and 3,181 MHz under the stream and about 1,400 MHz idle. The new settings file /system/config/cpu (`clock = performance | balanced | powersave | top | firmware`, its comments list them and these figures) is read by the kernel after the root mount and applied through CPPC (arch/x86_64/cpuclock.c); the default, also without the file, is performance (the user's choice), elsewhere the boot log says it is not used. New disks and osinstall get the file with its comments. Tests: config_shell (a file written unusually: the kernel reads `top`; proven by a kernel that ignored the file), osinstall (the installed copy is mkdisk's), and the normal boots' line on both architectures; `threads`' line was tried on the laptop only (QEMU has no such counters) |
| 65a-1 | several cores, the groundwork's first part (roadmap item 31; still one core): each core's own state is a block (`struct cpu`, kernel/percpu.h: the running thread, its slice, the interrupt depth, the reschedule flag, the address space in place, the idle thread; on x86 also the system-call stack and the program's RSP during entry), found through a register only the kernel uses: x86's GS base and ARM's TPIDR_EL1. On x86 the kernel's GS base and the program's are exchanged (SWAPGS) on every entry from ring 3 (interrupts, exceptions, SYSCALL) and on every return to it, so a program that loads GS changes only its own; until 65a-1 the system-call entry kept its values in two global variables. The block is set as the first thing at the start (kprintf already asks for the current thread) and again after the GDT reloads the segments. Test: the new test program `gsload` loads GS with the user data selector and then makes 1,000 system calls, runs 200 ms through timer interrupts and sleeps (in the fork test; on ARM it says there is nothing to try); proven by a kernel that never swaps: a page fault at its first system call. A broad set (power, programs with their faults, quick boot, login, fork, signals, threads, pthreads, cancel, exec, spawn, pipes, poll, kill, pty, ssh, sshexec, fetch, math, malloc, mmap, ASLR, noruntime, USB hot plug, usbnet, Realtek, windowd, js, libc, TLS, dl) passed on both architectures (426 and 420 checks) |

The lock keeps the holding core, so this core taking it again is still a deadlock reported by a panic, not a hang. Test: the new crash test `spinlock` (in the shared software crashes) takes a lock twice and expects the report; proven by a lock without the holder check (the boot hung). The broad set of 65a-1 and the software crashes passed on both architectures (467 and 461 checks) |
| 65a-3 | several cores, the groundwork's third part (still one core): the scheduler has its own lock (kernel/sched.c `sched_lock`: the run queues, every wait queue, every thread's state and deadline, the list of threads), held across a switch and given back by the thread that runs next, so a thread is on a core exactly while its new `on_cpu` says so; a thread's stack is freed only once it is off its core. Waiting is three steps (`wait_prepare` puts the thread on the queue, then the caller looks at its condition, then `wait_cancel` or `wait_commit`, which sleeps only if nothing woke it meanwhile; a wake-up of a thread still on its core just marks it running), so a wake-up between the look and the sleep can no longer be lost when another core makes the condition true; the wait_event macros, poll, the futex, `sleep` and the mutex use them (the mutex has an inner spinlock for its owner and joins the queue while holding it). The lock is given back without touching the interrupt state where the code continues with interrupts off (`spin_unlock_only`: the idle thread before its halt, a new thread's start). Found on the way: our own assembler did not know ARM's TPIDR_EL1, which 65a-1's `arch_this_cpu` uses, so RitterOS could not build its own ARM kernel since 65a-1 (the selfbuild test, which 65a-1's and 65a-2's scoped sets lacked); as knows it now, byte for byte as clang. A lost wake-up cannot happen on one core with interrupts off, so its proof comes with real parallelism (65c). Tests: 65a-2's broad set plus poll, nonblock, termios, local sockets, the guard, elves, iBrowser and selfbuild passed on x86 (810 checks); ARM 802 of 804, the two failures the self-build's (since 65a-1), which passes with the assembler fixed (selfbuild and cc on both: 13 checks each) |
| 65a-4 | several cores, the groundwork's fourth part (still one core): every place that used "interrupts off" as its lock now has a real spinlock, since on several cores turning interrupts off on one stops nothing on another: the xHCI event ring (`events_lock` per controller: the interrupt handler, the tick's look, a thread waiting for its transfer, endpoint registration, the doorbell, detach, the hot-plug bits), the USB hubs' list (`hubs_lock`), the keyboards (`keyboards_lock`), the terminal's input ring (`input_lock`), the AMD GPIO pins (`pins_lock`, given back around a pin's handler), the firmware calls (`firmware_lock`), and two descriptor-table looks in process.c (a disk opened for writing, the busy check before an unmount) now take each process's `fds` lock. Kept as they are, because they only mean "not interrupted on this core": exec's and fork's register sections, the touchpad's `emit`, the futex's interrupt save. The rule for 65c: device interrupts and the tick's handlers run on core 0 only; only xHCI events can be handled elsewhere (by a waiting thread), under `events_lock`. Panic, power-off and reboot must stop the other cores (65b). Tests: 65a-3's set plus typing, PS/2, keys in windows, USB hubs, sticks, USB boot, late sticks, osinstall, the mice, the tablet, volumes, partitions, format, edit and term: x86 1,101 checks passed; ARM 1,083 passed, its 12 failures the PS/2 tests, which the full run has only on x86 (the scoped script ran them on ARM by mistake) |

Each core keeps the spinlocks it holds (`struct cpu`'s `held`, always on: a store and a count per lock), so a sleep, a wait, a yield, a thread switch or a mutex taken while holding one panics with the lock's and the thread's names (the holder would keep it through the sleep, and every other taker would spin for good); a mutex is checked even when it is free. With `lockcheck` on the kernel command line the order is checked too: every lock name gets a number, taking B while holding A records "A before B", and an order that closes a circle panics before the lock is waited for, naming both locks (two of the same name nested, and unnamed locks, are counted and skipped); `threads` reports how many names and orders it saw. `make test` sets `lockcheck` on every test boot, next to `selftest`. Found on the way: kprintf under a spinlock took its output mutex, which could sleep with the spinlock held; it now only tries, as in an interrupt handler (the line goes out unlocked if another is printing). The i2c bus's mutex got a name. Tests: the crash tests `locksleep` and `lockorder` (expected panics), the new `lockcheck` test (a short session, then `threads`: 21 lock names, 21 orders, no circle on both architectures); 65a-4's broad set with lockcheck on every boot passed on x86 (1,124 checks) and ARM (1,106), the PS/2 tests on x86 only |
| 65b | several cores: the other cores started and parked (roadmap item 31). The kernel reads the cores from ACPI's MADT (x86 Local APIC and x2APIC entries, ARM GIC CPU interfaces with their MPIDR) and starts each but the boot core, up to `cpus=N` (`cpus=1`: none); each gets a block (`struct cpu`) and a 32 KiB stack (kernel/cpus.c), sets itself up, reports in and parks with its interrupts off, so nothing runs on it yet (65c). x86 (arch/x86_64/smp.c): INIT, 10 ms, STARTUP (again after 1 ms if needed) into a page below 640 KiB (`pmm_low_page`, from the first 1 MiB the page allocator never gives out), whose code climbs from 16-bit real mode through 32-bit protected mode (PAE, start-up tables below 4 GiB mapping the first 2 MiB at their own address and the kernel's upper half, EFER.LME and NXE, paging) to 64-bit mode and into the kernel, which loads the kernel's GDT, IDT, CR0 (INIT leaves caching off), CR4, PAT, EFER and page tables and takes its GS block, then `cli; hlt`. The start-up code is commented bytes in C: it begins in 16-bit mode, which our own assembler does not encode; the bytes are clang's for the same lines. ARM (arch/aarch64/smp.c, smp.S): PSCI CPU_ON (power.c's call now takes arguments) at the physical address of an entry routine that turns the MMU on with the kernel's MAIR, TCR, SCTLR and TTBR1 and, for the jump, a TTBR0 mapping the 1 GiB around itself, then switches the lower half off, takes its stacks and TPIDR_EL1 and parks (WFI, interrupts masked); what it reads with its MMU still off is cleaned to memory first. The boot log has a `cores:` line; `cpus` (the agreed name: the rescue console's command, and a program of the same name with REPORT_CPUS, a recorded compatible ABI addition) lists each core's number, hardware ID and state. A core that faults before it has an IDT resets the whole machine (seen in the proof: the boot looped), so on a PC where starting the cores fails, `cpus=1` is the way back. Parking is HLT/WFI; deeper sleep (ACPI `_CST`, MWAIT hints) only after measuring on the laptop. Tests: the new `cpus` test on a 4-core machine (x86 TCG: 3 parked, about 10 ms each, the INIT wait; ARM: 3 parked in microseconds) and with `cpus=2` (1 parked, 2 left off); proven by a start-up page whose 32-bit jump was wrong (every check failed). A scoped set (crashes, power, programs, quick boot, login, fork, lockcheck, signals, threads, man, sdk, selfbuild, osinstall, USB boot, noruntime, mmap, noserial on x86) passed on x86 (350 checks) and ARM (338) |
| 65c-1 | several cores: each started core ready to run threads (they take none yet). One core is the default now (the user's choice at 65c: until several are proven on the J1900 and the laptop); `cpus=N` starts more. Its start-up code then becomes its idle thread, `idle1`, `idle2`, ... (the agreed names; `threads` lists them). Tests: the `cpus` test on 4 cores with `cpus=4` (all ticking, idle1-idle3), `cpus=2` and the default (the others left off), a reboot and power-off with 4 cores and a panic with 4 (one report: the others stopped; proven by an NMI handler without the halt, whose cores printed their own crash reports interleaved); a broad scoped set passed on x86 (742 checks, with the CPU crash tests, the PS/2 tests and noserial) and ARM (696) |

A program's page unmapped is forgotten on every core: ARM's invalidation is the broadcast form (`tlbi vmalle1is`), x86 interrupts each other core whose active address space it is (vector 0xF1) and waits for its answer, answering any request to itself meanwhile, so two cores never wait for each other; before a program's page tables are freed (exit, exec) no other core may still have them in place (`cpus_wait_space_unused`). Every test machine now has two cores (`-smp 2`, `cpus=2` beside `selftest lockcheck`; the normal boot counts `idle1` among its threads). (2) A file just written read back as zeros: read() took the file's inode and its data in two holds of the file system's lock, and another thread's close (a commit) in between moved the data (`ritterfs_read_now`/`vfs_read_now`: both in one hold). (3) A program's own GS selector stayed in the core, not with its thread (`gsload` read 0 after moving): the scheduler now saves and loads it (`arch_user_segments`). New checks stay: a thread queued twice or chosen while it runs on a core panics, a fork child must find itself as its own thread, an ARM exception from EL0 must save its frame on its thread's own stack, and crash reports name the core and thread. Tests: `smpstress_test` (four cores, two 10 s runs: on ARM about a billion reads of the replaced page, 270 forks, 120,000 heap blocks a run, all checked), before the fixes crashing or wrong in most ARM runs; the broad scoped set with two cores everywhere passed on x86 (745 checks) and ARM (700) |
| 65c-4 | several cores: every core by default (the user's choice, after the J1900 ran on its 4 and the laptop on its 16: both started each core in 10.0 ms, the stress test clean on both, the parallel test 3.6 times one thread's work on the J1900's 4 cores and 12 on the laptop's 16). Tests: the `cpus` test's default case (4 cores, all started) and a scoped set on both architectures |

Those indexes are the ones Linux's DCN 2.1 headers give (segment 2 0x34C0 + 0x28D1, + 0x28F5), so the register layout used for the rest is confirmed by the card's own table.
