An AUX transfer goes as Linux's dce_aux does it: the engine asked of its arbiter (software must be granted it), the last DONE acknowledged, the request (command, 20-bit address, length, data) written into AUX_SW_DATA, GO, DONE waited for (at most 5 ms), the reply read (ACK, NACK, DEFER; I2C over AUX NACK, DEFER), the engine given back; DEFERs retried 7 times 400 us apart, timeouts 3 times, bad replies twice. The EDID is I2C over AUX at 0x50 (the offset with "middle of transfer", 16-byte reads, an address-only stop); a hotplug pin that senses nothing answers "no monitor" without a transfer. With every whole read the DPCD's receiver capabilities (0x000-0x00F), eDP's revision (0x700) and, when the top rate is 0 (eDP 1.4), the panel's own table of link rates (0x010-0x01F). `displays`: the transfers' counters, the time of a whole read, the DPCD. On the laptop: the panel is read (256 bytes, native 1920x1080 at 60.036 Hz, its only mode, no name in its EDID) and logged `gpu0: edp0 connected`; 545 transfers in 6 minutes, all ACK (no DEFER, NACK, time-out or busy engine); 128 bytes in 3.46 ms; DisplayPort 1.4, eDP 1.4a, 4 lanes, link rates 1.62 2.16 2.43 2.70 3.24 4.32 5.40 8.10 Gbit/s a lane. Now both, on both architectures, do as a panic does: interrupts off, the other cores stopped, kprintf's emergency path (no lock), then the reset. The kernel keeps the sound layer, `kernel/sound.c`: the cards registered while the modules load are numbered `pcm0`, `pcm1`, ... by kind after them (`sound_announce`: speakers and headphones, line outs, digital; the registration order within a kind, so the PCI order as before), a later card gets the next number; `/dev/pcmN`'s writes and the drain at close go through it, and the `hdatone` test tone is the layer's now (`sound: test tone played on pcm0`, after the modules). HD Audio controllers are every maker's, so a module's table may now match a PCI class: `MODULE_CLASS(class, subclass)` (an entry whose vendor is `MODULE_BY_CLASS` 0xFFFE, the table's layout unchanged); hda's is `MODULE_CLASS(0x04, 0x03)`, and its start checks the class again. In QEMU: `modules: hda (00:05.0, 00:06.0), usbnet (no device yet) loaded; 7 not needed`, hda.mod 13 KB on x86, 12 on ARM; the x86 kernel 2,711,472 -> 2,689,368 bytes. Tests: `hda_shell_test` (the module line; the tone captured), `hdamulti_shell_test`, `soundmix_shell_test`, `tune_shell_test`, `video_shell_test`, `noterminal_shell_test`, the normal boot's terminal checks (the counting of each module's table knows `MODULE_CLASS`), `realtek_shell_test`, `drivertemplate_test`, `usbnet_shell_test`, on both architectures; proven by a loader that ignores class entries (hda not needed, no `pcm0`, a silent capture) |
| 67h | the self-build builds the modules: since 67b the kernel needs `drivers/include/` and `arch-build` makes every module, but `selfbuild_shell_test` copied no `drivers/`, so RitterOS could no longer build itself. Now it copies `drivers/*.c` and `drivers/include/*.h` too; RitterOS's `make TOOLCHAIN=ritteros` builds the nine modules with our own cc and as (x86 18,368 bytes for hda.mod, zig's 12,520), each passing the `kexports` check, and every file of `build/system/<arch>` and the bootloader, 207 on each architecture, the modules among them, is byte for byte the Mac's build with the same tools (RitterOS's make: 205 s on x86, 19 s on ARM; the copy over SSH 83 and 64 s). Our toolchain's modules use only relocations the loader knows (x86 64, PC32, PLT32; ARM ABS64, ADRP, the LO12 forms, CALL26), and they work: zig's kernel with them passed `hda_shell_test` (the tone captured) and every network check of `realtek_shell_test` on both architectures (only the report's sizes differ from zig's: rtl8168 code 20 KB, file 32 KB). | 66g-1 | the AMD mode read back further, before setting modes (only read): `displays` now shows, for the running OTG, its pixel clock's source (the DCCG's DP DTO: phase and modulo in Hz of the DP reference), the pipe's DPP clock DTO, its blanking, double buffering, variable refresh and OPTC source; the DP stream's MSA (the timing the panel is told) and M/N; the plane's request sizes and its DLG/TTU schedule; the clocks the clock block counts (display, DPP, DP reference, DCF; its VCO and the DENTIST's dividers; amdgpu now maps BAR 5 up to the clock block, 366 KiB); the hub's four watermark sets and its reference clock. The firmware's plane schedule does not follow Linux's formulas (REF_FREQ_TO_PIX_FREQ gives a 21.8 MHz reference, REFCYC_PER_HTOTAL 32.4 MHz, the hub's is 48): 66g-2 keeps it rather than computing one. | 65e-35 | TCP's lock per connection (the user's, after measuring: the laptop's `cpus` showed TCP's one lock waited for 2,136,286 times, 718 ms, during the Pluto's stream). `tcp table` guards the slots, the ports, the matching of a segment to its connection and the listeners' queues; `tcp connection`, one in each connection, the rest of it; the table's always first, a slot freed only with both held. Each connection has its own outbox (sent after its lock is given back); replies of no connection (resets, SYN cookies' SYN-ACKs) a one-segment box on the stack, sent after the table's lock; the ACKs owed at a burst's end a mark in the connection (by the receiving thread) instead of a shared list; the counters and netloss's generator atomic. The branch that put a SYN_RECEIVED connection on a listener's queue is gone (a listener's connections start ESTABLISHED, from SYN cookies). `smpstress` has a `tcp` part: three loopback connections at once, every 1 KiB block checked. It found an old bug (fast retransmit, 38b): `send_again` resent a whole MSS from snd_una while less was outstanding, bytes snd_max never counted; the receiver took them, its ACKs (beyond snd_max) were thrown away by RFC 5961's check together with the window they opened, and both ends waited for ever: with every part running it hung every time, the old TCP code too; now a resend keeps to what was sent (six runs passed after). On the laptop (16 hardware threads), `smpstress 10 tcp` three times each: 1.49-1.62 million blocks before, 1.67-1.69 million after (+6%; the one loopback thread carries all three); the TCP lock's waits a run 8.3-8.6 million spins (~2.7 s) and 170,000-194,000 sleeps (~0.7 s) before, 4.9-5.2 million spins (~1 s) and 333-650 sleeps (11-15 ms) after, none on the table's. Tests: `smpstress_test`, `net`, `netloss`, `sockets`, `socket`, `fetch`, `https`, `ssh`, `sshexec`, `lo`, `tcp6`, `wscale`, `sacksend`, `netrate`, `guardlimit`, `usbnet`, `ibrowser` on both architectures (532 checks each) |

The inode, read, lookup and directory functions and the check take it to read, the changes to write. Two new locks (names agreed): `ritterfs cache` (a spinlock; a block is copied out under it, never handed out by pointer; its 8 MiB made outside it) and `ritterfs index` (the directory index and the snapshots' views: the check makes views under its read hold). Found on the way: an O_APPEND write found the file's end in one hold and wrote in another, so two appenders wrote at one place (eight shells appending a line each kept seven); `ritterfs_append` finds the end and writes in one write hold (`vfs_append`; FAT and exFAT as before). `smpstress` has an `append` part (four threads, one file): all 130,641 lines there with it, 51,177 of 125,058 without. On the laptop, 8 shells reading the 197 KB manual 300 times each: 1.59 s before (the readers asleep on the lock 454,000 times, 10.1 s in all), 1.05 s after (RitterFS's lock 92-97 waits, 34-44 ms); the waiting moved to `ritterfs cache` (1.9 million spins, 5.9 s a run: the copy under it is the next thing to shorten). Tests: `smpstress_test` (a new check: RitterFS's waits by kind; proven by the mutex before), `fs`, `write`, `snapshot` (it found the view taken to write under the check's read hold), `attr`, `mmap`, `fstat`, the normal boot's terminal checks, on both architectures |
| 65e-37 | the boot's RitterFS check beside the boot (the user's; the name `fscheck` agreed): after the root mount a kernel thread `fscheck` checks the file system while the boot goes on (`RitterFS check: beside the boot (the thread fscheck)`, then `RitterFS check: done in N ms, beside the boot`); it holds the file system's lock to read (65e-36), so nothing changes under it and changes wait for it, which made the planned pinning of its blocks unnecessary. `RitterFS time:` keeps the mount (and the self-test's tree and files). | 65e-38 | parallel page faults (the user's, item 31's "after" list; the name `faults` agreed): a process's `mappings` lock is a readers-writer lock. A page fault that only fills an empty entry (anonymous memory's first touch, a file page mapped, a private file page's first write when nothing was there) holds it to read, so a program's threads fault at once; a fault that changes a page already in place (copy-on-write after a fork, a read-only page made writable) takes it again to write and looks again; mmap, munmap, mprotect, msync, fork and the process's end hold it to write. On the laptop (pages a second): 1 thread 1.69 M before and after, 4 threads 0.72 M -> 2.26 M, 8 threads 0.31 M -> 1.62 M (before, the 8 slept 49.6 s of 10 on the lock); the waits now are the rwlock's own spinlock (`mappings (spun)`) and faults waiting for the part's munmaps (`(read)`). A woken thread goes to the waker's core when nothing else waits there (the user's choice after measuring: the waker mostly sleeps next), else to its last core if that is idle, else to an idle core; an idle core takes threads from the longest other queue (ready ones before woken ones), a busy one evens out a queue two longer at every tenth tick. Ended threads are freed by the idle loop and `thread_create`, outside every run lock. smpstress's `wakeups`: 8 pairs of threads pass a token through two pipes each. Tests: `smpstress_test` (proven on x86 by a kernel skipping the last announcement: the kernel's heap was written through a translation left behind, a panic), `mmap`, `malloc`, `fork`, `pthread`, `exec`, `spawn`, `lockcheck` and the programs test, on both architectures |

MAP_FIXED still empties the range under the write hold. On the laptop (65e-47 -> 65e-48, 10 s each): faults 3.39 M -> 3.83 M pages a second, `mappings` spun 9.44 s -> 3.04 s; tlb, fork, spawn, heap, pipe and wakeups alone as before. Tests: `smpstress_test` (proven on x86 by a kernel that gives the lock back before clearing the pages: the next mmap took the same addresses and the late clearing freed its pages, a double free; a kernel that downgrades before taking the mappings out passed: that race needs a fault in the list's 0.2 us change), `mmap`, `malloc`, `fork`, `pthread`, `exec`, `spawn`, `lockcheck`, `pipe`, `sockets` and the programs test, on both architectures |

On the laptop (65e-48 -> 65e-49, 10 s each): faults 3.83 M -> 3.93-4.02 M pages a second (three runs), `mappings` spun 3.04 s -> 2.35 s; fork, tlb, spawn, heap alone as before. Tests: `smpstress_test` (proven on x86 by a kernel that marks only the parent: the child gave back pages it shared, a double free), `mmap`, `malloc`, `fork`, `pthread`, `exec`, `spawn`, `lockcheck`, `pipe` and the programs test, on both architectures |

Now memtest waits for the check to end first (`fscheck_running`, a wait queue in main.c); boots without `memtest` are unchanged. Tests: the memory test boot, three times on ARM (the check done in 551 ms, then 1,040,967 pages written and verified) and on x86; the kernel before failed it on ARM (its proof) |

Documentation only; `sdk_shell_test` on x86 and ARM |
| 65e-52 | `_Atomic` in RitterOS's `cc` and its own `<stdatomic.h>` (found while writing 65e-51's note: `cc` took `_Atomic` and ignored it, so `n++` was a plain load, add and store): a read of an `_Atomic` lvalue is an atomic load, an assignment an atomic store, `++`, `--` and `op=` one atomic update (`+=` `-=` `&=` `|=` `^=` of an integer, `+=`/`-=` of a pointer, a fetch-and-op; the rest, floats included, a compare-and-swap loop over what the operator computes); all sequentially consistent (the `__atomic_` functions' instructions: x86 `lock`/`xchg`, ARM `ldar`/`stlr`/exclusives); scalars of 1-8 bytes only (an atomic structure or long double is refused: clang calls libatomic). `<stdatomic.h>` is among cc's own headers (zig's clang keeps its own); `atomic_fetch_add`/`_sub` step a pointer by elements through clang's names `__c11_atomic_fetch_add`/`_sub`; `__STDC_NO_ATOMICS__` is gone. Fixed on the way: `_Atomic(T)` with a declarator (`_Atomic(int *)`) did not parse (its declarator began at the type again). Found: clang stores `b += 5` of an `_Atomic _Bool` unconverted (6), and a later `b -= 1` loops for good (cctest's first run hung there); clang refuses `+=`/`-=` of an atomic pointer. `cc`'s output for code without atomics is unchanged (328 files of user/bin, lib and apps, both architectures, byte for byte). Tests: `cc_shell_test` on x86 and ARM (cctest: every operator, `<stdatomic.h>`'s functions, 4 threads x 100,000 rounds: exact totals); proven by a `cc` whose `op=` and `++` load and store apart: ARM lost updates (399,995 of 400,000 `++`, 399,429 of 400,000 `+= 1.0`) |
| 65e-53 | stdio safe between threads (found with 65e-51's note: a `FILE` had no lock, and `fopen`/`fclose` changed the list of streams unguarded): each stream has a lock (threads.c's futex mutex, nesting, its owner named by a `_Thread_local`'s address), held by every function on it for its call (the _unlocked cores inside); POSIX's `flockfile`, `ftrylockfile`, `funlockfile`, `getc_unlocked`, `getchar_unlocked`, `putc_unlocked`, `putchar_unlocked` (libritteros.so.1: 7 new, recorded; no new major version). Until a program makes its first thread no stream lock is taken (`__ritteros_threads_made`, set by thread creation). The list has its own lock (`fclose` takes its stream off before holding it; `fflush(0)` holds each in turn under it, as glibc); `exit` flushes holding only the list (a thread waiting in `fgets` holds its stream) and removes tmpfiles' names without closing; `fork` holds the list across the call, before the heap's, and the child frees every stream's lock but its own thread's. `puts` writes up to 255 bytes and its newline in one `write`. `pthread_spin_lock`'s comment no longer speaks of one processor. | 65e-54 | `fflush(0)` never waits for a stream holding the list's lock (65e-53's, as glibc's, musl's and the Mac's, did: a thread holding that stream with `flockfile` and calling `fopen` waited for the list, the flush for its stream, for good): a free stream is flushed under the list's lock; a busy one is pinned (`fclose` leaves its freeing to the last pin), the list given back, then waited for; a thread that holds a stream itself waits for no other in `fflush(0)` and passes the busy ones (two threads each holding one and flushing all waited for each other; `streams_held` counts a thread's own). Tests: `pthread_shell_test`'s own part (both cases end within 5 s; a watchdog reports a deadlock), `libc`, `fork` and the programs test on x86 and ARM; proven on ARM by 65e-53's `fflush` (the first case deadlocked) and by one that lets a holder wait (the second did) |

Tests: `fork_shell_test` on ARM 8 times (before and after equal to the page every time; before: 0 to 10 apart), `fork` and the programs test (its leak check) on both architectures |
| 65e-56 | the J1900's ~18 ms waits for the mappings (`smpstress 10 faults`: readers slept 1,374 times, 25.5 s in all) looked at: holders switched out holding the lock came back within 0.09 ms on average (1,387 times, 122 ms in all), and a woken reader waited on the waker's busy core for the next tick and for the slices of others woken with it (2,283 times, 24.6 s). Woken ones sent to their last core at once (tried): 6,047 sleeps of 3 ms instead, the same 1.10 M pages a second, and every core 100% busy before and after (the idle threads gained no tick in the 10 s): 8 threads on 4 cores each wait half the time for a core, and a sleep on the lock counts that wait as the lock's. Found on the way, and fixed: `rw_sleep` (65e-45) joined the queue (which makes the thread asleep for the scheduler) and only then set the sleepers' mark, with interrupts on between; a thread switched out there slept unmarked, so no release woke it, and as a writer counted waiting it kept every reader asleep behind it (QEMU, 4 ARM cores, woken threads sent at once: smpstress's faults hung in 4 of 5 runs; a ring of the lock's last 128 events showed the writer waking, finding the lock held and never marking). Now interrupts are off from the queue to the sleep, as `wait_event` keeps them. Tests: that configuration 6 of 6 runs; `smpstress_test 4`, `mmap`, `pthread`, `lockcheck`, `fork` and the programs test on x86 and ARM |
| 32a | AVX and AVX2 for programs on x86 (roadmap item 32, the user's: names as recommended). Where the processor has XSAVE and AVX (CPUID 1), the boot core chooses once (`fpu_choose`, arch/x86_64/user.c) and every core switches CR4.OSXSAVE on and sets XCR0 = x87, SSE and AVX (AVX-512's and PKU's parts stay off); a program thread's vector registers are then saved with XSAVE/XRSTOR (arch/x86_64/fpu.S), the YMM registers' upper halves included, in 832 bytes (`ARCH_FPU_STATE_SIZE`, CPUID leaf 0xD's offset and size checked against it: else FXSAVE as before, which the J1900 and QEMU's default model keep). The boot says `FP/SIMD: x87, SSE and AVX (XSAVE, 832 bytes per program)`. A new program starts with all vector registers zero (before: the XMM registers as the firmware left them). The signal context's area moved to a 64-byte boundary (192; the frame on the program's stack 672 -> 1,024 bytes, its layout private: no ABI change) and is zeroed first (the unused bytes carried kernel stack contents); at sigreturn the XSAVE header keeps only XCR0's parts and the rest of it is zeroed, as MXCSR's reserved bits were already (a forged header made XRSTOR fault in the kernel: a #GP crash). Programs find AVX themselves (CPUID's AVX and OSXSAVE, XGETBV; AVX2 in CPUID 7): no system call. Our `as` learnt `xsave`, `xrstor`, `xsave64`, `xrstor64`, `xgetbv`, `xsetbv` (clang's bytes); it has no VEX instructions, so the test program `avxtest` writes its few AVX ones (vmovdqu, vpaddd) as bytes, compared with clang's. `avx_shell_test max` (QEMU's `-cpu max`: AVX, AVX2, FMA, XSAVE): all 16 YMM registers kept across thread switches (4 threads x 6 rounds of 50 ms on 2 cores), across signal handlers that change them (sent during a 300 ms hold), through fork (parent and child), zero upper halves after exec, an AVX2 addition of 8 lanes, and a forged context cleaned; `avx_shell_test` (the default model): FXSAVE and `avxtest: no AVX`. Each part proven by a kernel broken for it: AVX on with FXSAVE saving (switches, signals, fork, exec failed), sigreturn not restoring (signals), fork's child with clean registers (fork), exec keeping the old ones (exec), the header not cleaned (the kernel's #GP). Scoped tests passed (x86: avx max and default, signals, sigmask, fork, exec, pthread, programs with the FP check, selfbuild; ARM: selfbuild). 32b next: measuring on the laptop, the SDK note |
| 32b | what AVX2 gives, measured on the laptop (roadmap item 32): `avxtest speed` adds 32-bit integers with SSE2 (`paddd`, 4 sums of 16 bytes) and AVX2 (`vpaddd`, 4 of 32), both clang's bytes. In the cache (16 KiB, the clock read every 4 MiB: read after every 240 ns pass it cost as much as the pass, 1.34x) SSE2 122 GB/s, AVX2 226 GB/s (1.86x); over 64 MiB of memory 30.2 against 34.5 GB/s (1.15x: the memory is the limit); the same sums both ways, three runs within 1%. Saving and restoring the registers, as each switch between program threads does: FXSAVE 65 ns, XSAVE 116 ns, the same with the YMM upper halves unused (after `vzeroupper`) or in use (this Zen 3 skips nothing for plain XSAVE): 51 ns more per switch (at 10,000 switches a second, 0.5 ms a second). On the laptop after the reboot: `FP/SIMD: x87, SSE and AVX (XSAVE, 832 bytes per program)`, its 5 drivers loaded, every `avxtest` part kept (switches, signals, fork, exec, the AVX2 addition, the forged context). The SDK guide's note: AVX code in functions marked `__attribute__((target("avx2")))` with zig's `<immintrin.h>`, called after CPUID and XGETBV say yes, behind `#if defined(__x86_64__) && defined(__clang__)` (RitterOS's own `cc` has no AVX and defines no `__clang__`); its example built with the SDK ran on the laptop ("AVX2: same sum") and compiles with our cc and as. `avx_shell_test max` runs `avxtest speed` too (its lines and equal sums; proven by AVX2's sum of half its lanes). Scoped tests passed (x86: avx max and default, selfbuild) |
| 32c | AVX, AVX2 and FMA in our assembler (roadmap item 32, the user's: "AVX instructions in the cc and as"). `as` on x86-64 has one table of vector instructions (`simd_table` in user/bin/as/x86_64.c: prefix, map 0F/0F38/0F3A, opcode, operand form, flags) that gives each SSE instruction (to SSE4.1) and its AVX form (`v` + the name: a VEX prefix with REX's bits, the map, the second source register and the vector length), plus the AVX-only ones (broadcasts, `vinserti128`/`vextracti128`, `vperm2i128`, `vpermq`/`vpermd`, `vpermilps`, `vpblendd`, `vpblendvb` with its 4th register in the immediate, the variable shifts, masked moves, `vtestps`), FMA (the 132/213/231 forms of fmadd, fmsub, fnmadd, fnmsub, fmaddsub, fmsubadd), the compares by predicate name (8 for SSE, 32 for AVX), `vmovd`/`vmovq`/`vmovss`/`vmovsd` and `vzeroupper`/`vzeroall`; `%ymm0`-`%ymm15`; up to 4 operands. The old SSE table keeps the forms cc writes (taken first: their bytes unchanged). LLVM's choices are copied: the 2-byte VEX prefix where W is 0, the map 0F and no X or B bit; and where only the r/m register being r8-r15 prevents it, commutable operations swap their sources and moves use their store opcode (LLVM's optimizeInstFromVEX3ToVEX2; not the scalar ss/sd ones, nor compares whose predicate is not symmetric). `avxas_host_test` (on the Mac, scripts/avxas.py): every table entry written in its forms (SSE and AVX, xmm and ymm, registers 0-15 in each place, memory with base, index, r8-r15 and rip, immediates), 14,944 instructions, 87,417 bytes, all the same as clang's; the as is built by the test itself (make test does not rebuild build/host/as: the first proofs ran a stale one and passed). Proven by an as without the swaps (`vpaddsb %ymm15, %ymm3, %ymm4` with a 3-byte prefix) and one with vpaddd's opcode wrong. Not there: AVX2's gathers, SSE4.2's string instructions, F16C, AVX-512. Scoped tests passed (avxas_host_test; cc and selfbuild on x86 and ARM: RitterOS's cc builds the new as, and RitterOS builds itself as the Mac does) |
| 32d | the vector types and `<immintrin.h>` in our compiler (roadmap item 32, the user's: "AVX instructions in the cc and as"). cc takes GCC's `vector_size(16 or 32)` on a typedef (x86-64): a vector is a structure without members for cc, aligned to its size, copied, passed and returned whole in one xmm/ymm register (a new class, CLASS_VEC: arguments, results, parameters; not through `...`), without operators, subscripts or brace initializers; `vector_size` anywhere but a typedef is refused (before 32d it was ignored: `long long` silently). `user/bin/cc/include/immintrin.h` (ours, 655 intrinsics): SSE to SSE4.1 in SSE's encodings, AVX, AVX2 and FMA, Intel's names; each operation a function (a macro where an argument must be an immediate) around its instruction on vectors in memory (32c's assembler), data movement (set, cast, extract, insert, broadcasts) in C. `-mavx`, `-mavx2`, `-mfma` define `__AVX__`, `__AVX2__`, `__FMA__` (x86-64 only: ARM refuses them); `target(...)` is taken. cctest calls every intrinsic on fixed vectors and prints each result (`user/test/cctest_simd.h`, written by `scripts/cctest_simd.py` from the header: 1,241 calls): `cc_shell_test max` (QEMU's `-cpu max`) has clang's build (with clang's own header) and RitterOS's cc's build print the same 1,399 lines (the three 128-to-256 casts compared in their defined half: clang leaves a copy of the low half above); and vectors between clang's code and cc's (scripts/vabi: vabi1.o compiled by clang on the Mac, vabi2.c by cc on RitterOS, both ways with integers and doubles among the vectors): all right. Measured with a static stringtest (the new library inside, nothing installed), GB/s at 4 KiB, before -> after: the laptop (AVX2) strlen 3.4 -> 155.7, memchr 4.2 -> 186.8, strchr 4.2 -> 59.2, memcmp 4.2 -> 41.0, strcmp 4.1 -> 37.1, memcpy 46.0 -> 74.9-100.7, memmove 21.7 -> 92.2, memset 58.9 -> 111.3; at 64 MiB the memory's limit (strlen 3.4 -> 34.3, memcpy 9.9 -> 9.4); the J1900 (SSE2) strlen 1.3 -> 17.0, memchr 0.5 -> 12.9, strchr 0.5 -> 6.0, memcmp 0.5 -> 5.7, strcmp 0.4 -> 3.1, memcpy and memmove as before (14.6 and 9.9; the backward loop was one block a pass first: 6.9, then unrolled). libctest has stpcpy (memrchr is not in the Mac's libc). | 32h | our cc keeps vectors in registers (roadmap item 32, the user's, after plutosa's agent found cc's AVX2 slow: a FIR filter of 32 taps measured on the laptop, clang scalar 10.56 ns a sample, clang AVX2 1.38, cc scalar 75.59, cc AVX2 322.66: slower than its own scalar code, each intrinsic a call with its vectors through memory). Vectors are values of their own (V_V16, V_V32; no longer memory values, so the header's small functions are inlined), loaded, stored, copied, passed and returned in xmm/ymm; the x86 backend gives them xmm8-xmm15 (regalloc's second pool: a class mask, CLOBBERS_FPRS for calls, where every xmm register is lost; scalar floats stay on the stack, unchanged). Inline assembly with "x" operands (GCC's; %x, %t) is allocated like an instruction when it has one output at most, "x"/"r"/immediate inputs, no memory, clobbers only xmm0-xmm7 (asm_in_registers; its output a value, not a store; other statements keep 62c's barrier), and its template may give {SSE|VEX} forms; the header (654 intrinsics) is written that way, with setzero, set1_ps/pd/epi32, casts, cvtss_f32, cvtsi128_si32 and the broadcasts as single instructions. The first result, 247 ns, was the processor's penalty for an SSE instruction beside dirty ymm registers (one movss made VEX: 3.61 ns): so a function with 256-bit values, or marked target("avx"/"avx2"/"fma") (32h: the attribute is read), is AVX code: line() writes its scalar SSE instructions in VEX (vexify), 128-bit vectors move with VEX, vzeroupper before calls and returns without ymm values, and a marked function is never inlined into one without the mark (its caller's SSE fallback stays SSE: the J1900). Our as learnt vcvtsi2ss/sd and vcvt[t]ss/sd2si (avxas_host_test: 15,160 instructions as clang's). The FIR now: cc AVX2 3.60 ns a sample (90 times faster, 2.5 times clang's AVX2, 3 times faster than clang's scalar code). Scoped tests passed (avxas, cc and cc max, selfbuild, sdk on x86 and ARM) |
| 35a | the swap file (roadmap item 35, the user's choices and names, 2026-11-02): `/system/swap`, a *fixed* file of RitterFS (inode flag bit 1, `RFS_FLAG_FIXED`; docs/ritterfs.md's new section, a compatible addition): its data blocks stay where they are and the kernel writes them in place, so their checksums are not kept; the check (kernel and `ritterfs.py`) only claims them, a scrub skips them and never relocates the file, programs cannot open it (`Device or resource busy`), write, cut or instantly copy it (a folder's copy leaves it out), and while swap is on it cannot be removed. A file becomes fixed only when it is whole blocks without holes and no snapshot or copy shares a block (each born after the pin). The program `swap`: `swap` (anyone), `swap make SIZE` (writes zeros once, which gives the file its blocks, then turns swap on), `swap on`, `swap off` (root's); on and off write `/system/config/swap` (`on = yes`/`no`), which the thread `fscheck` reads after the boot's check (turning swap on takes the file system to write: in the boot it would wait for the check). `SYS_SWAP` (112: `SWAP_STATUS`, `SWAP_ON`, `SWAP_OFF`, `struct sys_swap`), `ritteros_swap` in the library (recorded in sdk/abi), `mem`'s third line. kernel/swap.c: slot *s* is the file's content block *s*, found through runs of consecutive blocks read once from its tree (a commit places a file's new blocks from the last one down, so runs also go down: 16 MiB in 34 runs), pages through the partition's block device past RitterFS's cache, each encrypted with XTS-AES-256 (kernel/xts.c on common/aes.c, the slot as tweak) under a key made each time swap goes on and kept only in memory. With `selftest` every slot is written and read back (16 MiB: 4096 of 4096 as written, none readable on the disk). Nothing is paged out yet (35b). Tests: `swap_shell_test` (the XTS code built on the Mac against LibreSSL's `aes-256-xts`, 6 cases; three boots of one image: the program's answers and refusals, a snapshot holding the file while the self-test writes in place, the host finding the file fixed with every block written in the kernel's runs and everything else intact, a `scrub` boot, swap on from the settings, a part block and a file a snapshot shares refused; 39 checks), proven by 11 broken variants (the kernel's check, the host's check or the scrub verifying the file's data; opening, removing or copying allowed; pages not encrypted; the tweak not advancing; runs only going up; shared blocks accepted; the setting ignored). Scoped tests passed |
| 35b | paging out (roadmap item 35, the names agreed at 35a): with swap on, the kernel thread `pageout` wakes when free memory falls below a low mark (a 64th of memory, between 1 and 64 MiB) and works until twice it: one process at a time (by ID, `process_pageout`), a batch of up to 32 of its own pages (anonymous memory and copy-on-write copies; not the page cache's, not shared memory, nothing of a process that forked: 35c), a clock over its pages (`pageout_at`): a page used since the last look only has its mark cleared (x86's accessed bit; ARM's access flag, whose next use faults and is answered by `paging_user_touch`), an unused one is taken out of the page table (a swap entry: not present, bit 1, the slot), announced to every core, written encrypted through one bounce page and freed. A fault on a swap entry reads the page back (holding the mappings to write); unmap and exit give the slots back; fork reads the parent's back first; `swap off` reads every process's back (refused with ENOMEM when they do not fit). A fault that finds no page below the reserve (a quarter of the low mark) waits for `pageout` without the lock, ten times a second at most, and takes the reserve only once pageout has looked at twice the machine's pages without writing one. Found on the way: under paging the scattered free pages left no 8-page run for a kernel stack, and `kill` could not start ("Cannot allocate memory"): with swap on, programs' pages now come from the top of memory down (`pmm_alloc_page_high`), the kernel's runs from the bottom up; and pageout's first test of being stuck (two rounds of one call per process) gave up on a large program after 4096 of its pages. A process is freed, and exec renews its memory, only once neither pageout nor swap off is at it. The boot option `memory=N` gives out only N MiB. The test program `swaptest MIB [ROUNDS [fork]]` writes and checks every word of MIB MiB per round. Measured in QEMU with `memory=128` and a 192 MiB swap file, `swaptest 144`: both rounds every word as written, about 121,000 pages written to swap and 115,000 read back on ARM (126,000 and 120,000 on x86), a child forked after the rounds read its 16 MiB with 4,113 pages brought back, a run killed with 10,000 pages in swap left them free, `swap off` read the last 37 back; x86's emulation pages at about 0.5 MB/s (software AES). Tests: `swap_shell_test`'s fourth boot (50 checks in all), proven on ARM by 5 broken variants (no read-back at the fault, slots kept at unmap, `swap off` without reading back, fork without reading back, `memory=` ignored). Scoped tests passed |
| 35c | pages forked processes share (roadmap item 35): a slot now has a count of the page-table entries naming it, so a fork copies the parent's swap entries into the child (each naming the same slot) instead of reading them back first (35b's limit: a program could fork only if its pages in swap fitted in memory). A page a fork shares (read-only in every holder until one writes, so its content cannot change) goes out of one holder's entry at a time: written once, the slot remembered with the page in the forked pages' table, and the next holder's entry names it without a write; the page is freed with its last holder. A page read back from a slot other entries still name stays linked to it, read-only, so going out again it is not written a second time; a write in place first unlinks it (page_own: the copy-on-write fault and mprotect). Found on the way: a fork's child was never pageable (35b's flag was set only when a program is loaded), so pageout looked at 65,000 pages without writing one and the child's faults ran out of memory; and faults now wait for pageout as long as it frees pages (a minute without, or pageout stuck, before the reserve), since freeing a page held by two takes two looks. Shared anonymous memory (MAP_SHARED | MAP_ANONYMOUS, used in the tree only by forktest) still stays in memory: paging it out needs every holder's entry changed at once, and RitterOS keeps no record of who maps a page (a reverse map). Measured in QEMU (`memory=128`, 192 MiB of swap): `swaptest 144 2 fork` forks with about 10,000 slots in use; the child reads all 144 MiB, writes its own round and reads it back, the parent then reads its own again (ARM: 110,850 pages read back in that step; x86: 114,464; every word as written). Tests: `swap_shell_test`'s fork step (50 checks), proven on ARM by 5 broken variants (a fork not holding the slot; a shared page freed with holders left: the kernel panicked, a double free; a fork's child not pageable; a page's slot never given back: 36,916 slots stayed in use; a write not unlinking the page). Scoped tests passed |
| 35c-2 | shared anonymous memory goes to swap too (the user's option b, after 35c: "I don't know what programs will do"): the pages of a MAP_SHARED | MAP_ANONYMOUS mapping belong to an object (`struct anon_obj`) that every mapping of it holds, a fork's child too, so a page-table entry is only a view of the object's page, made at a fault (fork no longer makes every page of it first). pageout collects such pages it finds unused in a process and then, holding no mappings' lock, marks one leaving (a fault on it waits, without its lock), clears its entry in every process that maps the object, one process at a time (`process_each_space`, ending processes too), and writes it to swap unless one of them used it meanwhile; the object keeps the slot, and a fault reads the page back into it. A mapping lets go of its object only after its entries were announced gone (the last one frees the pages and slots); swap off reads the objects' pages back too. Measured in QEMU (`memory=128`, 192 MiB of swap): `swaptest 144 3 shared`, a forked child writing 144 MiB of shared memory three times and the parent reading it: every word as written, 93,556 pages written to swap and 83,941 read back on ARM (x86: 102,948 and 92,798). Not tested: swap off with shared memory still in swap (the test machine has no room to read it all back). Tests: `swap_shell_test`'s shared step (52 checks in all), proven on ARM by 4 broken variants (entries left in place when a page leaves: the program faulted on a page not present; fork not holding the object: the kernel panicked, a double free; a page in swap not read back; shared pages never collected: the program ran out of memory). Scoped tests passed |
| 35d | swap measured on the machines (the user's option 1: their real memory, not `memory=`; the swap files removed afterwards). The J1900 (8 GiB, SATA SSD): `swap make 10G` took 279 s (~37 MB/s of zeros); `swaptest 9216 2` round 1 written in 723.6 s (most of it fits in memory), read back in 8,649.3 s; round 2 written in 8,593.8 s, read back in 8,598.9 s; every word as written; 8,482,887 pages written to swap and 8,089,629 read back in 7.4 hours: ~2.5 MB/s both ways together, ~1.6 ms a page. The laptop (32 GiB, NVMe): `swap make 36G` took 258 s (~143 MB/s); `swaptest 34816 2` round 1 written in 277.2 s, read back in 6,037.2 s, every word as written; stopped by the user after 3 h 53 min in round 2's read-back (22,002,316 pages written, 21,236,576 read: 12.7 MB/s, steady from the first minutes). From a USB stick with `memory=1024` and 512 MiB of swap (the file in 1,091 runs): `swaptest 1200 2` 35.1 s / 283.2 s and 277.7 s / 277.4 s, every word as written, ~9.1 MB/s. What the numbers say: a sweep over more memory than the machine has sends every page through swap, not only the part that does not fit (the clock takes the pages ahead of the sweep), so each sweep costs the whole size; a write to a page in swap reads it back first (the kernel cannot know the whole page will be overwritten), so round 2's writing takes as long as a read. Open: on 2026-11-02 the laptop switched itself off completely (no crash on the screen) under heavy disk work, with several kernels (35a on plain writes, 35c-2 paging to NVMe or to a stick, `nomodules`, the clock left to the firmware); not the processor's load or heat (16 threads busy survived), not a stray kernel write to the chipset's power-management page (0xFED80000 mapped read-only: no fault). On 2026-11-03 the same kernel and stick survived every run (two with that probe, two without, and the 3 h 53 min on NVMe), the battery at the same charge: a hardware or power question, not reproduced. No code changed |
| 35e | swap on but unused costs nothing measurable (the user asked whether a system could always have a swap file without an effect on speed). Measured on the laptop before: 16 programs faulting in 1 GiB each at once took 1.93 s with swap off, 2.08 s with swap on (+8%; each program's writing 1.3 -> 1.5 s), with 3.7 million waits for the page allocator's lock (`pmm`, 5.6 s of spinning) against 0.12 million (0.36 s): with swap on, every program page came from the top of memory (`pmm_alloc_page_high`, 35b) under that lock, past each core's own free pages (65e-47); 16,000 program starts showed no difference. Now each core keeps its own pages from the top too (struct cpu's `high`, `PMM_HIGH` 32 at a time, under its `pmm cache` lock; given back pages still go to the core's other cache, so only pages from the top go to programs): with swap on 1.85-1.87 s and ~117,000 waits (0.20-0.29 s), as with swap off (1.88-1.89 s, ~119,000; three runs each). The x86 swap test then failed: with swaptest filling memory, no program could start (`sleep`, `swap`, even `kill`: Cannot allocate memory), and the committed kernel did the same in a short session (it had passed by luck). The cause, found with probes on the allocator: spawn and exec took a 128 KiB buffer for the arguments (`ARGS_MAX` since 64j), 33 pages in a row, and while paging the 133-176 free pages lay scattered. Now the arguments (and the environment) go into a buffer of one page first (`copy_strings_sized`, 4,000 bytes), the whole size only when they do not fit. Tests: exec_shell_test's line of 6,060 bytes of arguments (sh spawned with them, echo exec'd with 60); proven by three broken kernels (a page from the top given out twice: the ARM swap test panicked with a double free; only the one-page buffer: Argument list too long; straight to 128 KiB: the x86 swap test's Cannot allocate memory again). Scoped tests passed on both architectures (swap, exec, spawn, mmap, malloc, fork, lockcheck, smpstress, selfbuild); installed on the laptop |
| 36a | the Intel Wi-Fi driver begins (`drivers/intelwifi.c`, the module `intelwifi` for the Dual Band Wireless-AC 8265, 8086:24fd; Intel's firmware `iwlwifi-8265-36.ucode` in `firmware/` with its licence, installed in `/system/firmware/`). The driver takes the card, clears PCI_RETRY_TIMEOUT, makes the chip ready (NIC_READY, else PREPARE), resets it and wakes its MAC (the GIO chicken bits, HPET, HAP_WAKE_L1A, INIT_DONE, the MAC clock polled), reads the silicon step through the periphery window (the power block WFPM on first, then AUX_MISC), and on its own thread lists the firmware file's parts. Nothing is loaded into the chip yet. On the other laptop (an ASUS VivoBook; no Ethernet port, so a stick and photos): hardware revision 0x230 (type 0x23), subsystem 8086:0010, ready at once, the MAC clock after 613-628 us (the limit 25 ms), the radio switch on, GP_CNTRL 0x8040005; WFPM_CTRL 0x0, then 0x80000000; AUX_MISC 0x10 (silicon step 0). Without WFPM on, AUX_MISC read 0xA5A5A5A2, a filler (the first stick: the proof). The firmware: 63 parts, version 36.c8e8e144.0, 2 CPUs; run-time image 4 + 4 + 2 paged sections (477,956, 158,564, 160,408 bytes), init image 5 + 4 + 2, wake-on-WLAN 4 + 4 + 2; antennas 0x3/0x3, 52 scan channels; every line as the Mac's reading of the file. No QEMU model: the test machines list `intelwifi.mod` as not needed and carry the firmware; the self-build builds the module |
| 36b-1 | the Intel 8265's firmware runs (`intelwifi`): on the driver's thread the init image goes into the chip through its DMA engine's service channel (CPU1's 5 sections and CPU2's 4, 676,972 bytes in 11 loads of up to 128 KiB, ~18 ms, each load's end an MSI; FH_UCODE_LOAD_STATUS marks every section), its CPUs start, and the firmware's "alive" comes through the receive ring (256 buffers of 4 KiB; the chip writes how far it got to a status in memory): status 0xCAFE, LMAC 36.c8e8e144 (the file's version), UMAC 25.0, its error log at 0x813A6C, the UMAC's at 0x88105C, the scheduler at 0x817080, 33.5 ms after the load; then one more notification (0xB1, MFUART loaded); 14 interrupts, none missed. Found on the way, on four sticks: Linux's order matters (the step read with WFPM on, then the whole chip reset with 5 ms after it, which clears WFPM and NIC_READY; NIC_READY set again and the MAC powered up again before the load), and the firmware stays silent (secure boot CPU1 0x3, CPU2 0x29F6; no interrupt, nothing in the ring) until the transmit side is set up as Linux's tx_init does it: every queue's ring (31 of 32 KiB), the keep-warm page, the scheduler's FIFOs off and its mode (queues active by themselves, 31 of them). A loader bug fixed meanwhile: a load's end seen both by the interrupt and in the register counted for the next load too. Nothing is sent to the firmware yet. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36b-2 | the Intel 8265's firmware takes commands (`intelwifi`): after "alive" the transmit side starts as Linux's tx_start (the scheduler's context cleared, its byte-count tables, the command queue 0 on FIFO 7 with window and frame limit 64, every FIFO and DMA channel on); a command is a descriptor on queue 0 (its first 20 bytes in one buffer, the rest in a second) and its reply comes through the receive ring with the same sequence number. On the other laptop: the scheduler at 0x817080 as "alive" said; ECHO answered in 76 us; the NVM read with NVM_ACCESS, 2 KiB at a time: 5,772 bytes in 1.38 ms, sections 1 (64 bytes), 3 (2,584), 4 (2,068), 5 (200), 6 (44), 7 (256), 10 (548), 12 (8); version 0x101B. The card's data as Linux reads it for the 8000 family: MAC 02:aa:bb:cc:00:03 from the fused registers (no MAC override section), 4 addresses; radio 0x33020303 (type 32, step 3, dash 0, flavor 3), antennas send 0x3 and receive 0x3 (as the firmware file's); SKU 0xF: 2.4 and 5 GHz, 11n, 11ac, MIMO; 13 of 14 channels at 2.4 GHz, 33 of 37 at 5 GHz (32 with 80 MHz); LAR on (the firmware decides the country's rules). Paging moves to 36b-3: Linux gives paged memory only to the run-time image. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36b-3a | the Intel 8265's init firmware calibrates (`intelwifi`), as Linux's iwl_run_init_mvm_ucode for the 8000 family (no Bluetooth setting there): TX_ANT_CONFIGURATION with the antennas to send with (the file's and the NVM's: 0x3), then PHY_CONFIGURATION with the file's PHY SKU narrowed to those antennas (0x330018) and the init image's calibration triggers from the file's defaults (flow 0x1B8E00DB, event 0x0); the results come as PHY database parts until "init complete". On the other laptop: replies in 113 and 83 us; init complete after 61.5 ms; 17 parts, 27,352 bytes: the configuration (36 bytes), the channels (4), 7 PAPD channel groups (10,864) and 8 TX power groups (16,448), all kept for the run-time image (36b-3b). Corrected: the NVM's version is 0x101B (36b-2's row said 0x1018: misread from the photo). No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36b-3b | the Intel 8265's run-time firmware runs (`intelwifi`), as Linux's iwl_mvm_load_rt_fw and the start of iwl_mvm_up: after "init complete" the chip is stopped as _iwl_trans_pcie_stop_device (the debug engine's sampling, interrupts, the 8 transmit DMA channels and the receive DMA until idle, the bus master, two resets of 5 ms, NIC_READY again), started again (reset, MAC up) and the run-time image loaded the same way (the rings and pages made once and reused); after its "alive" the transmit side starts again, then paging (as iwl_save_fw_paging and iwl_send_paging_cmd: the paged part's 664-byte signature in a page, its 159,744 bytes in 5 blocks of 32 KiB aligned to 32 KiB, the last holding 7 pages; FW_PAGING_BLOCK_CMD, group 1 with the 8-byte header: flags secured, enabled, 7; block size 2^15; the addresses >> 12; the pages stay the firmware's), then the antennas, the 17 kept calibration results (PHY_DB_CMD each: the configuration, the channels, the PAPD groups, the TX power groups) and the PHY configuration with the run-time triggers (flow 0x88E10DB, event 0xB02909B). On the other laptop: the stop's DMA idle at once, the bus master off after 6 us; the run-time image's 8 sections, 636,520 bytes in 10 loads, 16.8 ms; alive (0xCAFE, image type 1; its error log at 0x811A94) 30.8 ms after; paging answered in 19.7 ms; antennas in 116 us; the 17 parts sent in 4.4 ms; the PHY configuration answered in 1.6 ms; no firmware error. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36c-1 | the Intel 8265's firmware finishes its start-up (`intelwifi`), as the rest of Linux's iwl_mvm_up for this firmware (its capability bits now kept from the file: EXTEND_SHARED_MEM_CFG, CT_KILL_BY_FW, TEMP_THS_REPORT_SUPPORT, CTDP_SUPPORT, BT_MPLUT_SUPPORT, the API's STA_TYPE): before the antennas and calibrations, the shared memory's layout asked (the system group's command; 100 bytes: RX FIFOs 45,056 and 32,768 bytes, TX FIFOs 50,048 in all; 132 us) and the Smart FIFO set "init off" (Linux's watermarks and timers; 90 us); after them, Bluetooth coexistence (modules 0x15; 121 us), DQA (the command queue 0; 76 us), the scanning station as iwl_mvm_add_aux_sta (its queue 1 on the multicast FIFO 5: our pointers and SCD_QUEUE_CFG, 174 us; ADD_STA version 10, station 1, type auxiliary, MAC index 4: status 0x1 in 103 us), 3 PHY contexts on channel 1 at 20 MHz (send 0x3, receive 0x2806; 270 us), the temperature thresholds (none: the firmware does CT-kill itself; 74 us) and its power budget (cTDP 2000 mW; 90 us, status 0), LTR only when the card's PCIe link has it on (Device Control 2 bit 10: off on that laptop, not sent), the device's power saving (Linux's default scheme; 88 us). No firmware error, no notification in the second after. Commands with the 8-byte header now go to groups 2, 4 and 5 too. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36c-2 | the Intel 8265 is `wlan0` and scans (`intelwifi`): at the end of the start-up the firmware's current country is asked as Linux's first query (MCC_UPDATE "ZZ" with the source "get current": the firmware's "00", 38 of the NVM's 51 channels allowed, 13 at 2.4 GHz and 25 at 5 GHz), the scan's settings sent as iwl_mvm_config_scan (version 1: chains, the 12 legacy rates, Linux's dwell times, the scanning station, the 51 channels; 802 us), the station's MAC context added as Linux does when the interface comes up (not associated, other networks' beacons let in; 890 us), then the card registered with the Wi-Fi layer (its channels the allowed ones). The driver's thread stays: `scan` (the layer's operation) asks it, and it sends the firmware's UMAC scan (version 7 for ADAPTIVE_DWELL: passive, one round, pass all, adaptive dwell), hands every frame (RX_PHY, then RX_MPDU: the channel, the strongest antenna's energy as the signal, the FCS checked) to `wlan_receive` and ends the scan at SCAN_COMPLETE_UMAC (or after 15 s). On the other laptop `wifi scan` listed 19 networks (17 WPA2, 2 open; 15 at 2.4 GHz on channels 1-11, 4 at 5 GHz on 44 and 157; -45 to -83 dBm): the scan of 38 channels took 4,480 ms, 75 frames heard, 70 beacons, none with a bad FCS. Found on the way (two sticks of logs): the UMAC stopped with BAD_COMMAND on the command after the country query, its header garbage (the LMAC's log: NMI_INTERRUPT_UMAC_FATAL, 0x71); MCC_UPDATE is 28 bytes and 32 were sent, so the firmware read every later header out of step. Both error logs are now printed with Linux's field names, and a command without a reply shows the queue (the chip's read pointer, the descriptor, the command's bytes). Not yet: sending and tuning by hand (36d), an active scan by name. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36d-1 | the Intel 8265 tunes and sends (`intelwifi`), as Linux when a station connects: `set_channel` moves PHY context 0 to the channel (20 MHz), binds our MAC context to it the first time (then the MAC's power: saving allowed, keep-alive 25 s) and protects the session (TIME_EVENT: 600 TU, at most 500 TU away, renewed before each authentication and association unless 400 TU are left, as mgd_prepare_tx); the first frame to an access point gives our MAC context its BSSID, adds it as station 0 (type link) and enables DQA's first management queue (5) on the voice FIFO for it (our pointers, SCD_QUEUE_CFG, the station's queues); every frame goes out as a TX command (56 bytes, the header after it padded to 4 bytes; buffer 0 its first 20 bytes with the scratch field the chip writes, buffer 1 the rest, buffer 2 the body; the band's lowest rate on each antenna in turn; the scheduler's byte count), broadcast frames through the scanning station; the TX response's status is logged for management frames and failures. `transmit` queues the frame (8 at most) for the driver's thread: waiting for it would have held the layer's lock, which the thread takes to hand received frames up. The country is the firmware's (LAR): when it announces one (MCC_CHUB_UPDATE) it is confirmed back as Linux does; `wifi country` is saved but not applied on this card. On the other laptop `wifi join` to a WPA2 network on channel 153 (5 GHz): the firmware chose "US" after hearing it; tuning (PHY context 825 us, binding 2.0 ms, session protection 1.3 ms); the access point's station and queue (status 0x1); authentication and association request each acknowledged at the first try (6 Mbit/s, antennas A then B); authenticated, associated (AID 14), the handshake done, joined, the link up. Then the session protection ended and DHCP got no answer: the firmware does not know of the association yet (36d-2). No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36d-2 | the Intel 8265 carries IP (`intelwifi`): the card's `join` operation (the layer calls it with the AID after the association response) tells the firmware of the association as Linux after mac80211's station state ASSOC and BSS_CHANGED_ASSOC: the access point's station updated (AID; HT/VHT: streams, A-MPDU size and density; 20 MHz for now), a rate table (LQ_CMD) of legacy rates from 54 Mbit/s down to 6 starting where the access point's signal allows (Linux's own rate scaling later), the MAC context associated (the beacon interval, DTIM, the TSF and the chip's time of the access point's last beacon, captured as they come; QoS and HT flags; no foreign beacons), the binding's whole airtime (TIME_QUOTA, 128 of 128: without it the firmware left the channel when the session protection ended and DHCP got no answer), the Smart FIFO "full on", multicast passed, the session protection stopped; data frames to the access point then use its rate table (STA_RATE). On the other laptop `wifi join` to a WPA2 network on channel 153: associated (AID 11, VHT, WMM, 2 streams; beacon every 100 TU, DTIM 3; -49 dBm, so the table starts at 54 Mbit/s); station 138 us, rate table 157 us, MAC context 477 us, quota 340 us; DHCP gave an address, neighbours answered ARP, and pings reached servers outside the network. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36d-3 | the Intel 8265's data on queues of their own (`intelwifi`), as Linux's DQA: after the association a QoS data frame goes on its TID's queue, opened at its first frame (the station's reserved queue 4 for the first TID, the next free of 10-30 for others; on its access category's FIFO: TIDs 0 and 3 best effort, 1 and 2 background, 4 and 5 video, 6 and 7 voice; our pointers at the frame's sequence number, SCD_QUEUE_CFG with aggregation allowed and window 64, then the station's queue mask); management frames stay on queue 5. A ring of 256 refuses a frame while 240 have no TX response yet (a slot written over would be sent wrong; counted). `net` shows the driver's lines (`details`): the firmware version from the file, the country the firmware chose, channel, AID, signal; frames sent, acknowledged and failed, by queue; frames received and with a bad FCS; interrupts, firmware errors, frames refused. On the other laptop: "TID 0's data on queue 4 (FIFO 1) from 2: 181 us; queues set 147 us"; 22 frames sent, 22 acknowledged (queue 4: 20, queue 5: 2, the authentication and association request), 546 received, 0 bad; 505 interrupts (3 found in the register), no firmware error; pings 5 of 5 (15-31 ms). Speed measurements wait for the Mac on that network. No QEMU model: the test machines list the module as not needed; the self-build builds it |
| 36d-3b | a machine whose only network card comes after login gets its network elves (`netnamesd`): login runs `net up` only when a card is there at its start, and the Intel 8265's wlan0 registers about 5 s later (its firmware starts on the driver's thread), so the other laptop had dhclient (50h) but no dnsd, sshd or guardd, and the kernel let nothing in (`guard`: "no rules loaded yet: nothing comes in"; ping and ssh to it timed out). Now a new card on a machine where `net up` never ran (no file for guardd) also gets the files `net up` would make: dnsd, sshd, and guardd last (its first start then lets in ping, tcp 22, udp 53). `netnames_shell_test`'s machine without a card at the boot checks it (the old netnamesd failed exactly so). On the other laptop: ssh came in at once; the first measurements over the 8265 (20 MHz, legacy rates): download 0.84 MB/s, upload 1.02 MB/s (the Mac counted the same), 30,001 frames all acknowledged, 96 refused by a full ring (240 in the chip), 184 fast resends |
| 36e-1 | the Intel 8265 receives in bursts (`intelwifi`): the card's `block_ack` operation for receiving, as Linux's iwl_mvm_sta_rx_agg (ADD_STA modify of the access point's station: STA_MODIFY_ADD_BA_TID with the TID, the first sequence number and the window, or STA_MODIFY_REMOVE_BA_TID), done on the driver's thread; the layer keeps the order (this family's firmware reorders nothing). Sending in bursts is refused for now (36e's last step). The receive loop is a batch for the stack (`net_batch_begin`/`end`, at most NET_BATCH_MAX frames: one TCP acknowledgement per burst, 1,429 segments sent for 8,813 received instead of 6,886 for 6,870), and the driver's thread wakes for frames handed to it (its wait looked only at the chip: a frame waited up to 20 ms). `net` adds the agreements, the frames refused (no place to wait, not sent) and how data comes in (RX_PHY's rate_n_flags and A-MPDU flag). On the other laptop: "Block Ack for receiving TID 0 on (from 1, up to 64 frames): status 0x1, 149 us"; 10,006 of 10,520 data frames in bursts; but downloads 0.84 -> 0.62 MB/s: the access point sends at VHT MCS 1, one stream, 20 MHz although the signal is -45 dBm, since it runs 40 MHz and the card's PHY context 20 (its wide tries fail): 40/80 MHz is next |

On the other laptop the access point then sent at VHT MCS 8, two streams, 40 MHz instead of MCS 1 at 20 MHz, nothing out of order; but downloads stayed ~1 MB/s. The driver's new counters showed why: TX responses seen after up to 365 ms, a receive pass up to 270 ms, the driver's thread busy 92% (9.2 s of 10): ~1.3 ms per frame, the layer's bitsliced, constant-time AES opening CCMP in software. Receiving: RX_MPDU's status CCM with MIC OK: the CCMP header and MIC taken off, the packet number and key index to `wlan_receive_decrypted`; anything else goes up whole for the layer. The serving loop ends when the firmware reports an error, two commands in a row have no reply, or a queue's oldest frame waited 5 s; then its error logs are written, the layer is told the link is gone (`wlan_link_lost`: wifid joins again), the driver's state of the association, queues, keys, agreements, waiting frames and requests is forgotten, and the boot's path runs again (Linux's restart runs the init image and calibration again too), wlan0 kept; paging reuses its blocks and the PHY database is emptied first. | 36e-8 | TCP closes with a RST when received data is unread (`kernel/tcp.c`, RFC 1122 4.2.2.13, as Linux and the BSDs), and answers data that comes after the close (FIN_WAIT_1/2, CLOSING) with one: found measuring 36e, where every download after the first seemed to halve with both cards. A client that stopped reading closed with a FIN and left its window at zero, so `netrate serve`'s write waited on it until FIN_WAIT_2's limit (5 s) freed the closed side: ~6 s without data for the next client (over the cable too: 0 MB/s for 6 s, then 36 MB/s). The RST goes out through release() (a closed connection is freed by the tcp thread afterwards, not at once: that would drop it with the outbox). `net`'s tcp line counts them ("N reset with data unread"). `netrate_test` downloads twice in a row from `netrate serve` (proven by a TCP that still sends a FIN: the second got 0 bytes) |

Waiting for a frame alone failed: right after an association the firmware passes few beacons up, and the first version took the link down. | 38a | virtual cards (roadmap item 38; the user: "a virtual Ethernet card that can be owned by a program, i want to have a tool to be able to create as many as needed"): `net tap add [user NAME]` (root) makes `tap0`, `tap1`, ... (the lowest number free, up to `tap63`; SYS_TAP 113, `ritteros_tap`), a card of the stack like any other (`kernel/tap.c`: driver `tap`, a random locally administered MAC each time it is made) whose wire is a program: it opens `/dev/tapN` (FILE_TAP) and reads one frame per `read` (what the stack sent: a ring of 256 KiB made at the open, freed at the close) and writes one per `write` (14-1514 bytes, handed to `net_receive` as a card's received frame); `poll` works. One holder at a time (`Device or resource busy`), only root and the card's user (`Permission denied`); the link is up (10000 Mbit/s) while it is held. A frame written for another MAC is dropped, as a card's filter would, and counted in `net` ("the program's side" line). `net tap remove TAP` (not while held: "a program has it open"); a removed tap keeps its place in the card list, hidden, for the next one made (its memory never freed: senders may hold it). A tap gets no settings file made for it; one written by hand is applied by `net up`. The card list grows from 16 to 64 (MAX_NETDEVS; dhclient's too; `net`'s report up to 64 KiB). Test `tap_shell_test` (27 checks, x86 and ARM): `taptest` on tap0 answers ARP and three pings as 192.168.77.2, busy and another user refused, removal and reuse; proven by a kernel that let a second program open the card (it failed exactly that check). |
| 38b | bridges (roadmap item 38; the user: "a bridge in the network stack"): `net bridge add PORT...` (root; SYS_BRIDGE 114, `ritteros_bridge`) makes `br0` (up to `br7`, 16 ports each; `kernel/bridge.c`), a card of the stack with its first port's MAC address, whose ports are wired cards and taps (a Wi-Fi card is refused: an 802.11 client carries only its own MAC address). A port's received frames go to `bridge_input` instead of the stack (`net_receive`, the port's `bridge` pointer): the source is learned (256 addresses, forgotten after 300 s unheard, as 802.1D), a frame goes out the port its destination is on, or out every other port when not known and for broadcast/multicast; a frame for br0's MAC, or a group frame, goes up the stack as br0's. A port carries no traffic of its own: `net_link` (the stack's look at a link: routes, IPv6, dhclient through netinfo) sees none on it, its IPv4 settings go and netset refuses it (`net up`: "a bridge's port: its own settings are not used"). Ports take every frame: the new card operation `set_promiscuous` (driver interface 6.1: struct net_card grew at its end, recorded in abicheck's agreed list as the user chose; 0: refused as a port) in e1000 and igb (RCTL.UPE and MPE), rtl8168 (AcceptAllPhys and an open multicast hash, kept across its resets), usbnet (its NCM filter took everything already) and taps; promiscuous mode and the multicast filter are changed one at a time ("card filters"). No spanning tree; a frame with the bridge's own MAC coming back is dropped and counted. `net` shows the bridge's ports, the addresses it knows, frames passed on, sent to every port, kept on their own port, come back; under a port "a port of br0". `net bridge add br0 PORT...`, `net bridge remove br0 [PORT...]` (the ports' own DHCP comes back by itself). `taptest` also pings a target with a MAC of its own (02:00:00:00:00:NN). Test `bridge_test` (27 checks, x86 and ARM): br0 = QEMU's e1000 + two taps takes eth0's address by DHCP; the program on tap0 pings QEMU's gateway through the e1000 (its answers come to the program's MAC: promiscuous mode) and the program on tap1 (port to port); the refusals; ports leave and eth0 gets its address again. Proven by an e1000 whose set_promiscuous did nothing (exactly the gateway's two checks failed). |
| 38b-2 | the firewall names taps and bridges (found on the J1900: `guard add ... out tap0` said "'tap0': not a card (like eth0)"; the user: "fix the firewall names"): a rule's card is still one byte (ethN, 0x80 | N for wlanN), and since 38b each rule says what kind of card it names in its reserved bytes, now `kinds[3]` (the card's and the outgoing card's: GUARD_KIND_ETH, GUARD_KIND_TAP, GUARD_KIND_BRIDGE; a drop's reserved word is its `card_kind`), compatible for programs built before (abicheck: reserved fields taken into use). `on tap0`, `in br0`, `out tap1` are understood, matched by the kernel (`names_card`) and shown again in `guard` and its last drops; the message is now "not a card (like eth0, wlan0, tap0, br0)". On the J1900 the Mac had pinged a program on tap0 through RitterOS's forwarding (5 of 5, TTL 62: routed both ways), with the rule written by addresses. tap_shell_test (32 checks): a rule `drop out icmp on tap0` stops the pings to the program on tap0 (its drops say tap0) and a forward rule `in eth0 out br3` is read back; proven by a kernel that could not name taps (4 checks failed). |
| 38b-3 | taps and bridges as any card everywhere (the user: "Bridges and taps should be threated as any physical interface everywhere"; "remember the SDK, other programs should be aware of the taps and bridges and how to use them"): `net default` (SYS_DEFAULT_ROUTE) takes tapN and brN as it takes ethN and wlanN ("not a card's name (ethN, wlanN, tapN or brN)"); `net up` makes a tap's settings file (`address = dhcp`) as any card's (38a made none); `guardload` names taps and bridges in its drops. The names file stays the physical cards' (a tap's MAC is made anew each time). The SDK guide (`sdk/CLAUDE.md`) has a part on network cards, virtual cards and bridges: how a program lists them (`ritteros_netcard`, `ritteros_netinfo`; a port shows no link), makes them (`ritteros_tap`, `ritteros_bridge`, their errors) and is a tap's wire, with an example built for both architectures with the SDK. bridge_test (30 checks): `net default br0` and the route on br0, `net default auto`; proven by a kernel that refused br names (exactly those 2 failed). tap_shell_test (33): tap1's settings file made. |
| 38c | taps and bridges kept for the next start (roadmap item 38): `/system/config/network/virtual` (`tapN = user NAME`, `brN = PORT...`; its default text in common/settings_defaults.h, written by `net up` when missing) is changed line by line by `net tap add/remove` and `net bridge add/remove` (a bridge's line follows its ports as the kernel has them) and read by `net up` before the cards' settings: taps first, each with its own number (TAP_ADD's value carries (N + 1) << 24 for tapN itself), then the bridges, each port in order (BRIDGE_ADD's number -2 - N: a new brN itself); a card not there is said and passed over. login runs `net up` also on a machine without a card when the file names one. The user's "fix the print of DHCP line": `net up` asks the kernel's report whether a card is a bridge's port and says `net: eth0: a port of br0: its own settings are not used` instead of `DHCP: ...`. virtual_test (13 checks, three starts of one image, x86 and ARM): the default text, the commands' lines (tap1's taken out again), tap0 and br0 = eth0 tap0 made again with br0's DHCP and the ports said to be ports, a port's removal in the bridge's line, a start without any card that still makes tap0; proven by a net whose virtual_up did nothing (12 failed) and one that never found a port (exactly the 2 DHCP-line checks failed). |
| 38d-1 | the J1900's cards bridged (roadmap item 38, on the user's J1900 with the Acer's cable moved to its ports): br0 = eth0 eth1 eth2 took the J1900's address from the router; the Acer got its addresses through it (DHCP, DHCPv6) and pinged the router across eth1 (the 82576's second port) and eth2 (the RTL8168G): promiscuous mode works on both real chips (RCTL 0x0400801a: UPE, MPE; RxConfig 0x...0f: AcceptAllPhys). Measured with netrate against the J1900: by the switch 36.8 MB/s from it, 82 to it; through the bridge's eth1 21.9 and 66; eth2 linked at 100 Mbit/s only (both Realtek ends) and carried the line's 11.1 and 11.4. To find why, read only: rtl8168's report has the PHY's registers (BMSR, ESTATUS, the advertisement ANAR and 1000BASE-T control, the partner's), the bridge's how often its link was asked and its ports' links read. And a removed tap or bridge is no card by name any more (card_named), so `net up` made no settings file for it (it made one for the J1900's removed tap0). tap_shell_test (33) proven by a kernel still finding removed taps; bridge_test (31) checks the new line. |
| 38d-2 | what 38d-1's counters showed on the J1900, fixed: (1) while sending 11 MB/s through br0 the stack asked for its link ~65,700 times a second (about 9 times a frame: routes, sends), each reading its three ports' link registers (~197,000 chip reads a second on the Celeron): the bridge now keeps its ports' fastest link and reads them again at most once a timer tick (10 ms; at once after a port joins or leaves). bridge_test (32): over a download of 1,000,000 bytes br0's link was asked 237 times and its ports read 45 (x86; ARM 247 and 69); proven by a bridge without it (711, three a time). (2) The J1900's RTL8168G linked at 100 Mbit/s with a gigabit partner: its PHY can do 1000BASE-T (ESTATUS 0x2000) and the partner offered it (0x0800), but its 1000BASE-T control was 0x0000 (the firmware's; the driver never set it): rtl8168's phy_on advertises what the PHY says it can, as Linux's PHY layer does (10/100 from BMSR into ANAR, 1000BASE-T from ESTATUS into its control register). QEMU's model has no gigabit PHY registers: checked on the J1900. |
| 38d-3 | every card's link asked of its driver at most once a tick (the user: "option 1 for now to improve speed on all interfaces"): 38d-2's bridge cache moved into the stack, `net_link`, for every card (eth, wlan, tap, br): the stack asks for a link several times a packet (on the J1900 ~9 a frame sent), and a driver's answer is often a register read (igb's STATUS, the Realtek's PHYstatus); it is now asked at most once a timer tick (10 ms), the answer kept meanwhile (the thread link and net's report still ask the driver itself). After 38d-2's bridge cache alone, the J1900 sent 73.7 MB/s through its bridge's Realtek port where its own eth0, asking igb every time, had sent 36.8. `net` has a line under each card: its link asked N times, M of them read from the card. bridge_test (31): a download through br0 asked its link 224 times and read its driver 15 times; proven by a net_link without the memory (231 of 231). Measured on the J1900 (eth0 alone, through the switch): sending 36.8 -> 75.3 MB/s, receiving 82 -> 80 (receiving asks for no link); through its bridge 75.0. wscale_test asked for a fourth doubling of the receive buffer within 8 MB ("past 1 MiB", its comment said three): the buffer grows only while the window holds the sender back, and the faster guest often did not need it (x86 passed 1 of 3, then 4 of 5 with 16 MB; the commit before 5 of 5): now 16,000,000 bytes and at least 1 MiB (10 of 10 on both architectures). |
| 38d-4 | a bridge made again got no IPv4 address (found on the J1900: `net bridge add eth0 eth1` at run time, br0 got its DHCPv6 address but no lease for 5 minutes, until a restart; nothing answered at the J1900's address): dhclient keeps each card's lease itself, and when the earlier br0 was removed its look returned at `no such card` (since 38d-1 a removed bridge is no card by name) without forgetting it, so the new br0 had a lease renewed hours later and a stale link. Now a card that is not there loses its lease and counts as without a link, and a card whose address is no longer the lease's (a bridge's port, a tap made again) is asked for again; dropping a port's address (refused: the kernel took it) is not reported ("Device or resource busy"). bridge_test (33): br0 removed and made again at once gets its second lease; proven by the dhclient before (1 lease, and the busy line). On the J1900, with the bridge from its file after a restart, the Acer on eth1 (the 82576's second port) at 1000 Mbit/s: through the bridge 75.8 MB/s from the J1900 (21.9 before 38d-3) and 74.5-77.0 to it (66.1; its receive ring's "no buffer" the limit, as on eth0 alone). |
| 68a | `mv` and `rename` replace an existing target, as POSIX (the user's, 2026-11-07, after SDRfi's report found that a program cannot be installed by writing `prog.new` and moving it over `prog`): the kernel's rename replaces a file by a file and an empty folder by a folder; a folder with something in it is refused (`Directory not empty`), a file over a folder `Is a directory`, a folder over a file `Not a directory`, two names of one object change nothing; in a sticky folder the replaced entry must be the caller's too (`sys_rename` asks `sticky_allows` of the target). RitterFS removes the old entry and makes the new one in the same transaction, so the commit holds one or the other, never neither; FAT and exFAT, without transactions, remove the target first under their lock. `mv` replaces as POSIX's: on one volume through rename; to another volume it removes the target first (by the same rules), then copies; FROM and TO the same file is refused (`mv: FROM and TO are the same file`, GNU's). Unchanged: a program that has a removed or replaced file open finds it ended at its next read (Unix keeps the old object until it is closed). Tests: fs_shell_test (a file over a file, its content the host's; a folder over an empty one; the three refusals; a hard link onto its other name), fat_write_test and exfat_write_test (a file and a folder replaced), links_shell_test (anna may not replace root's file in /tmp, but her own). |
| 39a | cc keeps floats and doubles in registers on x86-64 (roadmap item 39, part 1, the user's): they join the vectors in xmm8-xmm15 (the allocator's second pool; not in an ms_abi function, whose caller keeps xmm6-xmm15), are loaded and stored straight between memory and their registers (`movss (%r8,%rbx,4), %xmm9`), computed in their own register (in xmm0 only when the result takes its right operand's register and the operation does not commute), copied whole with movaps, a zero made by xorps and any other constant through rax; negation and fabs through an integer register, long double conversions through the scratch slot, inline assembly's "r" operands of a float as its bits. A float alive across a call still lives on the stack (a call keeps no xmm register). Both architectures: an integer constant made a float (`float s = 0`) is the constant itself when a double holds it exactly. The J1900 (my own versions of the report's kernels, ns an element; old cc, new cc, clang): dot 8.08, 3.61, 1.72; double dot 8.42, 3.89, 1.85; a 19-tap FIR on complex floats 267.7, 152.6, 46.8 (the rest is part 3's address arithmetic); a complex rotation 54.3, 23.6, 15.0; compare-and-select 24.6, 11.3, 5.4. The system built by our cc (TOOLCHAIN=ritteros) passed math, complex, libc, js, image, png, jpeg, audio, video, awk, string, ibrowser and programs (643 checks on each architecture). The user's choice: what is installed stays built by clang until cc is good enough. Tests: `cc` (cctest's new part: more floats alive than registers, values kept across calls, `t = b - t`, NaN compares, conversions, register negation, fabs and sqrt, constants; proven by subtraction and division taken as commutative, and by unsigned constants folded as signed), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both; our as encodes the new code byte for byte as LLVM |
| 39b | cc inlines small static functions with structure, union, `__int128` and `_Complex` parameters and results (item 39, part 2): the arguments are copied into the parameters' places, a `return` into the call's own result buffer (a call of such a function already has one). x86-64: zeroing up to 128 bytes (every `struct cf r = { ... }` zeroes first) is stores of zero instead of `rep stosb`, and a copy up to 128 bytes no longer keeps values out of rsi, rdi, r8 and r9 (it uses rax, r10 and r11 only). The J1900, a rotation through `static inline struct cf cf_mul(struct cf, struct cf)` (the report's kernel, my own code), ns a sample: before 39a 128.6 (the report: 128.9), now 51.1, clang 14.0; the values still go through stack slots (39b-2 next). The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests as 39a (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (cctest's inlining part: a complex multiply in a loop, nested calls, a callee changing its copy, returning its parameter, a union, `__int128`, `_Complex`, a 3-byte struct; the functions testing how structures are passed are noinline now, so the calling convention stays tested; proven by arguments not copied), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 39b-2 | cc keeps small local structures as values (item 39, part 2's rest; both architectures): a local structure, union, `__int128` or `_Complex` of up to 64 bytes whose address only reaches loads, stores, copies and zeroing of its own bytes (or a value set once to it, as an inlined callee's pointer parameter) becomes a value per field (at most 16): the places the code loads and stores, and integer fields for the bytes between them and members never named, so a copy carries every byte; locals copied into each other share one layout (an inlined call's parameters and result buffer). A union read as another member, an address that reaches a call or is stored, a copy to a shifted place and the function's own parameters stay in memory. The J1900, the rotation through `cf_mul`: before 39a 128.6 ns a sample, 39b 51.1, now 21.7, clang 14.1 (no stores to the stack left in its loop but the phase's two, the xmm registers being 8). The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (cctest's new part: a union read as another member, narrow and signed members, padding, a member never named but copied, bit-fields, a nested structure assigned, constant indexes into an array member, an address taken, copies between locals and memory, a structure in a loop, 64 and 72 bytes; proven by a split without the filler fields, which loses cctest's _Complex results), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 39c | cc makes a computation once in a block (item 39, part 3's start; both architectures): an operation, conversion or builtin whose operands (a constant by its value) are those of one before it in the block, none set again between, takes that one's value (both set only there, the later not read between them); loads are not merged (the code does not say which are volatile), nor an index's scaling by 2, 4 or 8 (free inside an address: merged it cost a copy and a shift, the dot product 3.59 -> 3.99 ns until it was left out). x86-64: a scaled address addition no load or store folds is one `leaq`. A FIR filter's x[k - i].re and .im: k - i, its extension and the address once (the inner loop 22 -> 15 instructions). The J1900, ns an element (39b-2, now, clang): dot 3.59, 3.30, 1.73; double dot 3.92, 3.68, 1.84; FIR 152.6, 117.0, 46.8; rotation 23.6, 22.9, 15.0; compare-and-select 11.3, 11.0, 5.4. Letting an address fold reach past its constant (x[k - i].im as `4(%r8,%rbx,8)`) made wrong code on the Mac (the folded addition still computed, into the index's register): left out. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (cctest's new part: an operand changed between, sign and width of conversions, a constant in two classes, chains, a structure array's members twice, division, builtins; proven by merges that ignore a changed operand and conversions merged across sign), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 39c-2 | cc's loops (item 39, part 3's rest; both architectures): a loop is a jump back to a label entered only by falling into it; what does not change in it goes before it (constants, operations, conversions, builtins, frame and global addresses whose operands are not set in the loop; never a division or a load: the loop may run no time), and an address base + sext(e) * scale with e = i + v, v + i, i - v or v - i, i a counter stepped once in the loop by a constant (a signed int's step and e: C says they cannot overflow, so the IR marks signed +, - and * now; or a long), becomes a pointer made before the loop and stepped right after i (equal addresses one pointer; e = i itself stays an extension and the addressing's scaling). A FIR filter's x[k - i]: a pointer going back 8 bytes a pass (the inner loop 15 -> 13 instructions besides the test and jump). Constants made before a loop exposed an old x86 hazard: a scaled index folded into an access through an added constant (y[k].im) while the addition between was still computed, into the index's register; an index now folds only into the access itself (cctest found it: its loops line, and cc built by itself failed).
