Tests: `cc` (cctest's madds part: a[i] * b[i], a[i] * b[k - i], a[k - i] * b[i], both going down, every length to 17, -32768 pairs, unsigned shorts and a sum read elsewhere staying plain; its first version's bounds read a volatile on every test, so nothing was packed and the proof without the reversal passed: plain bounds now; proven by the reversal left out), `cc max`, selfbuild and sdk pass on both |
| 40a | cc's results take the register of an operand whose life ends at the instruction making them (item 40's start; both architectures; the allocator's expiry: every backend reads its operands before it writes the result, which copy coalescing relied on already; not inline assembly given registers, whose template may write its output first): `movaps a, x; mulss b, x` is `mulss b, a`. It exposed an old fold in both backends: an address addition also computed for another use (F->nvalues++ in new_value: the store's place) was folded into the load right after it, reading its base's register, which the addition's own result now held: cc built by itself crashed (found by bisecting cc's files, then ir.c's functions, between the old and new compiler on the J1900); an addition now folds into an access only as its one use. The J1900, ns (39f-2, now, clang): dot 2.78, 2.42, 1.72; double dot 3.00, 2.60, 1.85; FIR 89.0, 75.6, 46.8; rotation 24.0, 19.9, 15.0; through cf_mul 22.1, 19.7, 14.0; compare-and-select 9.5, 9.05, 5.4; the int16 FIR 31.4, 30.2, 31.8; the int32 dot 3.26, 3.67 (slower: a product whose result took its right operand's register goes through rax), 1.93; saxpy 1.40, 1.39, 0.78. The system built by our cc passed the same 13 tests (643 checks on each architecture). Tests: `cc` (proven by x86's float arithmetic not handling its right operand in the result's register: cctest's float_int line), `cc max`, selfbuild (it found the fold) and sdk pass on both |
| 15j | resizing windows (roadmap item 15, the user's wish after trying the GUI; the user chose resizing from any edge or corner, and that `term` should reflow its rows and columns). A press within ~6 px of a window's edge or corner grabs it (`resize_edges_at` in `windowd`); windowd drags a light outline (cheap -- no per-step reallocation) and, on release, sends the new `WMSG_RESIZE` with the target content size. The client answers with a new shared buffer at that size via the new `WMSG_BUFFER` (sent like `WMSG_CREATE`), which windowd swaps in for the window's old one, moving it to where a top/left-edge drag left it. Clients that ignore `WMSG_RESIZE` simply do not resize (so `wclient`/`wtext` are unaffected). The toolkit gains a `WINDOW_RESIZE` event and `window_resize` (makes the new buffer, hands it over, swaps it in); `term` handles it by snapping to whole character cells, recomputing its columns and rows over a fixed max-size grid (no malloc), telling the pty the new size (`ritteros_pty_size`, so the shell and programs see it), and re-rendering -- a small lock now guards the grid/window shared by its reader thread and the resize. Test (`wresize`, both architectures): windowd launches the terminal, dragging its bottom edge down grows it to cover what was desktop (the pixel there goes from the desktop colour to the terminal's black); proven by windowd not sending the resize (the window stays its size). |
| 15i | a close box, and only the changed area is flushed (roadmap item 15, the user's wishes after trying the GUI): two things in `windowd`. (1) A close box: each window gets an `X` at the right of its title bar; clicking it makes windowd send a new `WMSG_CLOSE`, which the toolkit turns into "the window closed" (`window_wait` returns 0) so the app exits — `term` closes, and with it the GUI session. (2) The lag fix: windowd used to recomposite and flush the whole screen (1280x800x4 ~ 4 MiB to the write-combining framebuffer, under its lock) on every damage and pointer event, so two windows redrawing at once serialized behind each other's full-screen flushes. It now redraws into the back buffer (RAM, cheap) but flushes only the rectangle that changed: the damaged window, the small box around the moved pointer, or the union of a raised/moved window and the one that lost focus. Same final pixels, a fraction of the framebuffer traffic. `windowproto.h` gains `WMSG_CLOSE`; `user/lib/window.c` acts on it; `user/test/wclient.c` too. Tests (both architectures): `wclose` — clicking a window's X (pointer driven over QMP) closes it and the desktop shows through (proven by not sending the close: the window stays); the dirty-rectangle flush is covered by the existing `windowd2` (focus/raise/drag) and `termwin` screenshots, which check the final frames. Resizing (15j) is next |
| 15h | the GUI starts itself usable (roadmap item 15, the user's after step 50): `windowd` is only a compositor — with nothing else it shows a bare desktop a user cannot do anything from. It now launches a session program when it takes the screen (`user/bin/windowd.c`): a terminal by default, or the command named by `startup` in `/system/config/windows` (`startup =` empty means a bare compositor), so a user on real hardware with no serial console gets a shell to launch programs from. When the session program ends, windowd ends too (`term` now exits when its shell does), giving the screen back to the text console — closing the terminal leaves the GUI. A Ctrl-Alt-Delete escape (the user's second exit path) works even if the owner hangs: the kernel's keyboard code (`press` in `kernel/keyboard.c`, modifier `KEYBOARD_ALT`) notices Ctrl-Alt-Delete while a program owns the screen and ends that program (`display_escape` in `kernel/process.c`, SIGKILL); the program's own teardown gives the screen back, in its thread context, so nothing repaints from the keyboard interrupt and a hung owner is still escapable (closing the open security-audit item). No new command: `windowd` does it all. Tests (both architectures): `wstart` — after only `windowd &`, the terminal comes up (windowd launched it), and typing `exit` into it ends the shell, so term and then windowd end and the console returns; `wescape` — Ctrl-Alt-Delete brings the console back. The compositor tests (`windowd`, `windowd2`, `wtext`, `termwin`, `wmany`) set `startup =` so they keep driving windowd with their own clients |
| 15g | `term`, a terminal in a window (roadmap item 15, the first graphical app): `user/bin/term.c` opens a window with `libwindow`, starts the shell on a pseudo-terminal, renders the shell's output as a grid of characters (a small VT100 interpreter handles the cursor moves and erases the line editor and programs use; white on black, colors not kept yet), and sends the keys it receives to the shell. One thread reads the pty and draws, the main thread reads window events and writes keys to the pty. Run it from a shell with windowd up (`windowd &` then `term`). Test (`termwin`, both architectures): the window's title is "Terminal", the shell's prompt is rendered, and typing `echo zzzz` on the keyboard (routed by the kernel to windowd, to the window, to the shell) renders its output on the next line (screenshots check the title, the prompt and the output). This completes the window system (item 15): mouse, local sockets, shared buffers, the display handoff, windowd with interaction, the toolkit, and a terminal app |
| 15f | `libwindow`, the window toolkit (roadmap item 15): graphical programs stop speaking the raw protocol. `user/lib/window.c` (`window.h`): `window_open` makes a titled window (a shared buffer), `window_fill` and `window_text` draw into it, `window_flush` shows it (a new `WMSG_DAMAGE` makes windowd recomposite), and `window_wait` returns pointer and key events. Text uses the same public-domain X11 misc-fixed glyphs as the console, now in user space: `scripts/bdf2c.py` gains an `--include` option and generates a font-data file for the library (`user/lib/wfont.c`, `wfont.h`), which windowd also links to draw window titles (the title travels with `WMSG_CREATE`). Test (`wtext`, both architectures): a toolkit app draws a background, a box and "Hello", and windowd draws the window title "RitterOS"; a screenshot checks the box, the background, and that text pixels appear in the window and the title bar |
| 15e2 | window interaction (roadmap item 15): windowd gains focus, raising and moving windows with the mouse. A press focuses the window under the pointer and raises it to the front (a per-window stacking order `z`; the focused window's title bar is drawn bright, the others dim), and a press on a title bar begins a drag that moves the window until the button is released. Pointer and key events are forwarded to the focused window. No kernel change — the pointer events (with buttons) already arrive through `DISPLAY_EVENT`. Test (`windowd2`, both architectures): two overlapping windows (green, red on top); clicking the lower one's exposed title bar raises it (green now over the overlap) and focuses it (its title bar turns bright), and dragging its title bar moves it (checked by screenshots, the pointer driven over QMP as deltas from the screen centre) |
| 15e | the window server `windowd` (roadmap item 15; the server split from its interaction, which is 15e2). `windowd` is a program run on demand (`windowd &`, later `elf start windowd`): it takes the screen with `SYS_DISPLAY`, keeps a screen-sized back buffer (a shared buffer), and listens on the local socket `/run/windowd`. A client makes a window by filling a shared buffer with native pixels and passing it over the socket (`user/include/windowproto.h`: a shared buffer then a `WMSG_CREATE`); windowd composites the desktop, every window with a title bar and border, and the mouse pointer, and flushes it. Input reaches windowd through a new `SYS_DISPLAY` op, `DISPLAY_EVENT`: while a program owns the display the kernel routes the pointer and keyboard to it (`kernel/winput.c`; the keyboard stops going to the terminal until release), and windowd forwards those to the window. The library adds `ritteros_display_event`. Test (`windowd`, both architectures): a client's green window is composited (a screenshot checks the desktop, title bar and content pixels), and moving the mouse delivers a pointer event through the kernel to windowd and on to the client |
| 15d | the display handoff (roadmap item 15, the window system): a program (windowd) takes the screen from the text console. `SYS_DISPLAY`: ACQUIRE parks the console (root, one owner at a time), INFO reports the screen's size and native pixel format, FLUSH copies a rectangle of pixels to the screen, RELEASE gives it back (the console redraws). The kernel keeps its own correctly-attributed framebuffer mapping (write-combining on x86, non-cacheable on ARM) and copies the program's pixels to it, so this is correct on real hardware, where a plain cacheable user mapping of the framebuffer would never display. While parked, the console still collects text in RAM; a crash (`console_emergency`) takes the screen back so a panic always shows, and a program's exit gives the screen back automatically. The library adds `ritteros_display_acquire/release/info/flush`. Test (`display`, both architectures): the program fills the screen red (a screenshot confirms the pixels reached it), then releases and the console's title bar is back |
| 15c | shared buffers (roadmap item 15, the window system): a region of physical pages mapped into more than one program's address space, so they share memory without copying (a client draws pixels, windowd composites them). `kernel/shbuf.c` owns the pages (reference counted); `SYS_SHARE` creates a buffer (a descriptor), maps it into the caller (a dedicated high address window, `paging_user_map`), reports its size, and passes it to or takes it from another program over a local socket (fd-passing, like Unix SCM_RIGHTS; the pending buffers ride the local connection next to its byte streams). Closing a descriptor unmaps the pages without freeing them (`paging_user_unmap`, new), and the pages are freed only when no descriptor holds the buffer — so a program's exit never frees pages another still maps. The library adds `ritteros_share_create/map/size/send/recv`. Test (`share`, both architectures): one program's two threads share an 8 KiB buffer passed over a local socket — the client's pattern is seen by the receiver, and the receiver's change is seen back |
| 15b | local (Unix-domain) stream sockets (roadmap item 15, the window system): a connection between two programs on one machine, named by a path-like string in a kernel registry (`kernel/localsock.c`; not a file on the disk — normal boots never write). A listener binds a name and accepts; a client connects to the name; each connection carries two byte streams read and written like a TCP connection. `socket(SOCKET_LOCAL)` with `bind`/`connect` carrying a name (length in the third argument), then `listen`/`accept` or `read`/`write`; the library adds `ritteros_local_listen`, `ritteros_local_connect`, `ritteros_bind_local`, `ritteros_connect_local`. The local transport windowd and its clients will use (and any two programs that talk on one machine). Test (`local`, both architectures): one program's two threads — a listener and a client — exchange a message and its echo over a named connection, no network needed |
| 15a | the mouse (roadmap item 15, the window system): `kernel/mouse.c` holds what all mice share (names `mouse0`, `mouse1`, ... in the order found, a pointer position clamped to the screen, the buttons held and the counters); drivers report relative motion, a wheel step and a button bitmask. `kernel/usbmouse.c` is the USB HID boot-protocol mouse (reports from an interrupt endpoint, like the keyboard); `arch/x86_64/ps2.c` adds the PS/2 mouse on the i8042's port 2 (the port and IRQ 12 enabled, 3-byte packets, x86 only). No window server yet, so the pointer is not a terminal input; the rescue console's new `mouse` command shows each mouse's state. Tests: `mouse` on ARM (QEMU's `usb-mouse`, the only relative-motion device there) and `ps2mouse` on x86 (the always-present i8042 PS/2 mouse, no USB mouse attached); each moves the pointer and clicks through QEMU's QMP `input-send-event` (the old HMP mouse commands were removed) and checks the `mouse` command reports it |
| remote dev 1 | a pipe() primitive (the user's; the keystone for an SSH-in dev loop where another machine edits, builds and runs on RitterOS). RitterOS had no pipe -- only ptys and local sockets gave connected fds. `kernel/pipe.c` adds a one-way, binary-transparent pipe: a ring buffer with per-end reference counts, a read that returns 0 once every write end is closed and a write that fails with `ERR_PIPE` once every read end is closed. `SYS_PIPE` (65) / `ritteros_pipe()` hands back a read fd and a write fd; `FILE_PIPE` joins `FILE_PTY`/`FILE_SOCKET` in process.c (read/write/close, and spawn inherits it refcounted so EOF only fires when all write ends across processes are gone). Groundwork for shell pipelines and a compiler driver later. Test (`pipe`, both architectures): a 200 KB stream between two threads round-trips with a correct EOF, a child spawned with the write end as its stdout gives the read end EOF once it exits, and a write with no reader gives EPIPE; proven by breaking EOF. |
| remote dev 2 | non-interactive ssh exec on pipes (the user's; same track). When a client runs a command without asking for a pty (an `exec`/`shell` with no `pty-req`, as `ssh HOST CMD` does), `sshd` runs it on three raw pipes instead of a pty: client input to the command's stdin (a real EOF on the channel's EOF), separate stdout/stderr (`SSH_CHANNEL_DATA`/`EXTENDED_DATA`), and the exit status once both drain. So a remote OpenSSH agent moves files and binaries (`ssh ritteros 'cat > FILE' < bin`, `ssh ritteros 'cat FILE'`) and captures output cleanly; interactive sessions (a `pty-req`, `ssh -t`, a login shell) are unchanged. `cat`'s binary guard now applies only when stdout is a terminal (adds `isatty()`, `TERMINAL_ISATTY`), so it can move binaries. Test (`sshexec`, both architectures): from the Mac's OpenSSH, push a binary, check the guest's sha256, pull it back binary-safe and compare, confirm stdout/stderr stay apart and the exit code propagates; proven by forcing the pty path. HTTPS (a TLS client) and an on-device compiler are the later parts of this track. |
| media 0 | a user-space heap (the user's; the media suite needs variable-size buffers). RitterOS had no `malloc`; `user/lib/malloc.c` is a K&R free list whose `morecore` grows by a 16 MiB shared-memory arena from the kernel (no anonymous `mmap` yet, so a single allocation is capped at one arena). `window_blit`/`window_shifts` added to libwindow for drawing images. Test (`malloc`, both architectures): patterned blocks freed shuffled and reallocated into the holes, calloc/realloc, several arenas; proven by a split bug corrupting a live block. |
| media 1 | `libimage` + `imageview` (the user's media suite). `media.h` holds the hardware-neutral shared types (`struct image`, `struct audio_buffer`). `libimage` decodes/encodes PPM and BMP to RGBA8888 (PNG/JPEG later), with `image_scale`. `imageview` shows an image in a `windowd` window, rescaling on resize. Test (`image`, both architectures): host SHA of the RGBA pixels, PPM and BMP agree, imageview fills its window; proven by a BMP channel-order bug. |
| media 2 | `libaudio` (WAV). Decodes/encodes WAV (PCM 8/16-bit, mono/stereo) to signed-16 samples, with resample and mix (for the mixer). Ends at PCM in RAM; playing needs `/dev/pcm0`. Test (`audio`, both architectures): host SHA of the samples, and a save/reload round-trip. |
| media 3 | `libvideo` + `reel`. `libvideo` reads a video as frames (`struct image`) plus an audio track (`struct audio_buffer`); the container is RVID, a simple own format (a real codec like H.264 would slot in behind the same interface). `reel` plays the frames at rate in a window and streams the audio to `/dev/pcm0` on a thread -- the first thing to join the image, audio and video libraries. Test (`video`, both architectures): host SHA of the frames, and reel draws a frame while QEMU captures the audio; proven by a wrong decode. |
| audio 1 | an Intel HDA / Azalia driver (the user's, for the laptop's AMD HDA; developed on QEMU's intel-hda). `kernel/hda.c` maps the controller, resets it, sets up the CORB/RIRB command rings (polled), walks the codec graph for a DAC and an output pin, and plays 48 kHz/16-bit/stereo PCM over a cyclic DMA ring (two-entry BDL); `sound_write` fills the ring ahead of the hardware play position. The `hdatone` boot option plays a test tone. A codec amp's gain is an index, so gain 0 is silence -- the amps are set high. Test (`hda`, both architectures): boot with `hdatone`; the codec is found and QEMU's wav audiodev captures a non-silent tone; seen to fail silent with the gain wrong. Real speakers on the laptop may be on the AMD ACP (I2S) path -- to resolve on hardware. |
| audio 2 | `/dev/pcm0` and the player `tune`. A program opens the write-only device `/dev/pcm0` (`FILE_PCM`) and writes 48 kHz/16-bit/stereo PCM to the HDA ring; closing it drains (plays out the rest) then stops. `tune FILE` decodes a WAV (libaudio), resamples it, and streams it -- the first program that makes sound. Test (`tune`, both architectures): tune plays a WAV and QEMU captures audible output (peak ~23000); proven silent without the drain. |
| audio 3 | the `soundd` mixer. `soundd` owns `/dev/pcm0` and listens on `/run/sound`, so several programs play at once: each connection's PCM is summed into a shared accumulator ring by a reader thread (a little ahead of the play point, resynced if behind), and an output thread clamps the sum and writes it out. Thread scratch goes on the stack (no TLS in RitterOS). Test (`soundmix`, both architectures): two clients send a constant level at once and QEMU captures about twice one client's level; proven by the peak being a single client's without the sum. |
| windows: desktop menu | right-clicking the desktop background (anywhere no window is) opens a menu: **Open terminal**, **Open owl** and **Close window server** (gives the screen back to the text console). `windowd` launches the two apps as its own children and a reaper thread waits on them (RitterOS has no auto-reap). The gesture matches `owl`'s own right-click context menu. Test (`wdesktop`, both architectures): the right-click opens the menu (a screenshot checks its three items), Open terminal launches a terminal, and Close window server returns the console; proven by disabling the right-click branch. |
| windows: the desktop stays | `windowd` no longer exits when its startup session (or any program) ends: the empty desktop stays up, waiting for the user to open something from the desktop menu or quit it (Close window server, or Ctrl-Alt-Delete). So closing the last window lands on the background, not back at the text console. Test (`wstart`, both architectures, reworked): windowd launches a terminal, typing `exit` closes it, and the desktop is still up (its background where the window was) with no "session ended"; Ctrl-Alt-Delete then returns the console. |
| kill | the program `kill [-SIGNAL] PID...` (the user's, for the SDK: a remote agent stops what it started): sends TERM by default, or KILL, INT, HUP or a number 1-31, to the processes `ps` lists; root may end any, other users only their own (the kernel checks). Test (`kill`, both architectures): two background loops, one ended with TERM and one with KILL (the shell reports each, ps no longer lists them running), a missing process, a wrong signal and no arguments refused; proven by kill sending nothing. |
| sdk | the SDK, `sdk/` (the user's: someone else's agent, pointed at it, makes a repository of its own for a new program, builds it, copies it over SSH to a RitterOS machine and runs it). `sdk/CLAUDE.md` is the guide for people and agents (the toolchain needed: Zig 0.17.0, make, Python 3, OpenSSH; the machine's one-time setup: network, sshd, an Ed25519 key added with two ssh commands; deploy, run and stop; what a program is, where it goes, the C library and what it lacks, the limits; the remote shell; troubleshooting with the real messages and exit codes). `sdk/ritteros.mk` is the build a project includes (`make`, `deploy`, `run`, `stop`; RitterOS's own compiler flags, linker script and start code; zig from RitterOS's tools or PATH, its headers found with `zig env`); the Makefile's new `sdk` target builds the library and start code it links for both architectures (`ZIG_LIB`: zig's headers next to whichever zig is used). `sdk/template/` (a command-line program with its own `CLAUDE.md`) and `sdk/examples/hellowin/` (a windowed app). Learned on the way: root passes every permission check, the x bit included, so a fresh 0644 copy runs for root (the chmod is for other users and owl). Test (`sdk`, both architectures): copies of the template and the example built outside the tree, the guide's key setup by password, `make deploy run`, an overwrite keeping the mode, the other architecture's build refused (126), a missing program (127), the windowed app on the screen with its output over ssh until `make stop` ends it (143); proven by a wrong ps filter in `make stop`. |
| sdk API | `sdk/API.md`, the list of what a program can use (the user's: the SDK needs one): made from the headers by `scripts/sdkapi.py`, so it cannot drift -- why there are no shared libraries (one static archive, a program carries the parts it uses), a table of the 26 headers with who each is for (apps, or the system's own: ssh.h, gpt.h, mkfs.h, windowproto.h, and ritteros.h's calls for root or one system program), an index of all 255 functions and macros with their declarations, and every header as it is, with its comments. A header missing from the script's list stops it. Test: the `sdk` test runs `sdkapi.py --check`; proven by a prototype added to a header and by a new unlisted header (both refused). |
| 22a | `mmap` and `munmap` (roadmap item 22, shared libraries: the base the loader maps libraries with). `kernel/mmap.c`: each process's mappings in a window of its own (48-112 TiB), placed by the kernel or exactly with `MAP_FIXED` (which replaces what is there, inside the window only), shrunk or split by `munmap`, released at exit before the address space is destroyed. A read-only mapping of a RitterFS file uses the page cache: one copy in memory of each version of the file (its object, inode generation, modification time and size), shared by every program that maps it; a file rewritten later is a new version, so programs that mapped the old one keep its pages, which is what lets a library be replaced under a running program. Private writable mappings, other file systems and anonymous memory are the program's own pages. Pages are filled when mapped (no demand paging). Not yet: writable shared file mappings, `mprotect`, mappings past a file's end; never writable and executable at once. `sys/mman.h` (PROT_*, MAP_*), and `malloc` now grows by anonymous `mmap` (1 MiB arenas, or what a large request needs): no more 16 MiB limit, and no shared-buffer descriptor per arena. Test (`mmap`, both architectures): anonymous memory and a 40 MiB malloc, a program file against `read()`, a private copy, four programs sharing a 4 MiB file (1211 pages used; back after they end), a rewritten file's old mapping keeping the old bytes, `MAP_FIXED` in a reservation and a split, ten refusals and four faults; proven by giving every mapping its own copy (4280 pages: the sharing check fails). |
| 22b | the dynamic loader `ld.so` (roadmap item 22). The kernel loads a program that names a loader (`PT_INTERP`) and the loader itself, starts the loader, and puts the System V auxiliary vector on the first stack (where a copy of the program's headers is, its entry point, the loader's base; a static program gets an empty one). `ld.so` (`user/ldso/ldso.c`, a static program linked just below 2 GiB, `user/ldso.ld`: its C library is compiled for the small code model) maps every library the program needs from `/system/<arch>/lib`, and the ones those need, each once: a reservation of the library's span, then its segments with `MAP_FIXED` (code and read-only data from the page cache, one copy for every program; data a private copy; `.bss` zeroed); it resolves every symbol at once (`-z now`: a missing library or function stops the program at its start with a message and exit code 127, never half way) and applies the relocations (`RELATIVE`, `GLOB_DAT`, `JUMP_SLOT`, `ABS64`; a copy relocation is refused: libraries export functions only), runs the libraries' initializers and jumps to the program with the stack the kernel made. `common/elf.h` gains the dynamic-linking definitions; `user/user.ld` places the dynamic sections with the others of their kind. Started by itself, `ld.so` says what it is. |
| 22c | the shared libraries (roadmap item 22; the user's: several, so each can be updated on its own): `libritteros.so.1` (the C library, system calls, network and DNS helpers, hashes, users, settings), `libwindow.so.1`, `libimage.so.1`, `libaudio.so.1`, `libvideo.so.1`, the library sources built again position-independent, each exporting what its headers declare (`scripts/libexports.py` writes the version scripts with `sdkapi.py`'s parser; the linker refuses a declared function a library does not define, and `-z defs` an undefined one). Every program links dynamically (the user's: bin, apps and tests; `--as-needed`, so `ls` needs only `libritteros.so.1`, `imageview` also `libwindow` and `libimage`); `ld.so` and the SDK's programs stay static, and the system's own SSH, cipher, GPT and mkfs code stays in `libritteros.a` only. Interface changes so libraries export functions only (the user's): `errno` is `*__errno()` behind the name, and the font tables are `wfont_regular()` and `wfont_bold()`. `syscall6` and `__thread_start` moved from each program's `start.S` into the library (`syscall.S`). `osinstall` copies `lib/`. `sdk/API.md` names the library of each header. Test (`ldso`, both architectures): `ldtest` dynamic (errno in a library's data in the mmap window) and `ldtest-static`, errno, malloc, a thread and a file in both, `ld.so` by hand, a missing library and a missing function (exit 127, the messages), and on the host the libraries' sonames and what programs need; proven by an `ld.so` that applies no relocations (`login` faults at its first call, the rescue console takes over). |
| fonts | `/system/fonts` (the user's, with 22: fonts as files, the compiled default kept as the fallback): the 13 Unicode faces of the public-domain X11 misc-fixed fonts (6x13, 7x13, 7x14, 8x13, 9x15, 9x18 each regular and bold, 10x20; unmodified, from the same X.Org package as before, in `fonts/` with `SOURCE.txt`; the kernel's console font is built from there too), read as BDF files by libwindow when a program asks (`wfont_find("10x20")`: the file mapped, parsed as `scripts/bdf.py` places glyphs, kept for the program's life; a bold face borrows missing glyphs from its regular one). The compiled 9x15 stays the default and the fallback: no file is read without a request, and a missing or broken font gives 0. `struct font` is opaque now (`wfont_width`, `wfont_height`, `wfont_text_width`; `window_text_font`), so other kinds of font need no interface change. `term -f FONT` sizes its cells to the font. `osinstall` copies the fonts. Test (`fonts`, both architectures): every font opens with its cell, 9x15/9x15B from their files draw exactly like the compiled faces, the file has glyphs the compiled tables leave out, the bold fallback, a missing font, a broken file and a path refused; `term -f 10x20` on screen (20-pixel rows, 10-pixel cells) and an unknown font falling back; proven by term ignoring -f. |
| 22d | the SDK on the shared libraries, `ldd`, and keeping the libraries compatible (roadmap item 22). `sdk/ritteros.mk` links shared by default (`LINK = shared`, like the system's programs; `LINK = static` carries the code), and `make sdk` builds the shared libraries too. `ldd PROGRAM...` lists the libraries a program needs and where they are, and every function none of them has, from the files (mapped, not run). `scripts/abicheck.py` records each library's interface in `sdk/abi/` (every exported function's declaration, its headers' types and constants, the system call numbers with libritteros) and refuses a removed or changed item (it needs a new major version, `libX.so.2`), records an addition with `--update`; its self-test checks the verdicts on made-up changes. Tests: `libswap` (both architectures; a library of the tests' own in two compatible versions: a program built with the first runs on the second without a rebuild, and one already running keeps its first version's pages; proven by a page cache that ignores versions), `ldso` with `ldd` (a missing library, a missing function, a normal and a static program), `sdk` (the template linked shared and static, both deployed and run; the interface check and its self-test; proven, on the headers, by a field added to `struct image`). |
| 22e | demand paging and copy-on-write (roadmap item 22, the user's: before TLS). Anonymous memory is made a page at a time at its first touch, so a large mapping costs nothing until used (malloc's arenas too); a private writable mapping of a RitterFS file (every library's data) shows the page cache's pages read-only and copies a page at its first write; a mapping may reach past its file's end, and touching a page there ends the program with `SIGBUS` (7: `bus error (past the end of a mapped file)`). A file's pages are still read when it is mapped (a version rewritten later may have its blocks reused), and before the mappings' lock is taken: a program's `read()` may hold the file system's lock while `copy_user` makes a page of its mappings. The page fault of a program (both architectures, interrupts on as for a system call) and `copy_user` go to `mmap_fault`, which makes the page or names the signal. Found on the way: x86's exception handler fell through from a returning `user_exception` into the crash report (a fault always ended the program before). Test (`mmap`, both architectures): a 1 GiB mapping costs 0 pages, 100 pages touched cost 101 (one page table), a private writable mapping read in full costs 1 page and 10 pages written 10, touching past a file's end is SIGBUS, and the rest as in 22a; proven by private writable mappings copied whole (257 pages for reading). |
| 22f | `mprotect` and read-only relocated data (roadmap item 22). `mprotect(addr, length, prot)` (`SYS_MPROTECT` 68) gives the mappings in a range a new access, splitting them at its ends; pages keep their contents, also through `PROT_NONE` (a page can now be there without the program's access: both architectures' user mappings without the user bit, and `paging_user_query` reports such pages with access 0, so unmapping and exit still free them right). A page-cache page stays read-only in a writable mapping (copy-on-write goes on); a page made executable gets the ARM cache maintenance. Refused: writable and executable, writing a shared file mapping, a range with a hole (ENOMEM), giving access to a file mapping made PROT_NONE. Libraries are linked with `-z relro`, and `ld.so` makes each one's `PT_GNU_RELRO` (the GOT and the data only relocations write) read-only once it has relocated it; programs follow with 22g. Tests: `mmap` (a split, PROT_NONE and back, copy-on-write kept, a write to a page made read-only stops a program, three refusals), `libswap` (writing the tests' library's relocated data stops the program; proven by an ld.so that skips the mprotect). |
| 22g | programs from the page cache, and a page cache that keeps files (roadmap item 22). The kernel loads a program's segments as mappings of the page cache, like `ld.so` loads libraries: its code and read-only data are one copy in memory for every program running the same file, its writable data copy-on-write (a page becomes the program's own at its first write), its zeroed data (bss) made when first touched; a segment that cannot be shared that way is still a copy. Programs now have RELRO too: their GOT and other data only relocations write are on pages of their own, which `ld.so` makes read-only after relocating. The page cache keeps a file no mapping uses any more (up to 32 MiB, at most an eighth of the memory; the longest unused goes first, an older version of a file as soon as a newer one is read), so a program started again or a library loaded again is not read again. `mem` shows the cache in a second line (files, pages, pages read; `REPORT_MEMORY` 7); counting free memory gives the unused cached files back first, so the count stays exact. Tests: `mmap` (a file mapped again after its last mapping is not read again, a rewritten one is and replaces the old version; `hello` is read at its first start and not at its second; writing a program's GOT stops it; four programs holding a 4 MiB file now share their code too). Proven by freeing unused files at once and by an `ld.so` leaving the program's RELRO writable. |
| 22h | `malloc` safe from several threads and giving memory back (roadmap item 22). The heap has one lock: a thread that finds it taken sleeps a moment (with one processor the holder is not running, so waiting by spinning would only waste time). A block of 128 KiB or more is a mapping of its own, given back to the system when freed; smaller ones come from 1 MiB arenas, and an arena whose blocks are all free again is given back too, but for one kept for the next requests. Tests: `malloc` (four threads allocating, checking and freeing 6,000 blocks each at once, every block intact; 48 MiB allocated and freed: free memory is back but for the arena kept and the page tables). Proven by a heap without its lock (blocks changed under the threads) and by one that keeps every arena. |
| window fixes | `term` freezing on the J1900 (the user's report from the 22e stick: after resizing, a terminal stopped reacting, even to its close box, and the pointer slowed down). `term`'s two threads shared their lock as a flag a waiter checked once a tick: while output streamed in, the reader gave it back and took it again at once, so the main thread -- which adopts a resize and reads every window event, the close box's too -- could wait forever, and `windowd`, writing that window's events, waited with it. Now a ticket lock, taken in the order asked. `windowd` no longer writes to clients from its input thread: events wait in a small queue per window (pointer moves merged), and each client's own thread sends them between its reads (at most 10 ms apart), so a client that stops reading stops only itself. Also fixed: a client leaving closed the buffer its window started with, which a resize had already closed (its number possibly another client's by then), and never the current one. Tests: the window-system tests on both architectures, and a stress session (three windows, the front terminal resized and moved over and over while two print continuously). |
| ctrl-click | Ctrl + click is a right-click (the user's choice, after the laptop's touchpad gave no context menu: in the PS/2 mouse mode firmware gives every touchpad, a two-finger tap is no right button; a native touchpad driver was the larger alternative). The kernel puts the modifiers held on any keyboard into each pointer event (`struct sys_input_event`'s spare byte becomes `modifiers`: `INPUT_CTRL`, `INPUT_SHIFT`, `INPUT_ALT`; same layout, so programs built before are unaffected); `windowd` treats the left button pressed with Ctrl as the right one until it is let go, so menus and apps see a plain right-click. `scripts/abicheck.py` now accepts a field named `reserved...` taken into use with the same type (its self-test has the case and its opposite, a retyped one). Test: `wdesktop` opens its second menu with Ctrl + click (proven by a `windowd` ignoring the modifiers). |
| open dialog | Opening files from the GUI (the user's, after `imageview` and `reel` did nothing when started from `owl` without a file): `imageview` and `reel` start without a file as an empty window with a **File** menu (Open..., Close); **Open...** brings up the shared dialog `window_choose_file` (in libwindow, the user's choice over a mode of `owl` or a service in `windowd`): a window of its own listing a folder, a click to select and a second to open, the arrow keys and Enter, a Name line for typing a name or path, Open and Cancel. The window then takes the picture's or video's size; `reel` stays open after a video and plays the next one chosen. `owl` opens data files by type (a fixed list, the user's choice): `.ppm`/`.bmp` in `imageview`, `.rvid` in `reel`, `.wav` in `tune` in a terminal, the rest in the editor (before, every data file went to the editor). Test: `wopen` (imageview's File > Open... with a typed path, owl opening a picture by its type, reel playing a video chosen in the dialog; proven by a dialog that never returns a choice). |
| 22i | Per-thread data (roadmap item 22): `__thread` variables in programs and libraries, a copy for each thread, and `errno` per thread. The kernel keeps a thread pointer per thread (`SYS_THREAD_POINTER` 70: x86's FS base, ARM's TPIDR_EL0), saved and loaded at every switch. `ld.so` collects each module's `PT_TLS`, places the blocks in each architecture's standard layout (the program's first, as its linker assumed), applies the per-thread relocations (x86 DTPMOD64, DTPOFF64, TPOFF64; ARM the same and TLSDESC descriptors) and hands the list to libritteros, which makes the main thread's area; `thread_create` makes one for each new thread, `thread_join` frees it; `__tls_get_addr` serves x86 libraries. Static programs set up their own from their headers (they now get the auxiliary vector too). A reserve in each area waits for 22k's `dlopen`. Test: `tls` (tlstest dynamic and static: initial values, each of four threads its own copy, a 64-byte aligned variable, the main thread's kept, errno per thread in two threads failing at once; proven by a scheduler that does not load the next thread's pointer); `ldtest` now checks errno per thread instead of where it lies. |
| 22j | Writable shared file mappings and `msync` (roadmap item 22). A `MAP_SHARED` mapping of a RitterFS file open for writing may be writable: every program mapping the file shares the page cache's pages, so each sees the others' writes at once. A page is mapped read-only until its first write, which marks it written; `msync` (`SYS_MSYNC` 69, flags `MS_ASYNC`, `MS_SYNC`, `MS_INVALIDATE` as on Unix, all writing back at once), `munmap` and the program's end write the written pages to the file and commit, and the cached copy takes the file's new version, so later mappings share it too. A descriptor open for reading gives EACCES; other file systems ENOTSUP. Test: `mmap` (written through a mapping then msync: read() sees it; munmap writes back; a child's write seen in another program's mapping at once; proven by a msync writing nothing and by private copies for shared mappings). |
| 22k | Loading libraries while running (roadmap item 22): `dlopen`, `dlsym`, `dlclose`, `dlerror` in `<dlfcn.h>`. `ld.so` does the work: it stays mapped and hands its entry points to libritteros at the start, so the same loader that linked the program maps the library (a name in `/system/<arch>/lib` or a path) and what it needs, links it, gives its per-thread data a block in every thread's area (from the reserve 22i kept; libritteros copies the initial data into each live thread's) and runs its initializers. A failure returns to `dlopen` with a message (a small catch and throw in `ldso.S`) and undoes what was loaded. `dlsym` looks in the library first, then in everything loaded (per-thread variables: the calling thread's copy). `dlclose` keeps the library loaded, as musl does (its symbols may be bound elsewhere). `ld.so` now has a plain `errno` (it runs on the program's threads). Test: `dl` (dltest and a test library: its constructor, a function calling into libritteros, its per-thread data in two threads, the same handle twice, a missing symbol and file with dlerror's messages, RTLD_DEFAULT, dlclose; a static build fails with a message; proven by a dlopen that links nothing). |
| 22l | Full address-space randomization (roadmap item 22): every process's pieces are somewhere else each time it runs. Dynamic programs are position-independent (compiled `-fPIE`, linked `-pie`; their static-only parts from a position-independent copy of the archive, `libritteros-pic.a`) and the kernel loads them at a random place between 4 GiB and 8 TiB higher (their headers are in memory now, so `ld.so` finds where from `PT_PHDR`). `ld.so` itself is a static position-independent program, put elsewhere at random and applying its own relocations first. Each process's mappings (libraries, the heap, files) start at a random page of the mmap window's first half, its stacks up to 1 GiB lower, its shared buffers up to 1 TiB higher. Static programs (and programs built before) stay at their fixed place. A fault report adds the address as linked, for a disassembler. The SDK builds apps the same way. Test: `aslr` (aslrtest twice: code, data, stack, heap, a mapping, a library function and a per-thread variable all elsewhere the second time; proven by a kernel placing nothing at random); the programs test checks ranges instead of the fixed addresses. |
| 23a-23c | TLS's cryptography and certificates (roadmap item 23), our own code from the standards: AES-128/256 and GCM, bitsliced so they take the same time for every key and message (four blocks at once as eight 64-bit bit planes; the S-box computed as the inverse in GF(2^8) and FIPS 197's affine map, never looked up); SHA-384, HMAC-SHA384 and HKDF with SHA-256 or SHA-384 (TLS 1.3's key schedule); checking signatures, on public data: RSA PKCS #1 v1.5 and PSS (1024-4096 bits, Montgomery multiplication) and ECDSA on P-256 and P-384 (Jacobian points, Shamir's trick); X.509 (`<x509.h>`): reading certificates (DER), a certificate's signature by another's key, host names (subject alternative names, one leftmost `*` label), chains to trusted roots with dates and CA flags, PEM bundles. Written and checked on the Mac first (every root of macOS's bundle reads; against OpenSSL's keys, signatures and chains), then here: `user/lib/aes.c`, `hkdf.c`, `verify.c`, `x509.c`, kept out of libritteros.so.1 for libtls.so.1 (23d). 22i's `user/lib/tls.c` is `threaddata.c` now. Test: `tlscrypto` (FIPS 197, the GCM spec's cases, RFC 4231 and 5869 vectors, OpenSSL-made RSA-2048/4096 and ECDSA P-256/P-384 signatures, a P-384 root, RSA intermediate and P-256 leaf chain at the time it was made: names, wildcard, expiry, trust, tampering; proven by a wrong GHASH reduction and a wrong point addition). |
| 23d | the TLS 1.3 client (RFC 8446), our own: records (AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305), the handshake with X25519 and the key schedule (HKDF), the server's certificate chain, host name and dates checked against trusted roots, its handshake signature (RSA-PSS, ECDSA P-256/P-384) and Finished; `libtls.so.1`, OpenBSD's libtls interface (`<tls.h>`: `tls_client`, `tls_configure`, `tls_connect`, `tls_connect_servername`, `tls_connect_socket`, `tls_read`, `tls_write`, `tls_close`, `tls_error`, the CA file or memory, `insecure_noverifycert`/`name`/`time`); as OpenBSD's, `tls_read` returns 0 when the server just closes and `tls_close` then reports the missing close_notify. Test: `tlsclient` against the Mac's `openssl s_server` (LibreSSL, TLS 1.3 only) with certificates made by `scripts/testcerts.sh` (a P-384 root, an RSA intermediate, P-256, RSA and expired server certificates): each cipher suite, 300,000 bytes, the wildcard, every refusal and each switch that lets it through (proven by the name and date checks skipped and a byte lost per record). |
| 23e | `fetch https://` (item 23 complete): HTTP over TLS 1.3 through libtls, port 443 by default, redirections between http:// and https://; a body's completeness is HTTP's to say (its length, its last chunk), so a server closing without close_notify is accepted, as other HTTP clients do; a receiving error is reported. The root store: Mozilla's trusted roots as curl extracts them (`certs/cacert.pem`, 118 certificates of 2026-10-20, MPL-2.0, the user's exception; `certs/SOURCE.txt`) installed as `/system/certs/cacert.pem` (and by `osinstall`); RitterOS reads all but one (P-521). Test: `https` against the Mac's `s_server -HTTP` serving hand-made HTTP answers (a length, chunks, the end at the close, redirections to a path and to http://, 404) with the test root appended to the image's store: 300,000 bytes, a wildcard name; a name the certificate lacks, an expired one and one from a root outside the store refused; tlscrypto reads the store and checks every root's SHA-2 self-signature (115 of 115 with the test root); proven by fetch without the chain check and by 4096-bit RSA keys refused. `tlsclient` now meets the real store (the test root is not in it). |
| 24a | Wi-Fi's cryptography (roadmap item 24), our own code: AES decryption (the inverse cipher in aes.c's constant-time bitsliced form: the affine map undone before the GF(2^8) inverse, rows shifted back, InvMixColumns as MixColumns after a step of 04x^2 + 05), SHA-1, HMAC-SHA1 and PBKDF2-SHA1 (WPA2's passphrase to key: 4,096 rounds, the network's name as salt), IEEE 802.11's PRF (WPA2's keys) and KDF-SHA256 (WPA3's), AES key wrap (RFC 3394: the group key in the handshake), AES-CMAC (RFC 4493: WPA3's handshake check, protected management frames), AES-CCM (RFC 3610: CCMP); `user/lib/sha1.c`, `wpacrypto.c`, in `crypto.h`, static-only (libritteros.a, like SSH's). Checked on the Mac first (every value the standards print, also against Python's hashlib and hmac). Test: `wpacrypto` (FIPS 197 C.1/C.3, FIPS 180, RFC 2202, 6070, 3394, 4493, 3610, 802.11 Annex J.3 and J.4; tampering refused; proven by InvMixColumns left out and CMAC's subkeys swapped). The full test after 23e: 3670 passed; two checks the root certificates moved (owl's rows, the RitterFS cache's share) fixed. |
| 24b | SAE, WPA3-Personal's password key exchange (802.11-2020 12.4), group 19 (P-256), `user/lib/sae.c` (static-only, in crypto.h): the password element by hunting and pecking (all 40 rounds always run) or by hash to element (RFC 9380's simplified SWU map, PT per network, then PWE = val * PT per peer), the commit (scalar, element), the peer's commit checked (scalar range, on the curve, not our reflection), K, KCK, PMK, PMKID, both confirms. Constant-time where the password is: P-256 in Montgomery form on 4x64-bit limbs with masked carries, inversion and square roots as fixed exponentiations, Renes-Costello-Batina's complete addition (a = -3: no special cases), a fixed 4-bit window with a masked table pick. Checked first with a Python reference (big integers) against the vectors, and the addition formulas against affine arithmetic (doubling, infinity, P + -P). Test: `wpacrypto` adds RFC 9380 J.1.1 (SSWU, a sum), 802.11 Annex J.10 (the hunting commit byte for byte, KCK, PMK, PMKID, the hash-to-element PWE; as hostapd's tests carry them), refusals and two stations with random numbers; proven by the PWE's sign from the wrong seed byte and the reflection check dropped. |
| 24c | the kernel's wireless layer (`kernel/wlan.c`, `wlan.h`): a Wi-Fi card is a `struct netdev` named `wlan0` whose link is up once the elf wifid has joined. TCP/IP sends and receives Ethernet frames as on any card; the layer turns them into 802.11 data frames (To/From DS, LLC/SNAP) and protects them with CCMP (AES-CCM, moved to `common/aes.c` so the kernel shares it) under the keys wifid sets, refusing replays by packet number. Management and EAPOL frames go to wifid, which drives the radio through the system call `wlan` (71, root only: `WLAN_INFO`, `_CHANNEL`, `_SEND`, `_RECEIVE`, `_JOIN`, `_LEAVE`, `_KEY`, `_EAPOL`; structures in `common/syscalls.h`). A driver only moves 802.11 frames and tunes channels (`struct wlan`). `net` shows the link. No driver or user program yet (the test card and wifid are 24d-24e); checked by both kernels building it in and the network tests still passing. |
| 24d | a test Wi-Fi card of our own and a test access point: QEMU has no 802.11 device, so `scripts/qemu/ritterwifi.c` (invented, not a real chip, PCI 0xF055:0x11AC) is built into QEMU like the Realtek model -- a MAC, a channel register and two rings of 802.11 frames, carried over its netdev with a 4-byte header (channel, signal) to `scripts/testwifi.py`, which beacons networks and answers probe requests. `kernel/ritterwifi.c` drives it (polled like the e1000, registered through `wlan_register`); `ritteros_wlan` is the library wrapper for SYS_WLAN. Test `wifi`: the card beacons three networks (open, WPA2, WPA3) on channel 6, `wifitest` scans through SYS_WLAN and lists them with their security (from the RSN element's key-management suite) and answers to a probe; proven by the model dropping frames off the tuned channel (a scan on channel 11 finds nothing). |
| 24e | the elf `wifid` and the command `wifi` (item 24 done but for the real chip, 24f): `wifid` reads `/system/config/wifi/networks/`, scans, authenticates (open, or SAE for WPA3), associates and -- for WPA2/WPA3 -- runs the 4-way handshake, installs the CCMP keys and brings the link up; then the ordinary stack (dhclient, or a fixed address) runs, unchanged. `net up` and `dhclient` now handle `wlan%d` cards as well as `eth%d`. `wifi scan`/`join`/`forget`/`status` save networks (root, in `wifi/networks/SSID`) and show the link; `wifid` rejoins after a reboot or a drop. The test access point grew the join (`scripts/testwifi.py`, pure-Python crypto in `scripts/wificrypto.py`: AES, CCMP, CMAC, key wrap, the PRF, SAE) and a gateway (ARP, DHCP, ICMP) so the link carries IP. Test `wifijoin` (open, WPA2, WPA3 on both architectures): wifid joins, dhclient gets an address over the link and a ping to the gateway is answered (encrypted for WPA2/WPA3); proven by a wrong passphrase (the handshake fails, no join, no address). |
| 24f-1 | the MediaTek MT7921 driver begins (`kernel/mt7921.c`, `mt7921.h`; PCI 14c3:7961): probe and bind the chip, enable its PCI memory space and bus mastering, map BAR0 (1 MiB), read its identity through the chip's L1 remap window (chip addresses above the small window are reached by writing the top half of the address into `MT_HIF_REMAP_L1` and reading through BAR 0x40000) and take ownership from the chip's firmware (the `MT_CONN_ON_LPCTL` handshake). Verified on the user's laptop over SSH: "MediaTek MT7921 at 04:00.0, chip 0x7961 revision 0x10, host owns". No QEMU model (the chip is real hardware only, like the J1900's cards); the next steps are the firmware download, then the MCU and 802.11 offload. |
| 24f-2a | the MT7921 firmware on the disk and read by the driver: MediaTek's `WIFI_MT7961_patch...` and `WIFI_RAM_CODE_MT7961...` in `firmware/` (with `SOURCE.txt` and the licence, the user's MPL-style exception), installed as `/system/firmware/` by the Makefile and `osinstall`. The card probe runs before RitterFS is mounted, so the driver does the download from a thread that waits for the file system (`mounts_root`, new); 24f-2a reads the two files and parses their headers. Verified on the laptop: "patch: 92192 bytes, 1 region, section at 0x900000 len 92032; RAM: 792036 bytes, 5 regions". |
| 24f-2b | the firmware download, working on the laptop: a below-4 GiB page allocator (`pmm_alloc_pages_below`, the chip's DMA descriptor base is 32-bit), the WFSYS reset, the WFDMA0 DMA rings and the MCU message path, in the deferred worker. Three facts from Linux's `mt76` fixed the earlier dead ends: (1) the driver-own handshake (the L1 LPCR) must come *after* the WFSYS reset and DMA bring-up, not before -- the reset hands the MCU back to firmware-own, so taking it first left the chip ignoring the command ring (the dma_idx-stuck-at-0 symptom); (2) the WFDMA0 logic-reset pulse ends with the reset bits *set* (clear then set), as Linux leaves them -- clearing them again let the chip corrupt memory and hung the laptop; (3) the RAM firmware's region table is little-endian (unlike the patch's big-endian section map), so the addresses and lengths were byte-swapped and the RAM loaded as garbage. With all three, the laptop reports "patch downloaded" then "FIRMWARE RUNNING (MISC 0x3)". QEMU is unaffected (the probe is guarded by the MediaTek vendor ID); verified only on the laptop over SSH. |
| 24f-3a | the running firmware answers MCU commands: the driver sends `GET_NIC_CAPAB` and reads the chip's own MAC address from the reply's TLVs. The bootrom answered the download commands on RX ring 0, but the running firmware answers on RX ring 4 (mt76's "change mcu queue after firmware download"), so the driver arms both and `mcu_wait` scans them. Verified on the laptop: "MAC 02:aa:bb:cc:00:04" (a MediaTek OUI). |
| 24f-3b | MAC initialisation after the firmware is up: EFUSE_BUFFER_MODE (the chip loads its EEPROM from efuse), then the receive length, de-aggregation, the station table's counters cleared, and the band-0 timing/MIB/signal registers, as Linux does. The chip's WF_* sub-blocks are not in the L1 remap window -- they sit at fixed BAR offsets (mt76's fixed_map), reached with plain reads/writes; using the L1 window for them faulted the worker in the WTBL loop. Verified on the laptop: "set_eeprom ok", "MAC init done". |
| 24f-3c | the first scan, receive only: the MAC switched on (MAC_INIT_CTRL, SET_RX_PATH on channel 1), a station interface for the firmware (two "unified" commands, a 16-byte header instead of the 32-byte one: DEV_INFO_UPDATE with our address, BSS_INFO_UPDATE basic as an infrastructure station), the RX data ring (RX2), and a passive START_HW_SCAN of 2.4 GHz 1-13 and the 5 GHz channels outside the radar bands -- the chip only listens, it sends nothing. Received frames are stripped of their descriptor (24 fixed bytes, the optional groups rxd1 names, the header padding) to a plain 802.11 frame with its channel and signal (RCPI); the SCAN_DONE event (id 0x0D) ends it. Verified on the laptop: "scan done after 1120 ms", 55 networks with their channels and signals; for now only logged (24f-3d hands them to wifid). |
| 24f-3d | the MT7921 is `wlan0`, and `wifi scan` uses its firmware scan: an RX thread owns the three RX rings (the timer tick only looks and wakes it, as for the test card) and hands frames to the wireless layer (`wlan_receive`), command replies to the command waiting for them (named before the command goes out; commands from wifid's system calls and the boot one at a time under a mutex), and the SCAN_DONE event to the layer, which queues a new `WLAN_FRAME_SCAN_DONE` so `wifi` and `wifid` stop listening when the sweep ends. The driver's `scan` callback (WLAN_SCAN) runs a passive scan of 2.4 GHz 1-13 and 5 GHz 36-165 -- the radar channels too, since it only listens -- at 110 ms a channel (more than a beacon interval); `set_channel` moves the receiver; `transmit` sends nothing yet (24f-4, with the channel domain and power from the country). `wifi scan` now lists the networks strongest first with their signal and takes the channel from the beacon's own DS parameter set (2.4 GHz beacons are also heard on neighbouring channels); the wireless layer's queue grew to 128 frames. Verified on the laptop: `wifi scan` lists 35-41 networks in about 4.6 s, "ExampleNet wpa2 channel 6 -39 dBm" first. Test: `wifi` (the test card's fallback path: names, security, channel, signal; proven by dropping the signal column). |
| 24f-4a | the country, chosen by the firmware (the user's design: send the country, the hardware picks every channel and power variable). The RAM firmware carries MediaTek's certified per-country rules in its CLC region, which the driver now keeps at download instead of skipping. `wifi country CC` saves `country = CC` in `/system/config/wifi/radio` and applies it; `wifid` applies it when it starts (`/system/config/wifi/radio`, default "00" world-wide). The new `WLAN_COUNTRY` system call reaches the driver's `set_country`, which finds the two records (channels, power) for that code in the CLC data and hands each to the firmware with SET_CLC -- the OS reads and applies no limits itself. Verified on the laptop: `wifi country US` -> "country US set (2 rule record(s))". The radio still transmits nothing (24f-4b). |

On the first unicast frame to the access point the driver adds its station record (STA_REC_UPDATE: basic peer info + a WTBL entry) so the chip will transmit to it; `set_channel` now uses CHANNEL_SWITCH so the radio can send, not just listen. wifid's auth/assoc/handshake frames flow through this path unchanged. Verified on the laptop joining "ExampleNet" (channel 6): the auth frame goes out and the access point reacts on-air (a deauth came back), and we hear its beacons at -38 dBm -- so the radio transmits and the AP hears us. Not yet complete: the chip delivers broadcast frames (beacons) but not the unicast auth *response* addressed to us outside a scan -- continuous receive / the RX filter is 24f-4c. |

The cause: `tx_submit` waited after every data frame until the chip had read its descriptor, sleeping a tick or two (about 19 ms) per frame, often in the receive thread itself; data frames are now only posted (`tx_post`; the token keeps the page until the chip's report) and only commands wait. | 24f-12 | aggregation (A-MPDU): (a) data goes as QoS data (TID 0) when the access point does WMM (noted from its association response with its HT element; the BSS info's QoS flag tells the firmware); (b) sending in bursts: once the link is up the wireless layer asks for a Block Ack agreement (ADDBA request, TID 0, window 64; the MT7921's descriptor gets ADD_BA and the TID so the hardware fills in its starting sequence number) and passes an accepted one to the card (`block_ack`, the MT7921's STA_REC_BA, originator); (c) receiving in bursts: the access point's ADDBA requests are accepted (window at most 64), the card is told (recipient), and a reorder buffer per TID puts frames back in sequence before the packet number check (a hole older than 100 ms is skipped). What answers the access point or talks to the card runs on a work thread per card (`wlan0 work`), never on the driver's receive thread (a command waiting for its reply there timed out: the first try answered -1). On the laptop the access point proposed bursts for TID 0 and 5, both accepted; the 1.9 MB push went from 1.7-2.6 s to 1.0-1.2 s, the pull stayed 1.1-1.2 s (the Mac is on Wi-Fi too: its own leg likely sets the pace). The MT7921's interrupt masks the chip's interrupts and wakes the receive thread, which unmasked them only after emptying the rings; an interrupt that came before its frame was done (the firmware's wake-RX) left them masked, so every frame after waited for the tick. Now every interrupt ends with them unmasked (`kicked`; `net` counts the early ones). On the laptop: pings 1.1-2.0 ms, send to done 0.6-1.0 ms on average (was 8.5), the tick finding work 0-3 times. The first boot with it crashed (exception 14) seconds after login: the driver switched receiving on before `wlan_register`, which makes the layer's state for the card, and a frame handed over meanwhile met a null pointer -- a race the slow interrupts had hidden. The card is now registered first, and the layer's entry points ignore a card that is not registered yet. Unplugging a mounted stick, suspected too, was tried on the laptop: unmounted and removed cleanly. QEMU has no MT7921: checked on the laptop; the Wi-Fi join tests (layer changes) pass on both architectures. |
| 24g-12 | the MT7921's send ring (the user's, after 49l's check on the laptop: an 8 MB upload over Wi-Fi left 32 frames "not sent"): every frame sent holds one of the chip's 64 tokens until the chip reports it done (5 ms on average, up to 43 ms then), and with all 64 busy `transmit` refused the frame at once, so TCP had to send it again. Now 256 tokens and a 256-entry data ring (Linux's mt76 holds thousands), and a sender finding none waits up to 20 ms for one to be freed (never while delivering a received frame: the receive thread frees them); `net` shows how many waited and in vain. On the laptop (two 8 MB uploads and downloads over SSH): 0 not sent of 30,036, at most 109 in flight, no waits; uploads 2.59-2.68 MB/s (2.16 before), downloads unchanged (~2.0). No QEMU model of the chip: checked there only |

Test `wsteady` (both architectures, QMP moving as that touchpad did, with the test program `wclicks`): the creep held, the nudge kept, a fast drag whole; `wsteady off` the creep moving the clicks; proven with the filter off.
